Sanofi · SAN-FR · the new Memo conveyor's card of 19 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Sanofi

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 47,465 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external picture is a strong near-term tailwind sitting on a dated, visible headwind. Structural demand for type-2 inflammation biologics, concentrated in the US, gives Sanofi its best growth in two decades and high visibility to 2030; the US pricing agreement has removed the largest open-ended policy risk for three years. Against that, competition in Sanofi's one critical franchise is rising and has dated catalysts (KT-621 around year-end 2026, Rhapsido head-to-head 2027, zumilokibart 2029), vaccines face hostile US policy and better-convenience rivals, and the US dollar has become the largest single swing factor in reported earnings. For the modelling Analyst: grow Dupixent volume strongly to 2027 and decelerate thereafter, hold price slightly negative from 2027, model vaccines flat, and run every year at an explicit EUR/USD assumption with the EUR 0.23 EPS per 0.05 sensitivity.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0232C-0550C-0745C-0945C-3535C-3942
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

F3240th of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity is rising clearly and on several fronts at once, against Sanofi, and most sharply in the one franchise that matters most. The rise is not yet visible in Dupixent's reported growth but is visible in leading indicators (share of new, biologic-naive patients) and in a dated calendar of competitor events in 2026 to 2029.

  • Dupixent first-line share is falling while total volume rises. A Deutsche Bank dermatology expert panel (September 2026) put Dupixent's share of biologic-naive first-line starts at about 30%, down from about 80%, as Lilly's Ebglyss (every-eight-weeks maintenance dosing against Dupixent's every two weeks) and Galderma's Nemluvio gained. Other experts still report about 95% of practitioners choosing dupilumab as the primary systemic drug and about 80% of eligible patients maintained on it. Both can be true: the installed base is sticky, the new-patient flow is contested. This matters because new-patient share is the leading indicator of the post-2028 growth rate.
  • The next wave is longer-acting or oral, and dated. AbbVie bought Apogee (about USD 10.9bn) for zumilokibart, an IL-13 antibody with 3-to-6-monthly maintenance dosing, Phase III from H2 2026, launch targeted 2029. Kymera's oral STAT6 degrader KT-621 reads out Phase 2b (BROADEN2) around year-end 2026; Phase 1b showed a 63% EASI reduction, similar to Dupixent. Novartis's oral BTK inhibitor Rhapsido has head-to-head trials against Dupixent reading out in 2027. In COPD, AstraZeneca's tozorakimab showed a 43% exacerbation reduction in high-eosinophil patients against 30% to 34% for dupilumab; GSK's twice-yearly depemokimab and Tezspire compete in asthma. Keymed's stapokibart competes in China.
  • Vaccines: Merck's Enflonsia (single fixed dose) in infant RSV, mRNA flu (Moderna approved August 2026), GSK and Pfizer in adult vaccines, PCV21 possibly undifferentiated against Pfizer's PCV20.
  • The 2031 biosimilar wave is already preparing (Formycon FYB208 proof of similarity), and PBMs now own private-label biosimilar vehicles (CVS Cordavis, Cigna Quallent, Optum Nuvaila) that can switch patients automatically.

Direction and magnitude for the model. Through 2027 the effect is on new-patient share and on rebating, not on total Dupixent revenue: we would expect Dupixent growth to slow from the 2026 rate faster than volume alone suggests, because competitors buy access with rebates (experts report aggressive rebating in dermatology already squeezing net prices). From 2029, zumilokibart and any successful oral agent cap Dupixent's peak and steepen the post-2031 decline. The skew is negative: KT-621 success at year-end 2026 would be the first dated, high-impact event, and it would hit the share price before it hits earnings.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

C+5011th of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: broadly neutral for margins, mildly negative for cash flow, with one concentrated supplier risk that matters more than all raw-material questions combined.

  • Raw materials and API: low exposure. Only about 5% of Sanofi's active ingredients come from Asia (industry about 80%); about 60% of small-molecule APIs are made internally; Euroapi supplies 86 APIs under contracts with raw-material and energy pass-through. Gross margin is about 80% on the business basis, so input inflation is a small lever.
  • Capital cost of localisation is the real input-cost change. USD 20bn of US investment through 2030 (the price of the tariff exemption), a EUR 1bn Beijing insulin site, a Frankfurt insulin API facility (full production 2032), a CAD 925m Toronto flu facility (opened September 2026). Consensus capital expenditure rises to EUR 2.8bn to 3.4bn a year in 2026 to 2028 against about EUR 1.7bn of fixed-asset capex in 2023 to 2025 on FactSet's narrow definition (the peer table shows capex/revenue rising from 4% to 6%). This is a cash-flow headwind of roughly 1 to 2 percentage points of sales, not a margin headwind.
  • Supplier concentration that matters: Regeneron manufactures and supplies Dupixent; AstraZeneca manufactures Beyfortus. The AstraZeneca relationship is in ICC arbitration (commenced June 2026, Sanofi counterclaims), and AstraZeneca booked a 46% Beyfortus miss blamed on Sanofi's inventory reductions. Neither is a margin input cost; both are single points of failure.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

B457th of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the macro force that bites hardest is the euro against the US dollar, at the company level; healthcare demand itself is non-cyclical. At sector and industry level the binding macro forces are the discount rate and government budgets, which act on the multiple and on European pricing rather than on volume.

  • Company level (hardest): currency. The US dollar was 43% of sales in FY2023, 50.8% in FY2025, 56% in Q2 2026 and 60.4% in Q3 2026, while the cost base is euro-heavy (estimated 45% of cost of goods and 40% of operating costs in euros against 21% of revenue in FY2025, 15% in Q3 2026). Company sensitivity: a 0.05 USD/EUR strengthening of the euro reduces annual sales by EUR 1,069m and business EPS by EUR 0.23 (about 2.6% of 2026 consensus EPS). FX cut reported sales growth by 5.1, 2.7 and 3.7 points in FY2023, FY2024 and FY2025, and business EPS growth by 5 points in FY2025 (10% reported against 15% at constant rates). Hedging covers at most 12 months, and commercial hedges do not get hedge accounting. The exposure is rising as Dupixent's US share rises; it is structural, not cyclical. The FX consensus (Rabobank, BayernLB, Morgan Stanley) is a range-bound euro near 1.14 to 1.16 with a tail to 1.30 on aggressive Federal Reserve cuts and a tail below parity on a French presidential election shock in 2027.
  • Regeneron transaction exposure amplifies it: profit-share payments to Regeneron are dollar-denominated and rose to USD 2,575m in Q3 2026 from USD 1,456m.
  • Sector level: the discount rate and rotation. The Fitness for Fighting digest (13 November 2026) judges the regime "risk-off beneath an index at its 52-week high" with defensives leading; Pharma/biotech is Overweight. The sector's relative strength gain in October 2026 came entirely on index down days (money moving, which can reverse). This acts on the multiple, not on earnings.
  • Country level: France is Double Underweight on sovereign stress (French premium 150 basis points); Sanofi's revenue is only about 15% to 21% euro, so the France tilt applies to its listing more than its business, but a French election shock is the route to the "euro below parity" tail, which would be strongly positive for Sanofi's reported earnings.
  • Industry level: healthcare budgets. Germany's EUR 42.3bn savings target, pharma chairs' open letter on European budgets that do not adjust for inflation, and China's procurement regime act on price.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–45

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: three other external dynamics matter, two negative and one positive.

  • Negative: the industry-wide patent cliff makes late-stage assets expensive exactly when Sanofi must buy. USD 160bn to 275bn of industry revenue faces loss of exclusivity by about 2030; every large-cap with a cliff is bidding for the same late-stage and commercial assets (AbbVie for Apogee, Novartis for Avidity at USD 12bn, Merck, AstraZeneca). Sanofi's stated pivot to larger, later-stage acquisitions is therefore a pivot into the most crowded and expensive part of the market. The peer precedent is not encouraging: Novartis's USD 12bn Avidity lead asset (del-desiran) failed Phase III in September 2026, and Novartis also lost pelacarsen (HORIZON), removing up to USD 8.9bn of risked peak sales.
  • Negative: vaccine scepticism and US vaccine policy (covered in C-01 and C-04), a sector-specific external force acting on 17% of sales.
  • Positive: Chinese biotech as a cheap source of innovation. Sanofi has made China a priority both commercially and for in-licensing (cardiovascular Phase III assets, rovadicitinib licence, local fund investments). This is the most cost-effective source of mid-stage assets in the industry today, but is exposed to the US BIOSECURE Act and to geopolitics.
  • Positive for the multiple: rotation into cheap defensives (Fitness for Fighting: Healthcare relative strength A; the Sector Digest's "worry clearing at a low multiple" story names Sanofi the purest example at 7.5x). This is a share-price dynamic, not an earnings one.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

F3540th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the material threats are orthogonal and innovative entrants (oral degraders, ultra-long-acting antibodies), followed at 2031 by standard biosimilar entrants with PBM backing; adjacent-market entrants are a lesser threat.

  • Orthogonal or innovative (highest threat, 2027 to 2030): Kymera KT-621 (oral STAT6; Phase 2b year-end 2026), Apogee/AbbVie zumilokibart, Arcus oral TNFR1, Chinese biotechs (Keymed stapokibart in China). If one oral agent matches Dupixent's efficacy with a clean safety profile, the effect on new-patient share is large and fast.
  • Standard entrants (highest threat, 2031 onward): biosimilar dupilumab (Formycon and others), abbreviated pathways (regulators may waive Phase III for biosimilars), PBM private-label vehicles.
  • Adjacent entrants (moderate): AI-native discovery companies and Chinese CROs/CDMOs lower the cost of entry for mid-size biotechs; they increase the number of competing molecules, which compresses class pricing over time.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

F4233rd of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: commodity suppliers are a support; strategic suppliers (partners who manufacture Sanofi's two biggest growth products) are a source of risk. Regeneron manufactures and supplies Dupixent and takes an escalating share of its profit (US profits split equally; ex-US on a 55% to 65% sliding scale), and its economics improved further in the October 2026 deal; it is cooperative but extracts value. AstraZeneca manufactures Beyfortus (three filling lines now approved) and is in arbitration with Sanofi Pasteur. Sobi's US Beyfortus royalty steps up from 25% to 30%-35% by 2028, a rising cost. Euroapi (86 APIs; Sanofi holds about 30% and subscribed EUR 200m of its hybrid bond) is restructuring. West Pharmaceutical's dual sourcing for Dupixent packaging reduces a historic single-source risk. Low Asian API exposure (5%) is a genuine strength.

1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0166C-0335C-0450C-0870C-3262C-3748C-4360
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

C+6610th of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: demand for Sanofi's growth franchises is changing structurally upward and is visible to about 2030; demand for vaccines and legacy general medicines is structurally flat to down and less visible. The visibility of the growth leg is high because it rests on under-penetrated chronic disease, not on an economic cycle.

  • Immunology (type-2 inflammation) is the structural driver. Advanced therapy penetration in atopic dermatitis is about 20% in the US (Regeneron, high-teens to 20%) and biologic penetration in COPD is single-digit (10% to 12% of eligible patients). Dupixent has nine approved indications and more than 1.5m patients on therapy; it passed EUR 5bn of quarterly sales twice in 2026 (Q2 EUR 5,154m; Q3 EUR 5,687m, +34.8% at constant exchange rates). Indication expansion (COPD launched in eight countries, chronic spontaneous urticaria including children aged 2 to 11 from April 2026, Canadian reimbursement for four more indications from October 2026) adds patients cumulatively because these are chronic treatments. This is a two-plus-year visible structural trend; management's 2030 target was raised to about EUR 25bn from EUR 22bn.
  • The quality of the 2026 acceleration is lower than the headline. Morgan Stanley put the underlying Q2 Dupixent beat at about 11% after stripping a EUR 100m to 200m US gross-to-net (GTN) release (reported 15%); in Q3 GTN releases on Dupixent, Lantus and Toujeo were roughly half of the 9% sales beat (Jefferies about EUR 300m). Management itself says pricing will normalise in 2027 to the historical "slightly negative" pattern. So demand (volume) is strong; reported revenue growth in 2026 overstates it.
  • Launch medicines (ALTUVIIIO, Ayvakit, Rezurock, Sarclisa, Qfitlia, Tzield) grew 31% to EUR 1,390m in Q3 2026; the 2030 target of about EUR 10bn was held. Demand here is structural (rare disease, switching from older factor therapies) with good visibility for ALTUVIIIO and Ayvakit, weaker for Tzield and Qfitlia.
  • Vaccines (about 17% of sales) are the weak leg. Vaccine sales fell 6.6% in Q3 2026; 2026 is guided to a low-single-digit decline; the 2030 aspiration was cut to about EUR 9bn from more than EUR 10bn. Drivers are partly cyclical (Northern Hemisphere delivery phasing into Q4; US CDC central orders for Beyfortus down, US Beyfortus -39.3% in Q3) and partly structural (US vaccine-policy hostility and falling immunisation rates; declining birth rates, with polio-pertussis-Hib primary vaccines -17.2% in international markets; competition from Merck's Enflonsia in infant RSV and mRNA flu). Visibility is low and the skew is negative.
  • General medicines (Lantus, Toujeo, Plavix, Lovenox) are in managed structural decline, flattered in 2026 by US GTN releases on insulin; about EUR 1.3bn of 2027 sales leaves with the Cheplapharm and Medley disposals.

What it means for the model. Group demand growth at constant currency is roughly: immunology +20% to 30% in 2026 falling toward high single digits by 2028 to 2030; launches +25% to 30% fading to mid-teens; vaccines flat to slightly down; general medicines -5% to -10% before disposals. The weighted volume picture supports high-single-digit underlying growth in 2026 and mid-single-digit thereafter, before the 2031 cliff. The cone is narrow for 2026 and 2027 and widens sharply after 2029.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

F3542nd of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: two disruption forces are strengthening and both act against Sanofi; one is still unproven and acts weakly in its favour.

  • Against: convenience disruption of injectable biologics (strengthening). Oral small molecules (STAT6 degraders, BTK inhibitors) and ultra-long-acting antibodies (dosing two to four times a year against 26 Dupixent injections) attack the one weakness of Dupixent's otherwise leading profile. The evidence strengthened in 2026 (AbbVie's purchase of Apogee; KT-621 enrolment completed six months early; Rhapsido approval). Sanofi's own defence is real but late: four long-acting antibodies from the expanded Regeneron alliance (registration-enabling studies from late 2027 or early 2028) and its own oral STAT6 degrader with Nurix (Phase 1 from August 2026). The defence arrives after the attackers.
  • Against: vertically integrated PBM biosimilars (strengthening, acts from 2031). This changes the shape of the cliff: historically biologics lost share gradually; the new model lets the payer switch patients by formulary. Novartis lost about 96% to 97% of US sacubitril (Entresto) share within a year of generic entry, against a 70% to 85% historical base rate; that precedent, although small-molecule, is the right warning for Dupixent's slope.
  • Against: mRNA in vaccines (partly proven). Sanofi terminated its own mRNA flu programme in Q4 2025 (effectively writing off much of the USD 3bn Translate Bio purchase) and is betting on non-mRNA recombinant and Novavax-adjuvanted combinations (flu-COVID Phase III started in Europe, launch planned for 2029). If mRNA combinations win the convenience race, Sanofi's flu franchise (its largest vaccine line) is exposed.
  • For (unproven): AI in discovery. Sanofi claims 90 novel targets advanced in a year, 75% of the small-molecule pipeline touched by AI, a Recursion candidate optioned for Phase 1 in 2027, and the highest AI share of in-licensing value among the five largest buyers (31%). Experts are consistent that AI has not yet improved clinical success rates; the value is unproven until Phase 2 data. It should not be in the model.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

D5023rd of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: significant change is under way, and its net effect on the next 18 months is less negative than feared a year ago (an open-ended US threat has become a known, capped cost), while its effect on years three to six is a steady drip of pressure, concentrated on exactly the years the Dupixent cliff arrives.

  • US pricing agreement (December 2025): a worry clearing. Sanofi agreed Medicaid prices aligned with other high-income nations (average -61% on certain medicines), TrumpRx direct-to-consumer discounts (about 70% on certain cardiovascular and diabetes drugs) and USD 20bn of US investment through 2030, in exchange for a three-year Section 232 tariff exemption (to about 20 January 2029). The Sector Digest's judgement, which we share, is that this converts an open-ended risk into a capped cost; Medicaid is a small share of Dupixent (about 70% of its US business is in commercial channels). The cost is already in 2026 numbers and guidance.
  • Inflation Reduction Act: Dupixent is insulated until about 2031 (biologics are negotiated 13 years after approval). Two drips: the Medicare Part D redesign (manufacturer liability in the catastrophic phase; experts estimate about 5% sector revenue impact) and the November 2026 CMS rule grouping subcutaneous and intravenous versions together for negotiation, which weakens route-of-administration lifecycle defences across the industry.
  • Europe: Germany's Health Finance Commission targets EUR 42.3bn of 2027 savings (dynamic manufacturer rebates; mandatory rebate rising to 15.5% in January 2027); EU pharmaceutical legislation may shorten data protection; EU joint clinical assessment slows launches. Europe is about 21% of Sanofi's sales (15% in Q3), so the effect is a margin drip, not a thesis change.
  • US vaccine policy is the most adverse regulatory force (a May 2026 Executive Order on childhood schedules, ACIP changes, falling coverage). It bears on the vaccines segment specifically and is the reason the 2030 vaccines ambition was cut.
  • China: NRDL entry cuts averaging about 60%; volume-based procurement for insulin (where Sanofi won a renewed EUR 1bn contract).
  • Dates that matter: US midterms November 2026 (Jefferies: a Democratic Congress is more negative for long-term pricing), tariff exemptions end January 2029, Dupixent IRA selection around 2031.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

B709th of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: yes, strongly, for now. The industry-level shift of spending toward specialty biologics in chronic inflammatory disease is the single biggest reason Sanofi is growing at double digits for the first time in two decades.

  • Specialty therapies are expected to reach 46% of global medicine spend by 2029 (42% in 2024); immunology, oncology and diabetes are about 43% of global pharmaceutical value; global spending grows 5% to 8% a year to 2030. Within that, type-2 inflammation is one of the fastest-growing pools, and Dupixent is its leading product with 50% to 55% share among biologic-prescribing US dermatologists.
  • Competitor activity is also expanding the market: Deutsche Bank notes competitor consumer advertising drives overall advanced-therapy penetration even as it dilutes Dupixent's capture rate. In the short term the market grows faster than Sanofi's share falls; in the medium term that reverses.
  • The US is the accelerating geography (US sales +23.9% in Q3 2026 against Europe -0.9% and China +2.6%), which is why both growth and dollar exposure rise together.

For the model: the industry driver supports Dupixent volume growth in the mid-to-high teens through 2027, slowing to high single digits by 2029 to 2030 as share is ceded; that is consistent with management's EUR 25bn 2030 target (about a 5% CAGR from about EUR 20bn in 2026), which looks undemanding on volume and demanding only on price.

C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

C6213th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: patient and physician demand strongly supports the 2026 to 2028 earnings thesis and is only moderately fragile in that window; it is weak for vaccines. Physicians express deep loyalty to Dupixent on its long safety record across nine indications and in children; stable patients rarely switch; demand is expanding into COPD (eosinophil testing now routine; pulmonologists prefer Dupixent above 300 cells/μL) and urticaria. Fragility lies in new-patient choice (convenience competitors) and in payer behaviour (prior authorisation, step therapy). Vaccine demand is fragile: lower immunisation rates, CDC order cuts, birth-rate declines, misinformation. ALTUVIIIO demand (85% US) depends on switching from older factor therapies, which continues but faces Mim8 and Hemlibra loyalty.

C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

D4827th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: in the next 18 months regulators and politicians are net neutral to mildly helpful (the US agreement caps the downside and grants tariff exemption; IRA spares Dupixent until 2031; approvals continue to flow: Qfitlia, Infilbray, VaxRabeo); over three to six years they are net hurtful and the position is fragile (Part D redesign, CMS subcutaneous/intravenous grouping, Dupixent's IRA selection around 2031, tariff exemption expiry January 2029, German rebates, EU data protection, US vaccine policy, FDA scrutiny of safety signals: tolebrutinib's complete response letter for liver injury and Infilbray's psychiatric warnings show the FDA's safety bar is high). The fragility is political: a Democratic Congress after the November 2026 midterms would be more negative for long-term pricing (Jefferies).

C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

D6023rd of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the addressable markets are large relative to Sanofi's current share and allow mid-to-high single-digit growth through 2030 for the existing portfolio; the constraint is not market size but exclusivity and competition.

  • Type-2 inflammatory disease (atopic dermatitis at about 20% advanced-therapy penetration; asthma at about 25% biologic penetration; COPD at single digits; urticaria, prurigo nodularis, nasal polyps, eosinophilic oesophagitis) supports the EUR 25bn Dupixent target without heroic share assumptions; COPD alone is a USD 13bn opportunity by one estimate.
  • Global vaccines about USD 50bn (Sanofi about EUR 7.5bn to 8bn), growing slowly; adult and combination vaccines are the growth pools.
  • Rare disease and hemophilia: niches of USD 1bn to 10bn each with high prices.
  • Global pharma spend about USD 2.1trn growing 5% to 8% a year.
  • Mid-term growth allowed: about 6% a year for 2026 to 2030 (consensus revenue CAGR roughly 6% from the 2025 base, Redburn's EUR 61.4bn 2029 projection implies about 6.75% from 2024) before any acquisitions. Beyond 2030 the addressable market is irrelevant; exclusivity decides.
1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0655C-1050
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

D5526th of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Sanofi is at or near the peak rate of its current product cycle in 2026, and the rate of growth turns down in 2027, while the level keeps rising to about 2030. The cycle that matters, Dupixent's patent life, has about four years to run before the level turns.

  • Phases: 2019 to 2023 flat (revenue EUR 36bn to 43bn including consumer; growth largely offset by Lantus and Plavix erosion and FX); 2024 to 2026 acceleration (FY2025 +9.9% at constant exchange rates; 2026 guided to low-teens, Q2 +17.8%, Q3 +14.3%); 2027 to 2030 deceleration to mid-single-digit growth (consensus revenue +5.6% in 2027, +6.2% in 2028); 2031 to 2034 a decline phase unless replaced (Bernstein's base case for the industry analogue, Novartis, has five to six years of falling sales after its cliff).
  • The 2026 to 2027 turn is mechanical and already announced: (a) about EUR 400m to 500m of 2026 US GTN releases will not recur; (b) about EUR 1.3bn of sales leaves with Cheplapharm and Medley (2.5 percentage points of growth); (c) pricing returns to "slightly negative"; (d) vaccine phasing normalises. Underlying volume growth stays healthy, so the turn is in the rate, not the direction.
  • Leading indicators to watch: Dupixent new-to-brand share (falling), US weekly prescription trackers (Deutsche Bank publishes them), Beyfortus US orders for the 2026/27 season, and whether management's 2027 guidance (late January 2027) frames growth ex-divestments.

For the model: 2026 is a peak year for reported growth; do not extrapolate it. The second derivative of revenue growth turns negative in 2027 with high confidence.

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

D5027th of 42

Layer 1 · External change forces

By the four thematic drivers the Sector Digest names:

  • US pricing agreements (a worry clearing): a moderate tailwind to the multiple, small cost to earnings, already in numbers. Tailwind, moderate.
  • Obesity as a mass-market pill: Sanofi has no obesity franchise; neutral directly, mildly negative indirectly (obesity absorbs payer budgets and investor attention; Sanofi competes for neither).
  • Rare-disease launches lifting margins: a modest tailwind (ALTUVIIIO, Ayvakit, Rezurock, Qfitlia, Infilbray approved 18 November 2026), sized well below the Dupixent effect.
  • The 2027 to 2032 patent expiries: the dominant headwind for Sanofi specifically, because its own cliff (2031) is the largest single-product cliff in the European large-cap group, and because peers' cliffs inflate the price of replacement assets.

At value-chain level: headwinds from consolidated buyers (three wholesalers 42% of sales in H1 2026; three GPOs about 85% of US claims) and from PBM-owned biosimilars; tailwinds from low input-cost exposure. At industry level: a strong demand tailwind (specialty immunology) set against a rising competitive headwind in the same space. At sector level: the multiple has room to rise on a worry clearing; government budgets drip.

Overall picture: balanced, with a clear time structure. For 2026 and 2027 tailwinds dominate (demand, capped US pricing cost, launches); from 2028 headwinds dominate (new-patient share loss, long-acting and oral competitors from 2029, the 2031 cliff, tariff exemption expiry in 2029, Germany and EU reforms). The cone of external outcomes widens sharply after 2028 and is skewed to the downside, while the next 12 months are skewed modestly to the upside on demand and to the downside on currency (any euro strength).

Internal change forces/risks

–not yet rated

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

A new CEO, R&D head and deal-oriented CFO remit have produced fast, credible operational change: costs held, R&D governance rebuilt, the portfolio simplified, the Regeneron relationship repaired, and 2026 guidance raised three times. These changes underwrite the next two years of earnings. They do not yet address the post-2031 gap, which management now says must be filled externally; the coming strategy update and the first large acquisition are therefore the pivotal internal events. For the model: carry recurring restructuring as a cost, hold R&D roughly flat in 2027, include the Cheplapharm/Medley revenue removal and the Regeneron reimbursement loss, and build an explicit M&A scenario with debt funding rather than assume the balance sheet stays idle.

2a

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement50C-1462C-8632C-5936C-8142D-0356
Group judgement

Risks

A503rd of 42

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The core engine is not decelerating yet, and the stock's valuation already sits near its floor, which caps the share-price downside at roughly 15% to 20% in plausible cases. The risks that matter are concentrated and correlated: a high-priced acquisition, a successful oral or ultra-long-acting competitor, and the 2027 optics of three tailwinds reversing at once, all within the next six months to a year. For the model: build a base case without a deal and an explicit acquisition case; put the euro/dollar rate and the Dupixent new-patient share into the sensitivity table; assume no GTN releases from 2027.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

D6218th of 42

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: standards are visibly improving in cost discipline, working capital and R&D governance, and the improvement is the most credible part of the new management's record. The weakness is that repeated restructuring is itself a recurring cost and has damaged culture.

  • Cost discipline: Q3 2026 R&D of EUR 1,849m (+0.8% year on year, 7% below consensus) and SG&A of EUR 2,309m (-0.9% at constant rates, 6% below consensus) on sales +14%. Headcount fell to 74,846 in 2025 from 86,088 in 2023 (-13%, mostly Opella and France), with R&D headcount held.
  • Working capital: inventory cut by nearly 30 days in 2025 (a EUR 2.1bn working-capital inflow helped FY2025 free cash flow to EUR 8.1bn, 18.5% of sales); a further 30-day reduction targeted in 2026. Net working capital is still high at 27.7% of last-twelve-month sales in H1 2026 (about 100 days), so there is more to release.
  • R&D governance: scientific review boards; halting Phase 2-to-3 transitions pending review; decisive termination of assets that lacked differentiation. This is the right change; its output cannot be judged before 2028.
  • Counterweight: cash restructuring costs were about 10% of FY2025 free cash flow, the highest among European peers (1% to 4%), and have run at EUR 1.4bn to 2.2bn a year (EUR 1,396m in 2024; EUR 563m in H1 2026). Former employees describe a "transactional", insecure culture and attrition after successive reorganisations (including elimination of the US paediatric sales force). Restructuring that recurs every year is an operating cost the model should carry, not exclude.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

F3236th of 42

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the negatives are concentrated in R&D attrition, partner economics and organisational fatigue, and they are serious because they are cumulative.

  • Pipeline attrition in 2025 to 2026: amlitelimab in atopic dermatitis (Kaposi's sarcoma signal; EUR 952m impairment), itepekimab in COPD, balinatunfib, tolebrutinib (FDA complete response letter December 2025, PERSEUS failure), riliprubart (MOBILIZE terminated), venglustat in Fabry, SAR445399 paused in bronchiectasis; frexalimab Phase III readout delayed to 2028; efdoralprin alfa US filing delayed to 2027. Management concedes existing assets cannot cover the post-2031 loss.
  • Partner economics worsen: Regeneron's development balance was fully repaid in Q2 2026, ending deductions and lifting Regeneron's profit share (other operating expenses rose to EUR 2,168m in Q3 from EUR 1,303m). The end of Regeneron development reimbursement costs about EUR 300m of business operating income in 2026 and EUR 800m in 2027. New alliance terms (50:50, USD 7bn milestones; Regeneron's option on lunsekimig) are better for Regeneron than the original Dupixent deal (Jefferies).
  • AstraZeneca arbitration on Beyfortus (June 2026) and pediatrician frustration with Beyfortus return policies.
  • Organisational fatigue: senior turnover, thin bench, transactional culture, high restructuring cost (C-14).
  • Plausible: an expensive, dilutive acquisition; further pipeline write-downs during the review (the shift of R&D spend from late to early stage will itself create more terminations); a weak 2027 guidance framing in January.
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

F3641st of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement: low congruence. Engagement statistics are good, but repeated restructuring, functional silos and a shift to a transactional environment are reported consistently by former employees, and the R&D culture is being rebuilt rather than reinforced. Positive data: employee empowerment at 78%, about 90% say the ethical culture is strong, graduate conversion 77% (38% in 2024), early-career attrition 12.4%. Negative evidence: "highly transactional and hard-nosed" US specialty culture since late 2024; restructuring programmes (Play to Win, S-Drive) seen as headcount-reduction tools; reduced severance terms; merging regional roles drove experienced directors out; silos hindered launches (Tzield); experts allege unilateral decision-making in the Regeneron relationship. Goals (execution, accountability), strategy (buy science), incentives (TSR 30%, clinical milestones) and culture are not yet a single system; this is a turnaround culture.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

–56

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: highly concentrated, on four counts at once: one product, one country and currency, one partner contract, and one management decision. Two franchises supply almost nine-tenths of gross revenue growth and Dupixent alone rises from 36% of sales in 2025 to about 47% in 2027 and 2028. For the next 18 months this concentration helps predictability more than it hurts, because the concentrated drivers are the ones with the most momentum, the most inertia and the best outside data. Beyond three years the same concentration is the central risk of the case.

What is different about the next four years. Business EPS was flat from 2022 to 2025 (EUR 8.26 to EUR 7.83, through the Opella separation, the 2023 margin reset and currency losses of 3 to 5 points in several years). The forecasts show about 8% a year from 2025 to 2030. The difference is generated by a short list:

Driver of the difference Size in the forecasts How concentrated
Dupixent has become large enough for its growth to dominate +EUR 4.8bn of sales from 2026 to 2028, 58% of gross revenue growth; 43% of sales in 2026, 47% in 2027 and 2028 One product, 77% of it sold in the US, one partner taking about half of the profit
Launch medicines +EUR 2.4bn of sales from 2026 to 2028 (29% of gross growth), but about EUR 1.0bn of the 2027 BOI gain against Dupixent's EUR 1.3bn Six or seven products, of which two (ALTUVIIIO, Ayvakit) do most of the work
A flat cost base Worth about EUR 0.9bn of 2027 BOI against costs growing with sales One management decision, renewed yearly
Royalty income (Amvuttra) About EUR 1bn in 2026 from about EUR 0.5bn in 2025; +EUR 0.28bn assumed for 2027 One product owned and sold by another company (Alnylam), which has a royalty-free successor in Phase III
The absence of past drags No Opella deconsolidation, no margin reset, no large patent loss inside the window Mechanical
Capital allocation A swing of about +8% to -6% on 2028 EPS (second set) One decision by one management team

Against these stand three deterministic negatives that every model already contains (the end of Regeneron's reimbursement, the disposals, the non-recurrence of the Sarclisa reversal) and one diffuse negative (the erosion of general medicines and price across many products and countries).

Four kinds of concentration.

  • Product. Dupixent and launches together are about 87% of gross revenue growth to 2028. Vaccines (17% of sales) are modelled flat and other specialty care slightly down, so nothing else contributes.
  • Geography and currency. The US was 60.4% of sales in the third quarter of 2026 (50.8% in 2025). Growth, pricing risk, policy risk and currency risk therefore sit in the same place and are correlated.
  • Contract. The Regeneron agreement determines how much of the main driver's growth Sanofi keeps. It is deterministic and already in every model, so it concentrates the level of the margin without adding uncertainty.
  • Decision. The largest single swing in the 2028 forecast is whether, what and how large management buys.

Why concentration helps predictability over 18 months. Three reasons, which I think the earlier sets left implicit. First, the dominant driver is a chronic therapy with an installed base: its revenue next year is mostly determined by patients already on it, which is why falling share of new patients shows up slowly. Second, it is the best-observed product in the company: weekly US prescription data, and a partner that reports US and international sales in dollars on the same day. A forecast resting on one well-observed product is easier to track than one resting on thirty poorly observed ones. Third, the consensus revisions of the last twelve months confirm that the driver set is narrow and stable: FY2027 sales estimates rose 5% and EPS estimates rose 5%, almost all of it Dupixent.

That last observation carries a second message. Sales upgrades have been passing into EPS upgrades one for one, not with gearing. A business with ordinary operating leverage would show EPS revisions of 1.3 to 1.5 times its sales revisions. Sanofi's do not, because the upgraded product is the half-shared one. This is the second set's "half-shared leverage" visible in the revision data, and it means future Dupixent beats will lift EPS estimates roughly in proportion and no more.

Why it hurts beyond three years. Every force identified in the first set as converging on 2029 to 2033 (long-acting and oral competitors, pharmacy-benefit-manager biosimilars, Medicare negotiation, the end of the tariff exemption) converges on the one product that will then be nearly half of sales. Concentration is rising as the cliff approaches. The first set made this point for the years after 2030; the numbers above show that the build-up of concentration happens inside the forecast window.

A nuance on launches. They are the more diffuse half of the growth and, because Sanofi keeps more of each euro, the more valuable half per euro. They are also the less well observed half (no partner reporting, patchy prescription data for infused and rare-disease products, growth rates that include the acquired Ayvakit). So the part of the profit growth that is diffuse is also the part that is hardest to monitor. A shortfall there would be seen later than a shortfall in Dupixent.

Internal corporate change forces

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

C+6412th of 42

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: almost the entire top team has changed in nine months, and the change signals a board that has lost patience with the R&D engine and wants execution discipline and deal-making capability, not a new grand strategy.

  • Chief executive: the board did not renew Paul Hudson's mandate (announced 11 to 12 February 2026; shares fell about 5% to 6% that day). Belén Garijo (physician; 15 earlier years at Sanofi-Aventis including Genzyme integration; Merck KGaA healthcare chief from 2015 and group CEO 2021 to 2026) became CEO on 1 May 2026; shareholders raised the age limit to appoint her. The board found no internal candidate ready.
  • R&D: Houman Ashrafian left; Paulo Fontoura (neurologist, ex-Roche, ex-Xaira Therapeutics chief medical officer) became Global Head of R&D on 1 September 2026, with a mandate to replace "commercial wishful thinking" with scientific review boards.
  • Finance and deals: CFO François Roger (ex-Nestlé CFO for nine years, never missed guidance there) had his remit expanded on 1 September 2026 to include Business Operations, Global Partnering and Business Development. This concentrates M&A under the person most associated with discipline; Jefferies reads it as also helping retain him.
  • Other: Specialty Care head (Brian Foard to Manuela Buxo), General Medicines (Olivier Charmeil to Thomas Grenier), General Counsel, Chief People Officer (interim), and China moved under the Vaccines head. Board: Christel Heydemann (Orange CEO) joined; Patrick Kron retired; chairman Frédéric Oudéa took the nominations committee.
  • What it signals: (1) the board acted decisively on delivery, which is a governance positive; (2) the new CEO's first acts were to cut, not to promise (amlitelimab, itepekimab discontinued; all Phase 2-to-3 transitions halted pending review; a six-month post-mortem of research); (3) the combination of a deal-oriented CFO remit and an R&D head who spends about half his time sourcing late-stage assets signals that the bridge to 2031 will be bought.
  • Counterweights: Garijo's Merck KGaA record is mixed (group sales +20% over five years but adjusted EBITDA margin down 210 basis points; late-stage failures of evobrutinib and xevinapant; missed EUR 25bn 2025 revenue target). Her Sanofi record is six months long. Senior turnover is high and the divisional bench thin.
C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

F5636th of 42

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy has changed meaningfully in emphasis (narrower therapeutic focus, science-gated R&D, bigger and later-stage M&A) but not yet in articulated form; the full articulation is due at the strategy update promised before the end of 2026. It is well resourced financially and thinly resourced organically.

  • What changed: therapeutic focus narrowed to immunology, rare diseases and vaccines (oncology deprioritised; the PRO-XTEN oncology platform went to Vir; non-core areas to be reviewed over three years); R&D governance rebuilt around objective Phase 2 data review; R&D spend to be flat in 2027 then grow with sales, with resources shifted from late development to more Phase 1 projects; business development explicitly re-targeted from EUR 2bn to 5bn bolt-ons to larger late-stage and commercial assets; mature medicines outsourced (Cheplapharm); China and AI as named levers.
  • What did not change: the 2030 targets for Dupixent (EUR 25bn) and launches (EUR 10bn); the progressive dividend; the AA credit rating constraint.
  • Resourcing: financial firepower is large (net debt EUR 12.2bn at Q3 2026, about 0.9x EBITDA; stated capacity about EUR 15bn for early-stage and EUR 20bn to 25bn for late-stage assets; Jefferies estimates EUR 20bn to 30bn; Deutsche Bank cites up to EUR 30bn to 50bn for late-stage or commercial assets). Organic resourcing is thin: R&D at 18% of sales is below GSK (20%) and AstraZeneca (23%), and the late-stage pipeline has been emptied.
  • Articulation quality: the direction is clear; the quantitative bridge to 2031 is missing. Rothschild & Co Redburn and Deutsche Bank both frame the December strategy update as the clearing event.
C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

B+705th of 42

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: near-term guidance has been raised repeatedly and materially; mid-term product targets were raised for Dupixent and cut for vaccines; there is no mid-term margin or EPS target, which is the important gap.

  • 2026 guidance, three steps: January 2026 high-single-digit sales growth with business EPS "slightly faster than sales"; July 2026 sales "around 10%"; 30 October 2026 sales "low-teens" and business EPS "mid-teens" at constant exchange rates. FX guidance improved from -2% sales/-3% EPS to about -1%/-1% to -2%.
  • 2030 product ambitions: Dupixent raised to about EUR 25bn from EUR 22bn; launches held at about EUR 10bn; vaccines cut to about EUR 9bn from more than EUR 10bn (half of the cut was currency). The aggregate of the three is about EUR 44bn, up from EUR 42bn like for like.
  • Margins: the 32% business operating margin target was abandoned in October 2023, triggering a large de-rating (Morgan Stanley). Management told Deutsche Bank after Q3 2026 that operating margin should improve "each and every year through 2030", which is directionally helpful but unquantified.
  • Read for the model: 2026 is now well anchored; 2027 has no number, only qualitative headwinds (pricing normalising, R&D flat, Regeneron reimbursement ending, divested sales); 2028 to 2030 has product targets but no profit target. Expect the December update or January 2027 results to set some form of 2027 framework.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

B6610th of 42

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: strongly, and in a consistent direction. Sanofi has become a pure-play biopharma and is now shedding its mature tail. This raises margins and focus, and at the same time raises concentration on Dupixent.

  • Opella (consumer health): 50% sold to CD&R on 30 April 2025 at EUR 16bn enterprise value (about 14x 2024 EBITDA); EUR 10.4bn net cash; 48.2% stake retained (accounted as an associate). About 10% dilutive to business EPS (Jefferies), roughly half offset by the EUR 5bn buyback completed in 2025. Zantac and Gold Bond legal liabilities and some China hospital and Russia distribution stayed with Sanofi.
  • Mature medicines to Cheplapharm (September 2026): 20 mature medicines (including Lovenox/Clexane outside the US, 2025 sales EUR 822m) and three plants (Hungary, Ploërmel in France, Jurong in Singapore) with 548 employees, in exchange for a 26.4% equity stake in Cheplapharm. Transfer runs from Q1 to Q3 2027.
  • Medley (Brazil generics): sale to EMS agreed March 2026, closing no earlier than Q4 2026 (reported asking price at least USD 500m, about 11.4x 2026 EBITDA per Morgan Stanley). Cheplapharm plus Medley reduce 2027 sales by about EUR 1.3bn; smaller disposals cut about EUR 200m from 2026.
  • Other exits: Enjaymo to Recordati (USD 825m upfront); oncology deprioritised (PRO-XTEN to Vir); the Kiadis cell platform written off (EUR 877m impairments); the M1Pram diabetes option allowed to lapse.
  • For the model: remove about EUR 1.3bn of revenue from 2027 (and the residual from 2026), with lower-than-group gross margin; add equity-accounted income from the Cheplapharm stake (Deutsche Bank estimates the deal adds about EUR 440m of EBITDA to Cheplapharm; Sanofi's 26.4% share of net income will be small) and from Opella; deferred payments from Cheplapharm begin in 2028. The net effect on 2027 business EPS is a small negative (perhaps 1% to 2%), on margin a small positive.
C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

F5234th of 42

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: modestly and mostly sensibly. The expansion is inside existing therapeutic areas and geographies; the only genuinely new markets are China-sourced innovation and new vaccine modalities.

  • Products: rare disease and immunology via Blueprint (Ayvakit, systemic mastocytosis; USD 9.1bn), Dren Bio, Vigil (neurology, TREM2), Recursion, Nurix (oral STAT6); adult vaccines via Dynavax (Heplisav-B hepatitis B, shingles candidate Z-1018; USD 2.2bn) and Vicebio (RSV-hMPV; USD 1.15bn upfront); Novavax combination flu-COVID; ScionxBio (long-acting RNA) and Seawolf (redosable DNA delivery, up to USD 1.2bn milestones) in bleeding disorders. These are close adjacencies (shared customers or skills), which the Wisdom Library's "Core versus Adjacency" entry treats as the success-prone kind.
  • Geography: China is named a priority both as a market (China sales +2.6% in Q3 2026, under pricing pressure) and as an innovation source; the US is the growth geography (US sales +23.9% in Q3) and is being localised (USD 20bn investment, 50% of US volume made locally today, about 25% of manufacturing).
  • Sense check: the adult-vaccine and rare-disease moves are sensible and sized modestly. The weakness is that none of these is large enough to matter against a EUR 20bn Dupixent cliff; the market entries are portfolio hygiene, not a bridge.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

F3040th of 42

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent?

Judgement: Sanofi is not raising prices in real terms. Group pricing is slightly negative, the 2026 price contribution is flattered by non-recurring US rebate releases, and the policy direction in the US, Europe and China is toward lower net prices.

  • Management's own framing: the CFO says sales growth has historically come from volume with a "slightly negative pricing contribution" each year, and expects 2027 to return to that pattern after 2026's favourable US adjustments.
  • The 2026 "price" is accounting, not pricing power: GTN releases (prior-period rebate accrual reversals) of about EUR 100m to 200m in Q2 and about EUR 300m in Q3 (Jefferies; one-third to one-half of the Q3 sales beat). These are estimates being trued up, not prices being raised.
  • Direct price concessions: Medicaid prices -61% on average for certain medicines; TrumpRx discounts; Lantus list price -78% (January 2024); China NRDL entry cuts averaging about 60%; German mandatory rebate up to 15.5% from January 2027.
  • Latent pricing power exists in two places only: Dupixent (experts say its dominance lets Sanofi pay low rebates and standard fees; more than 70% of US Dupixent is in commercial channels) and Beyfortus (no price erosion in 2025, defended by real-world evidence). Both are now under competitive pressure (rebating in dermatology; Enflonsia in RSV), so the latent power is being spent defending volume rather than raising price.
  • For the model: use group net price of about -1% a year from 2027, with Dupixent US net price flat to slightly down and Europe, China and general medicines down.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

F4837th of 42

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: M&A has stepped up and is about to step up much further in size. Strategically it is necessary (the organic bridge to 2031 has failed); its discipline and integration record are mixed; the risk is overpaying in the most crowded asset market the industry has seen.

  • Recent deals: Blueprint Medicines (USD 9.1bn upfront, June 2025; Ayvakit growing 46% to EUR 203m in Q3 2026, the best of the recent deals), Dynavax (USD 2.2bn, closed early 2026, EUR 1,645m cash), Vicebio (USD 1.15bn), Vigil, Dren Bio (USD 600m plus USD 100m), the expanded Regeneron alliance (USD 1bn upfront plus up to USD 7bn milestones, 50:50 profits) and many licences. Bernstein estimates acquisitions contributed about 6.3 percentage points of inorganic growth recently.
  • The step-up now signalled: the CEO says the group will fund larger-scale business development focused on later-stage and commercial assets; Jefferies reports Sanofi considering Insmed (about USD 27bn market value). Redburn notes a shift from the former EUR 2bn to 5bn bolt-on range. The R&D head spends about half his time on external sourcing.
  • Record (the Wisdom Library's "Cockroach Pattern at M&A Integration Level" applies): Genzyme (2011) built the rare-disease franchise and is a success; Bioverativ (2018) produced ALTUVIIIO, a success; Ablynx (Cablivi) met expectations; Provention Bio (Tzield) relaunch struggled (experts describe screening bottlenecks); Translate Bio (USD 3bn) was effectively written off when mRNA flu was terminated; Kiadis was impaired. Roughly half the large deals of the last decade have created value.
  • Discipline signals: the CEO states every deal must meet strategic fit, scientific differentiation and financial return criteria; the CFO now owns M&A the AA rating is a stated constraint. Deutsche Bank (Papadakis) and Redburn warn that large, expensive deals could depress the multiple through investor anxiety.
  • For the model: a base case should include at least one late-stage or commercial acquisition of EUR 10bn to 25bn by end-2027, financed by debt (net debt/EBITDA rising toward 1.5x to 2.0x), initially dilutive to business EPS by low-to-mid single digits through financing cost and pre-launch spend, and accretive only from about 2029 if the asset launches. Business EPS excludes amortisation of acquired intangibles, so dilution on the brokers' basis is smaller than on IFRS.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

C5817th of 42

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the balance sheet is under-used and management is clearly mooting using it, but for acquisitions, not for buybacks. Shareholder returns are steady (progressive dividend, modest buybacks), not stepping up.

  • Balance sheet: net debt EUR 11.0bn at end-2025, EUR 15.5bn at June 2026 (dividend and Dynavax), EUR 12.2bn at September 2026; about 0.9x EBITDA; AA rating; EUR 2.3bn bond issued April 2026 (2029, 2033, 2037 tranches). Interest rate swaps take fixed debt to about 85%. Redburn models net cash of EUR 5.2bn by 2029 without M&A, which illustrates the capacity being built.
  • Returns: dividend EUR 4.12 for 2025 (31 consecutive increases; about 4.9% yield at EUR 83.79; payout about 53% of business EPS); consensus DPS EUR 4.24, 4.46, 4.66 for 2026 to 2028. Buybacks: EUR 5bn in 2025 (Opella-funded; it lifted 2025 EPS growth to 15% from 12.2%), EUR 1bn in 2026 (completed April). No new buyback has been announced. EUR 10bn was returned to shareholders in 2025.
  • What management is mooting: larger M&A within a leverage of about 1x after acquisitions (CFO) and an investment-grade constraint. At 8x forward earnings a buyback earns an earnings yield above 12%, which is higher than any plausible return on a late-stage acquisition at today's prices; the fact that management prefers M&A tells you its priority is the post-2031 bridge, not per-share arithmetic.
  • For the model: assume the dividend grows 3% to 5% a year; assume no further buyback in 2027 unless the strategy update announces one; share count roughly flat at about 1,212m diluted.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–60

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: several real positives exist that are under-weighted in the bear narrative, each modest on its own.

  • The Regeneron relationship has been reset: the October 2026 expansion resolved outstanding litigation, added four long-acting next-generation antibodies (IL-13, IL-4, IL-4Rα and an IL-4xIL-13 bispecific; binding affinity up to 100 times higher; 3-to-6-month dosing; registration-enabling studies by late 2027 or early 2028), and preserves the Dupixent sales force and payer relationships for the successor. This is the most credible lifecycle defence available.
  • Royalty income: Alnylam's Amvuttra royalty steps up to 30% on sales above USD 1.5bn (Q2 2026 Amvuttra sales USD 1,012m); Jefferies estimates about EUR 1bn a year; Deutsche Bank expects about EUR 500m more than 2025. This partly offsets the end of Regeneron development reimbursement.
  • Approvals in November 2026: Infilbray (venglustat; first oral therapy for neurological manifestations of type 3 Gaucher disease; label carries psychiatric warnings) and VaxRabeo (EU, rabies). Positive for diversification, small in size.
  • Working capital and tax: a further 30-day inventory reduction; tax rate structurally about 20% after Opella (from 21%).
  • Plausible: a larger buyback if the strategy update chooses per-share returns over a big deal; partial value crystallisation of the Opella and Cheplapharm stakes; method-of-use patents surviving challenge and moderating the post-2031 slope (Morgan Stanley cites possible extension of indication exclusivity into the mid-2030s).
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

D5019th of 42

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: there is no activist and no controlling shareholder pushing for change; the board itself has been the change agent. The one blockholder, L'Oréal, is a potential seller rather than a strategic actor.

  • No activist holds a declared stake; major shareholders are described as aligned with the refocused plan (Bernstein).
  • L'Oréal holds 7.27% of capital and 13.10% of voting rights (double voting rights); L'Oréal does not regard the stake as strategic; two directors are L'Oréal nominees. A placement would be an overhang event but would also normalise the voting structure.
  • The board's decision not to renew the previous CEO is the clearest evidence that governance can force change without outside pressure. The Leadership digest flags activist risk if share-price underperformance persists.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

D6028th of 42

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the positive changes are broad (leadership, governance, cost, working capital, portfolio, alliance reset) and financially well resourced, but they are mostly defensive and hygiene-type; the one change that would alter the long-term case, a credible post-2031 bridge, has not happened yet and will have to be bought. The near-term changes support the 2026 to 2028 earnings path (cost discipline, R&D held flat in 2027, inventory release, tax, Amvuttra). The long-term change depends on M&A quality under a six-month-old CEO whose prior record on R&D outcomes is mixed. Strength: moderate; breadth: high; resourcing: high in money, low in organic science.

C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

F3842nd of 42

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the staircase is thin in clinical novelty and front-loaded with corporate events whose sign is uncertain; the most novel positive steps (successor immunology antibodies, combination vaccines) arrive in months 18 to 36 and beyond, near the cliff they are meant to defend.

Next 6 months (to May 2027):

  • Strategy update (before end-2026): the most important dated event; novel (first under the new CEO); sign uncertain (positive if capital-return discipline and clear deal criteria; negative if a high-priced acquisition).
  • FY2026 results and the 2027 framework (28 January 2027): likely a beat on 2026 (implied Q4 is undemanding); the 2027 framing is the risk (C-68).
  • Launch updates: Infilbray (US) and VaxRabeo (EU) approved 18 to 19 November 2026; small but new.
  • Competitor events (negative skew): KT-621 Phase 2b around year-end 2026.
  • Portfolio: Medley closing; Cheplapharm transfer starting Q1 2027; Q1 2027 sales (April).
  • Possible: a large acquisition (novel; sign depends on price).

Months 6 to 18 (to May 2028):

  • PCV21 paediatric Phase III data (2027; management concedes it may not be sufficiently differentiated from Pfizer's PCV20, so weakly founded);
  • efdoralprin alfa US submission (2027);
  • US Phase III start of the Novavax flu-COVID combination (2027);
  • Regeneron next-generation long-acting antibody programmes moving to registration-enabling studies (late 2027 or early 2028; novel and well-founded strategically, early clinically);
  • Recursion-originated immunology candidate into Phase 1 (2027); Nurix oral STAT6 degrader Phase 1 data;
  • Dupixent label expansions and reimbursement (Canada and others);
  • Competitor events: Rhapsido head-to-head against Dupixent (2027), zumilokibart Phase III progress, tozorakimab filing.

Months 18 to 36 (to late 2029):

  • frexalimab Phase III in multiple sclerosis (delayed to 2028; high relapse-rate hurdle; competes with six-monthly anti-CD20s);
  • rilzabrutinib in IgG4-related disease (2028);
  • lunsekimig Phase III in COPD (2029; Phase 2b asthma data showed no clear differentiation);
  • Novavax-partnered flu-COVID combination launch (2029);
  • Amvuttra royalty at the top tier;
  • negative steps: zumilokibart launch (2029), tariff exemption expiry (January 2029), Dupixent IRA selection around 2031.

Novelty and foundation: of roughly a dozen positive steps, two are both novel and well-founded within 36 months (the Regeneron long-acting successors and the Novavax combination), and both pay off mostly after 2029. The rest are incremental or weakly founded. The positive narrative staircase for the next 18 months therefore rests on financial delivery and capital allocation, not on science.

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

F4734th of 42

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

F4734th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry structure is supportive while patents last and fragile when they end; for Sanofi that boundary is March 2031 for the product that drives the whole case, with competitive and channel disruption converging on it from 2029. Sanofi is the category leader in type-2 immunology and one of four vaccine oligopolists, a strong commercial operator but not among the most advantaged innovators, which is why its historic share gains have come from bought or partnered molecules. Precedents say the market discounts a cliff about five years out (now) and rewards a cliff-facing company only when the replacement becomes visible; Sanofi has not yet supplied that visibility. For the model: protect the installed base through 2028, erode new-patient share steadily from 2027, escalate rebates modestly, and treat Regeneron's rising profit share and Sobi's royalty step-ups as structural costs.

3a

Value chain dynamics

6 answers
C-3262C-3342C-3440C-3748C-3840C-3942
C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?C6213th of 42full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

F4239th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: intermediaries are a moderate and rising hindrance. The three largest US wholesalers took 42% of consolidated sales in H1 2026 (22%, 14%, 6%), up from 35% in H1 2025 and 34% in FY2024; this is a credit and negotiating exposure rather than a pricing one (wholesale margins are 1% to 2%). The real hindrance is the three GPOs (Ascent, Zinc, Emisar) covering about 85% of US claims, which extract rebates and enforce exclusions, and whose integrated owners now hold private-label biosimilar vehicles that will matter at 2031. Helping: Dupixent's dominance lets Sanofi pay standard rebates today; TrumpRx and direct-to-consumer channels are emerging bypasses (Novartis claims direct cash-pay recaptures up to 50% of revenue leakage). The volatility of GTN accruals (both the 2026 windfalls and the risk of reversal) is the symptom of this intermediary structure.

C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

F4037th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: existing competitors are behaving rationally but aggressively in innovation and access, which hurts Sanofi's new-patient share in immunology and its vaccines growth; the position is robust on the installed base and fragile on the flow. Lilly (Ebglyss in atopic dermatitis and moving into nasal polyps), Galderma (Nemluvio), AbbVie (Rinvoq, Skyrizi at over USD 31bn combined, and zumilokibart), AstraZeneca (Tezspire, tozorakimab), GSK (Nucala, depemokimab), Novartis (Rhapsido), Merck (Enflonsia), Pfizer and GSK in vaccines. AstraZeneca is simultaneously a partner (Beyfortus) and an arbitration counterparty. The position against them weakens each year from 2027: by 2029 Dupixent will face at least one better-dosed IL-13 antibody and possibly an oral agent.

C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?D4827th of 42full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

F4037th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: disruption is acting on demand (convenience-driven switching), on competition (oral and long-acting entrants), on intermediaries (PBM vertical integration into biosimilars; direct-to-consumer channels) and on regulators (price-setting moving to government), and every one of these converges on the same date and product: Dupixent from about 2029 to 2033. Suppliers are being disrupted by localisation (US and EU onshoring), which raises Sanofi's capex but reduces supply risk. Discovery is being disrupted by AI, which currently helps Sanofi's efficiency claims but also lowers barriers for its competitors. The combined effect leaves Sanofi robust to 2028 and fragile from 2029, which is exactly the boundary of the second set's forecast window.
