3b

Industry structure

10 answers
Judgement47C-2462C-2556C-2645C-2740C-2856C-2955C-3158C-3535C-3640
Group judgementGroup judgement: Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)F4734th of 42full answer under 3 Value chain dynamics and industry structure
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

D6225th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Sanofi is a top-ten global pharmaceutical company by revenue but not a top-two; it is number one in its key product category (type-2 inflammatory biologics) and one of four oligopolists in vaccines. The ranking that matters is at product level, where it leads today and is being approached from behind.

  • Group: FY2025 revenue about EUR 45.5bn (brokers' basis), against AstraZeneca USD 58.7bn, Novartis USD 54.5bn, Roche CHF 61.5bn and GSK about GBP 32.7bn. It ranks around eighth to tenth globally, behind Lilly, Johnson & Johnson, Roche, Merck, AstraZeneca, Pfizer, AbbVie and Novartis.
  • Type-2 immunology: Dupixent is the category leader (50% to 55% share among biologic-prescribing US dermatologists; the "800-pound gorilla" in experts' words). Competitors (Ebglyss, Nemluvio, Tezspire, Nucala) are individually far smaller.
  • Vaccines: one of four global players (Pfizer, Merck, GSK, Sanofi) in a roughly USD 50bn oligopolistic market; leader in flu and in pediatric combination vaccines and in infant RSV (Beyfortus, with AstraZeneca).
  • Rare disease and hemophilia: a top player (Genzyme legacy; ALTUVIIIO leads weekly factor therapy; Roche's Hemlibra leads overall).
  • Distance: at group level, Sanofi is about 20% to 30% smaller than the number-one European peers; at product level it is number one in its main category.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

D5631st of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30%, and how steep is the share distribution?

Judgement: the global pharmaceutical market is not concentrated (no company exceeds about 5% of global spend, so the top five hold well under 30%), but the relevant markets are therapeutic categories, which are highly concentrated and steep. In type-2 biologics one product holds a majority of share; in vaccines four companies hold most of the market and the top five dwarf numbers six to ten; in pneumococcal vaccines two products dominate (PCV20 took 98% of the US market against PCV15). Concentration at category level supports pricing while patents last; it reverses abruptly at loss of exclusivity because the replacement is a near-identical copy. Upstream (CDMOs, top 13 under 30%) and downstream (three wholesalers about 99% of US volume; three GPOs about 85% of US claims) concentration matters more for Sanofi's economics than horizontal concentration does: buyer concentration is the steep distribution here.

C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

F4536th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: product-level shares are unstable by design (patent cycles), and new entrants, typically biotech-originated molecules commercialised by large companies, take a large share of every growing category within five to eight years. In Sanofi's own categories: Dupixent entered in 2017 and took most of the type-2 market; since 2022 Adbry/Ebglyss/Nemluvio and JAK inhibitors have taken part of new-patient flow, and first-line naive share has fallen to about 30% (DB panel). In infant RSV, Beyfortus took the market from 2023; Enflonsia entered in 2025 (49% of surveyed physicians plan to choose Beyfortus against 17% for Enflonsia). In insulin, biosimilars (Semglee 13%, Basaglar 8%) cut Lantus's US share to about 38%. Company-level shares are more stable: Sanofi's revenue was essentially flat from 2004 to 2020 while Lilly, Novo Nordisk and AstraZeneca gained share. The base rate is that Sanofi has lost share at group level for two decades and gained it in 2024 to 2026 through one product.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

F4039th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: share accrues to the most innovative, not to the largest. Sanofi is large but has not been among the most advantaged on the dimension that decides share, R&D productivity.

  • The share gainers of the last decade are Lilly (GLP-1s), Novo Nordisk (until 2025), AstraZeneca (oncology; about 75% Phase III success against an industry 60% to 65%) and Regeneron. Redburn's finding that Sanofi spent about the same on R&D as AstraZeneca over ten years with far poorer output is the crux; Novartis was ranked last of 12 in Redburn's development benchmarking and Roche first.
  • Sanofi is advantaged commercially (Dupixent execution, vaccines scale, rare disease access), which is why it can take an in-licensed or acquired asset and grow it (Dupixent itself is a Regeneron molecule; ALTUVIIIO came from Bioverativ; Ayvakit from Blueprint). The model that works for Sanofi is "buy science, sell well", not "discover and sell".
  • For the model: Sanofi gains share where it acquires a leading asset and loses share where it relies on internal discovery. Do not assume share gains beyond the current launch portfolio without naming an acquired asset.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

D5621st of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: large pharma competes on clinical differentiation and access, not on list price, while patents last; the attitude is margin-first. The exceptions are rebate wars in crowded classes and price collapse at patent expiry, both of which apply to Sanofi.

  • Branded competitors buy formulary access with rebates in crowded classes (dermatology biologics now; insulin historically, where Novo Nordisk's insulin sales fell 17% in H1 2026 on price). Sanofi itself uses "rebate wall" strategies to protect Dupixent. This is margin-first behaviour that nevertheless erodes net price.
  • Biosimilar and generic entrants are volume-first: Biocon prices biosimilar ustekinumab 40% below the originator; PBM private labels capture USD 400 to 550 per prescription against USD 65 to 75 for branded Humira.
  • Vaccines: flu is price-competitive (Sanofi cited competitive pricing and lower immunisation rates in US flu in 2025; speed to market drives flu margins).
  • Prices do not fall when volume falls in branded markets; they fall when exclusivity ends.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

C-5518th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the branded attitude is stable; what has changed is that the US government and PBMs, not competitors, have become the price-setters, and that Chinese biotechs and integrated biosimilar players are willing to price aggressively. Within branded immunology, a shark attack on price is unlikely before 2031 (competitors want to preserve the class price for their own successors: AbbVie for zumilokibart, Lilly for Ebglyss), but rebate escalation to buy first-line position is already under way. At the 2031 loss of exclusivity, behaviour turns predatory by design (biosimilars, PBM private labels). The change in the last 18 months is the US pricing agreements (a policy-set floor for Medicaid) and Medicare Part D redesign, which make price outcomes more predictable but lower.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

C-5821st of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the structure is highly supportive for patented products (75% to 80% gross margins, high regulatory barriers) and that support is fragile by design: it lasts exactly as long as the patent and is shrinking at the edges through buyer concentration and policy. For Sanofi the support is concentrated in one product with a known end-date (March 2031), which makes the structure supportive for the forecast horizon of the second set (to four years) and fragile immediately after it. The vaccines oligopoly is the more durable structural support (capital, regulatory and manufacturing barriers; Bernstein applies a 20% valuation premium to vaccines for this reason) but is under demand-side pressure.

C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?F3540th of 42full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

F4040th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: substitutes are a growing threat, mainly in form of administration and prevention modality rather than therapeutic mechanism. In immunology: oral JAK and BTK inhibitors and future oral STAT6 degraders substitute for injectable biologics; in RSV: maternal vaccines (shorter protection, per Jefferies, but avoid infant injection) substitute for Beyfortus; in flu: mRNA and combination vaccines substitute for standalone flu shots; in hemophilia: non-factor therapies (Hemlibra, Mim8, Sanofi's own Qfitlia) substitute for factor replacement. The trend in each case is toward convenience. Sanofi is hedged in hemophilia (owns both a factor and a non-factor product) and partly in immunology (Nurix oral STAT6; long-acting Regeneron antibodies) and least in RSV and flu.

Memo topic 3, : the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

D5230th of 428 answers
Judgement52C-4152C-4258C-4360C-4458C-4534C-4640C-4750
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

D5230th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Sanofi's competitive advantage is strong within the second set's forecast window and finite beyond it. It leads its key category on breadth, safety and familiarity, holds durable scale in vaccines and commercial infrastructure, and benefits mechanically from an accumulating chronic patient base. It lacks pricing power beyond defending volume, lacks the R&D moat that would renew its franchises, and shares much of the value its scale creates with partners. For the model: assume volume-led growth with flat-to-negative price, margins rising mechanically on a flat cost base but capped by Regeneron's share and royalty step-ups, and no compounding advantage beyond 2029 without an acquired asset.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

F5235th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Sanofi's moats are real but mostly of the "time-limited" kind. The strongest (Dupixent's regulatory exclusivity plus physician familiarity) has a known expiry and is narrowing at the edges; the most durable (vaccines manufacturing and regulatory scale) is attached to the weakest-growing segment; the moat it lacks is the one that renews the others, a productive R&D culture.

  • Regulatory/political (patents and data exclusivity): strong, finite, narrowing. Dupixent composition-of-matter in the US to March 2031; method-of-treatment and formulation patents (portfolio to 2045) might extend indication exclusivity into the mid-2030s if they survive challenge (Morgan Stanley) and could moderate the slope via settlements (Redburn). Dupixent is insulated from Medicare negotiation until about 2031.
  • Customer-related (clinical familiarity, safety record, switching inertia): strong on the installed base, narrowing on new patients. About 80% of eligible patients maintained on Dupixent in surveyed practices; pristine long-term safety; paediatric labels from six months in atopic dermatitis; Beyfortus real-world effectiveness 87% to 98% across 45 countries and 71-day half-life against Enflonsia's 42 to 44 days; more than 90% of physicians with a preference still choose Beyfortus (Sobi survey). The erosion is in first-line new starts (about 80% to 30%).
  • Scale-based (commercial infrastructure, payer relationships, vaccines manufacturing): strong and stable. A global specialty sales force and payer contracts that the Regeneron alliance is explicitly designed to re-use for the next-generation antibodies; a vaccine production base (Swiftwater, Toronto, Modulus plants in France and Singapore) that new entrants cannot quickly replicate.
  • Production-related: low Asian API dependence and in-house manufacturing of about 90% of core products: moderate, stable.
  • Culture/knowledge (R&D): weak. Redburn's spend-versus-output comparison with AstraZeneca; an expert says no internally discovered molecule had moved into development for over a decade. This is the moat whose absence makes the others finite.

Net: moats are strong for the 2026 to 2029 forecast window and weak beyond it. The Wisdom Library's "Legacy Moat versus Execution Moat" entry fits: Dupixent is a legacy moat being consumed, not rebuilt.

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

D5822nd of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Sanofi has large-cap scale (about EUR 50bn of 2026 sales, about 75,000 employees, EUR 101.6bn market capitalisation, EUR 114bn enterprise value) comparable with GSK and somewhat below AstraZeneca, Novartis and Roche. Scale confers commercial reach, manufacturing and regulatory capability, a strong balance sheet for acquisitions and negotiating weight with governments; it does not confer R&D productivity. Scale is what lets Sanofi turn a partner's molecule into a EUR 25bn product and what gives it EUR 15bn to 30bn of acquisition capacity at an AA rating. The scale disadvantage is partner economics: Sanofi's largest products are shared (Regeneron, AstraZeneca, Sobi royalties, Alnylam royalties in the other direction), so its share of the value its scale creates is lower than a fully owned franchise would deliver, which is part of why its operating margin (about 27% to 28%) trails Novartis (about 40%) and AstraZeneca (about 34%).

C-43How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?D6023rd of 42full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

D5830th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: demonstrably not inferior, and leading on breadth and safety, in its key products today; inferior on dosing convenience, which is the dimension competitors are attacking.

  • Dupixent: leading on breadth (nine indications across skin, airway and gut; the only biologic with this footprint), safety record, paediatric use and clinician familiarity; not inferior on efficacy (KT-621 matched but did not exceed it in Phase 1b; tozorakimab's 43% exacerbation reduction in a high-eosinophil subgroup is not a head-to-head comparison); inferior on convenience against Ebglyss (every eight weeks), Nemluvio, and future zumilokibart (every three to six months) and oral agents.
  • Beyfortus: leading on efficacy (74.5% versus 60.4% on medically attended infections), half-life and second-season use; Enflonsia leads on fixed dosing; physician preference strongly Sanofi.
  • ALTUVIIIO: leading among factor therapies (weekly dosing); weaker against Hemlibra's subcutaneous convenience.
  • Vaccines in general: legacy recombinant and egg/cell-based technology against mRNA; not inferior on efficacy in most categories, possibly inferior in pneumococcal (PCV21 "may not be sufficiently differentiated" from Pfizer's PCV20, per management).
  • Infilbray: first oral therapy for neurological type 3 Gaucher disease, superior to imiglucerase, but with psychiatric warnings.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

F3438th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility?

Judgement: little demonstrable pricing power at group level; latent but defensive pricing power in Dupixent and Beyfortus. Evidence of applied price increases is absent; evidence of price given back is abundant. See C-17. The 2026 GTN releases are reversals of over-accrued rebates, not price increases. Morgan Stanley's statement that Dupixent's dominance allows "low rebates and standard fees" is the best evidence of pricing power, and it is being eroded as competitors buy first-line position. The Wisdom Library entry "Pricing power breaks before demand does" is the right frame: watch Dupixent's US net price per patient in 2027, not its volume.

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

F4033rd of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto?

Judgement: hardly at all, by design of the industry. Branded pharma prices to value, not to cost. Sanofi's "sharing" is policy-driven (Medicaid alignment with other high-income nations; TrumpRx discounts; insulin list-price cuts of 78%; patient-assistance and bridge programmes) rather than a strategy of passing scale savings to customers to build volume. There is no Costco-type flywheel here. The one place it applies weakly is vaccines, where tender pricing to public buyers (PAHO, GAVI, UNICEF) and volume contracts (China insulin procurement) trade price for volume.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

D5026th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: mechanical forces improve Sanofi's financial outputs strongly until about 2029 and then reverse mechanically. The competitive advantage itself is not compounding; it is being drawn down.

  • Reinforcing (to about 2029): Dupixent's chronic patient base accumulates (each year's new patients stay on therapy), so revenue grows even as new-patient share falls; indication expansion adds patients without new R&D the operating cost base is held flat (R&D flat in 2027) while revenue grows, lifting margins mechanically; launches climb their S-curves; working capital and tax improve.
  • Balancing: Regeneron's profit share rises in step with Dupixent profit (other operating expenses +66% year on year in Q3 2026), capping operating leverage on the biggest product; Sobi royalty step-ups on Beyfortus; competitors' rebates rise as their share rises; currency drag grows as the US share grows.
  • Mechanical reversal (2031 onward): patent expiry and PBM-driven biosimilar switching.
  • Against peers: Sanofi's margin and EPS improve faster than GSK's in 2026 to 2027 and roughly in line with AstraZeneca's (consensus EPS growth 15%, 8%, 9% against AstraZeneca 12%, 11%, 10% and GSK 4%, 4%, 3%), but its margin level stays below all three. No gravitational force closes the R&D gap.

Company - Management

F5034th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The new management is decisive, candid and operationally disciplined, and its first six months improved Sanofi's near-term execution and governance. Its ambition is defensive, its culture is a turnaround culture, and the decisions that will determine the investment case (the size, price and quality of the post-2031 bridge) are still ahead and fall into Sanofi's weakest historical area, capital allocation. For the modelling Analyst: trust management's near-term guidance (it has been conservative and raised), discount its long-term product statements (the record on R&D and on mid-term targets is poor), and model capital allocation as acquisition-led rather than buyback-led.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

F5033rd of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: the ambition is pragmatic and moderate, not large. The stated goal is to protect earnings and cash through the post-Dupixent decade, not to out-grow peers.

  • Management expects "only a moderate revenue impact" when Dupixent loses exclusivity and aims to "preserve core earnings and cash flow" through the transition; 2030 targets (Dupixent about EUR 25bn, launches about EUR 10bn, vaccines about EUR 9bn) were set or reset, not stretched; the CEO speaks of "profitable and sustainable growth", accountability and rigorous execution.
  • Against peers: AstraZeneca targets USD 80bn of revenue by 2030 organically (36% above 2025); Novartis targets a 5% to 6% sales CAGR to 2030; Roche aims for 20 transformative new molecular entities by 2030. Sanofi's ambition is closer to "defend and buy" than "build and lead".
  • The one ambitious element is appetite for larger, later-stage deals; ambition expressed through acquisition is, on precedent, the riskiest form.
C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

F5238th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities: widening the moat, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy for a plausible range of environments?

Judgement: the priorities are right but defensive; the strategy narrows the business to where Sanofi is commercially strongest and fixes R&D governance, but does not yet widen any moat, and depends on M&A execution for growth after 2030.

  • Right: focus (immunology, rare, vaccines), science-gated development, exiting margin-dilutive legacy, partnering with Regeneron on long-acting successors (the most credible way to keep the Dupixent franchise beyond 2031), China and AI as cost-efficient sourcing.
  • Bold? Selectively: halting all Phase 2-to-3 transitions and killing three late-stage assets in months is bold; the pivot to big M&A is bold in risk, not in insight.
  • Dynamic across environments: the strategy works if acquired assets are good and fails if they are expensive and mediocre; it is not robust to a scenario where oral agents commoditise type-2 immunology before the long-acting successors launch (2029 to 2031).
  • Moat: neither widened nor rebuilt yet; the Regeneron next-generation programme is the only moat-extension mechanism.
C-57

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

D5825th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: early actions are those of a capable, decisive operator; the record that would make her "great" (R&D outcomes, M&A value) cannot yet be judged, and her prior record on both is mixed.

  • Actions at Sanofi in six months: discontinued amlitelimab and itepekimab; halted all Phase 2-to-3 transitions pending review; ordered a six-month research post-mortem; terminated mRNA flu despite competitive data; hired Fontoura; restructured the Executive Committee (eight members) and put M&A under the CFO; Cheplapharm, Medley and Enjaymo disposals; expanded and de-litigated the Regeneron alliance; raised guidance twice; acknowledged that Sanofi had over-promised and under-delivered. Speed and candour are real strengths (several brokers and an insider describe her as pragmatic and fast).
  • Inherited versus created: the 2026 commercial momentum (Dupixent, launches) and much of the cost discipline (CFO Roger, from 2024) predate her.
  • Prior record (Merck KGaA): turnaround of the healthcare unit from 2015 (Mavenclad, Bavencio, a EUR 1bn China business built from scratch); group sales +20% and leverage down to 1.4x as CEO, but adjusted EBITDA margin down 210 basis points, missed EUR 25bn 2025 revenue target, late-stage failures (evobrutinib, xevinapant). Integration experience (Genzyme) is relevant to a buy-the-bridge strategy.

C-58

How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

D5031st of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: financially well resourced; organisationally and scientifically under-invested relative to peers, after a decade of restructuring. Balance sheet capacity and cash flow are ample. Manufacturing investment is heavy and current (US, Frankfurt, Beijing, Toronto, Modulus). But R&D intensity (18%) is below GSK (20%) and AstraZeneca (23%) and is being held flat in 2027; R&D headcount is flat while group headcount fell 13%; the paediatric sales force was eliminated; experts report commercial attrition and "transactional" culture. The business has been run for margin repair since 2023 and is not built "yesterday for resilience" in the sense of spare capacity in science and talent.

C-62

To what extent does the company hold outlier standards, driving higher, faster and better, with a record of out-executing competitors?

F3839th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: not an outlier. Sanofi out-executes peers commercially on one product (and on Beyfortus's launch), and under-executes in development. Redburn's benchmarking places it well behind AstraZeneca on R&D output for the same spend; Roche has cut development cycle times by 11 months and executes terminate-or-advance decisions six times faster than Merck; AstraZeneca's Phase III success rate is about 75%. Sanofi's new standards (review boards, faster pruning) are a catch-up to good practice, not an outlier standard. The Q3 2026 cost performance (R&D and SG&A 6% to 7% below consensus) is a positive signal worth watching for persistence.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

C+5811th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: governance is sound and shareholder-oriented on pay and returns, with one structural wrinkle (double voting rights) and one behavioural weakness (a long habit of excluding recurring costs from the profit measure that drives pay).

  • For: independent chair separate from the CEO; 79% independent board (11 of 14), 57% women; the board removed an under-delivering CEO; CEO pay in the lowest quartile of the global peer group and realised pay second-lowest of twelve peers in 2022 to 2024; TSR weight in long-term incentives raised to 30%; clinical readout and approval conditions in LTI since 2024; clawback (October 2023); 50% of vested gains held; 31 years of dividend increases; Opella proceeds returned through a EUR 5bn buyback.
  • Against: double voting rights (L'Oréal 7.27% of capital, 13.10% of votes); annual variable pay is 60% on sales growth, free cash flow and business EPS, and business EPS excludes recurring restructuring; directors own 0.018% of capital; minor net insider selling (a 650-share option-linked sale at EUR 80 in October 2026) while AstraZeneca's CEO bought about GBP 7.3m of stock; critics fear clinical-milestone LTI rewards quantity over quality.
  • Prudence: AA rating maintained through the Opella transition; conservative leverage; centralised treasury with no speculative derivatives; litigation (Zantac) contained after Delaware dismissal and the Boehringer arbitration win.
C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

F4033rd of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, with a record of allocating capital at a high return?

Judgement: not enlightened. The record is of a steady dividend payer, an occasional repurchaser (when a disposal funds it) and a mixed acquirer, now about to commit its largest capital to the hardest-to-earn-a-return use.

  • Record: acquisitions with good outcomes (Genzyme, Bioverativ/ALTUVIIIO, Blueprint so far) and poor ones (Translate Bio about USD 3bn written off with mRNA flu; Provention/Tzield struggling; Kiadis impaired, EUR 877m R&D impairments in one year); repeated restructurings costing EUR 1.4bn to 2.2bn a year.
  • Buybacks: opportunistic rather than systematic (EUR 5bn in 2025 funded by Opella; EUR 1bn in 2026; minimal in 2019 to 2024). With the shares at 8x forward earnings and the 2nd percentile of their ten-year P/E range, a dynamic repurchaser would be buying aggressively now; Sanofi is preparing to buy assets instead.
  • What would change the judgement: a strategy update that sets explicit return hurdles for acquisitions (for example return on invested capital above the cost of capital by year three) and commits surplus cash to buybacks.

Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

D5030th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Sanofi's economics are improving quickly but from a mediocre base, and the improvement is time-limited. Mid-teens EPS growth in 2026 includes a few points of non-recurring rebate releases; from a cleaned base, 2027 consensus needs about 11% to 12% underlying growth while three tailwinds turn into headwinds, so the 2027 skew is negative. Operating margin (about 26% to 27%) trails every large European peer by 4 to 12 points, largely because Regeneron takes half of Dupixent's US profit; it can rise about 3 points by 2029 on a flat cost base. Cash conversion is good (about 80% to 85%) with working capital still improving. Returns on capital are about 10% and will fall if the bridge is bought at today's prices. The adjusted earnings definition is generous (recurring restructuring excluded; acquired R&D capitalised), so the Analyst should model business EPS for comparability and track IFRS and cash alongside it.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

C+6013th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Sanofi is generating mid-teens business EPS growth on low-teens revenue growth in 2026, the best combination in its peer group this year, but roughly a quarter to a third of the 2026 acceleration rests on items that do not recur, and durable growth after 2026 is high single digit to 2029, followed by a cliff. Durability is high for three years and low for six.

  • History (brokers' basis): business EPS EUR 8.26 (FY2022, +17.1% at constant exchange rates), 8.11 (FY2023, including Opella), 7.12 (FY2024, restated without Opella; +4.1% at constant rates, -1.8% reported against the restated base), 7.83 (FY2025; +15% at constant rates, +10% reported; 12.2% excluding the buyback). Revenue at constant rates: about +5% in 2023, about +11% in 2024 (restated), +9.9% in 2025.
  • Consensus (median): EPS EUR 9.00 (FY2026, +14.9%), 9.70 (FY2027, +7.8%), 10.58 (FY2028, +9.0%), 11.35 (FY2029, +7.3%, 12 brokers); revenue +11.8%, +5.6%, +6.2%, +1.4% (FY2029 rests on 10 brokers and should be treated as thin). The FY2027 broker range is wide: RBC 8.49 and Santander 8.98 to Jefferies 10.07 and Berenberg 10.07, a 17% spread, against a 7% spread for FY2026.
  • Quality of 2026: our estimate of non-recurring items in 2026 business operating income is about EUR 0.5bn (US GTN releases of about EUR 100m to 200m in Q2 and about EUR 300m in Q3 on Dupixent, Lantus and Toujeo, partly shared with Regeneron; a EUR 200m Sarclisa manufacturing provision reversal in Q2), worth roughly EUR 0.3 to 0.4 of EPS, or 3% to 4%. A cleaned 2026 base is therefore about EUR 8.6 to 8.7.
  • The 2027 arithmetic is the most important modelling point in this set. Against a cleaned base, consensus FY2027 EPS of EUR 9.70 requires about 11% to 12% underlying growth while absorbing: the end of Regeneron development-cost reimbursement (incremental EUR 500m of business operating income lost in 2027 versus 2026, about EUR 0.33 of EPS), the Cheplapharm/Medley revenue removal (about EUR 1.3bn of sales, a small net EPS loss after equity income), slightly negative pricing, and any currency move. Offsets: Dupixent and launch volume, R&D held flat, Amvuttra royalties (about EUR 500m more than 2025 per Deutsche Bank), SG&A discipline. The consensus is achievable but contains no slack; the downside skew in 2027 is larger than the upside skew.
  • A note on 2026's fourth quarter: on our arithmetic (the files do not give Q1 2026 EPS precisely; it grew double digit from EUR 1.79), nine-month business EPS is at least about EUR 7.6, so the FY2026 consensus of EUR 9.00 implies Q4 2026 EPS of about EUR 1.4, roughly 9% below Q4 2025, despite vaccine deliveries shifting into Q4. Either guidance is conservative (consistent with management's pattern) or Q4 carries a cost catch-up (R&D and SG&A ran 6% to 7% below consensus in Q3). A small Q4 beat is more likely than a miss.
  • Durability: volume growth from a chronic, accumulating patient base makes 2027 to 2029 growth reasonably dependable; 2030 is the last year before the cliff; Bernstein's 2026 to 2031 adjusted EPS CAGR of 5.9% (50 basis points below European peers) already blends in the start of erosion.
C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

F4232nd of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: little scope at high returns. Internal R&D has historically returned poorly, external late-stage assets are being bought at full prices in a crowded market, and the highest-return use of capital available today, buying back stock at an earnings yield above 12%, is not management's priority. This justifies a below-sector multiple until a high-return use of capital is demonstrated.

  • Organic: R&D (EUR 7.8bn, 18% of sales) produced few late-stage successes for a decade; the new governance may raise the hit rate, but output cannot show before 2028 to 2030.
  • External: illustrative return on the best recent deal: Blueprint cost USD 9.1bn; Ayvakit runs at about EUR 0.8bn annualised (Q3 2026 EUR 203m, +46%); even at a high contribution margin the year-two pre-tax return is around 5%, rising only if Ayvakit grows into a multi-billion product. Late-stage and commercial assets of the kind now targeted (Insmed at about USD 27bn was rumoured) are priced on peak sales; the acquirer earns its cost of capital only if the asset beats consensus.
  • Capex: rising to about 6% of sales (US localisation, insulin plants, flu plant) is largely defensive (tariff exemption, supply sovereignty), earning returns through avoided costs rather than growth.
  • Implication for the multiple: a company with a dominant franchise expiring in four years and low reinvestment returns is valued on cash flow to the cliff plus the value of what it buys; the market's 8x to 9x forward earnings reflects exactly that. The multiple rises only if (a) the post-2031 bridge becomes visible at a reasonable price, or (b) capital is returned. The Wisdom Library's "Name the Trigger Before You Buy the Turnaround" applies: the trigger here is the quality of the first large deal.
C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

F4231st of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: group returns are mediocre for large-cap pharma because of accumulated acquisition goodwill and intangibles; returns on tangible capital are very high, as for any branded pharma company; incremental returns on recent organic growth are high, and incremental returns on recent acquisitions are low.

  • Return on invested capital: about 10% in 2025 (Bernstein's calculation, 10.3%), against AstraZeneca about 14.5% (core return on capital employed 22%), Novartis about 15% (management cites 17%), Roche 16.2% (Bernstein) or about 22% (calculated in the Roche digest), Eli Lilly about 22%.
  • Why: shareholders' equity is about EUR 73bn (consensus FY2026) against business net income of about EUR 10.6bn, a return on equity of about 14% to 15%; the balance sheet carries the Aventis, Genzyme, Bioverativ, Ablynx, Provention, Blueprint and Dynavax premiums.
  • Return on tangible capital: very high (fixed assets are small relative to profits; capex 4% to 6% of sales), but this is generic to the industry.
  • Return on new invested capital: the 2024 to 2026 profit growth came almost entirely from Dupixent volume with little incremental capital (high, though half the US profit goes to Regeneron); the capital deployed in acquisitions since 2023 (about EUR 12bn to 15bn) is earning low single-digit returns so far.
  • For the model: expect group ROIC to drift up to about 11% to 12% by 2028 on operating leverage, then down if a large acquisition is added; any acquisition priced on peak sales will depress ROIC for three to four years.
C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

C-5517th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: conversion is good but not best-in-class (about 80% to 85% of business net income), held back by recurring restructuring cash, high working capital and rising capex; working capital is the one lever still improving.

  • Levels: company-defined free cash flow EUR 8.1bn in 2025 (18.5% of sales; about 84% of business net income of about EUR 9.6bn); nine months 2026 EUR 6,993m (EUR 5,452m a year earlier), EUR 7,771m before restructuring and acquisitions, against nine-month business net income of EUR 9,020m (78%; 86% before restructuring). Target: free cash flow at least 20% of sales. Peers: Novartis and Roche above 90% core-EPS-to-FCF; GSK about 80% (a persistent 20% gap); AstraZeneca over 90% in 2025.
  • FactSet's narrower definition (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex) gives FCF/reported net income of 0.9x for FY2025 and 0.7x to 0.8x in consensus years against adjusted net income; consensus FactSet FCF is EUR 7.6bn, 8.8bn, 10.4bn and 12.2bn for 2026 to 2029 (15% rising to 21% of sales; FCF yield about 7.5% in 2026 and 8.7% in 2027 on the EUR 101.6bn market capitalisation).
  • Working capital: net working capital was 27.7% of trailing sales at June 2026 (about 100 days, against about 75 to 95 days at AstraZeneca, Novartis and GSK on Bernstein's comparison); a 30-day inventory reduction released EUR 2.1bn in 2025 and a second is targeted for 2026; H1 2026 still showed a EUR 412m outflow (EUR 77m in H1 2025) on receivables from strong US sales. Wholesaler concentration (42% of sales) means receivables grow with US sales.
  • Drags: cash restructuring (EUR 0.8bn to 1.0bn a year; 10% of FY2025 FCF), capex rising to about EUR 3bn to 3.4bn, the EUR 1.6bn Sobi royalty liability unwinding, and Regeneron profit-share payments that rise in cash as Dupixent grows.
  • For the model: free cash flow of about 80% to 85% of business net income through 2028, with working capital a source of about EUR 0.5bn to 1bn in 2026 to 2027 then neutral, restructuring cash at about EUR 0.8bn a year, and capex at about 6% of sales.
C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

F5033rd of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: gross margin is high and rising; operating margin is the lowest among the large European peers and rising only slowly, because Sanofi shares its best product's profit and because vaccines and general medicines carry lower margins.

  • Gross margin (business basis): about 77.5% in FY2025 (Morgan Stanley's core figure), 81.0% in Q3 2026 (78.9% a year earlier, helped by GTN releases and mix); Morgan Stanley projects 79.4% by FY2028. Divesting Lovenox, Medley and three plants lifts it further.
  • Operating margin (business operating income/sales): about 25.5% to 25.6% in FY2024 and FY2025 on Redburn's figures (Morgan Stanley cites 27.8% for FY2025 on its own core basis; definitions differ). Consensus EBIT margin on the brokers' basis: 26.7% (FY2026), 27.3% (FY2027), 28.1% (FY2028), 29.2% (FY2029). Morgan Stanley's Kapila assumed flat 2026 and a 20 basis point decline in 2027 before Q3; management told Deutsche Bank margins should improve every year through 2030.
  • Peers (brokers' consensus EBIT margin, FY2026 to FY2028): AstraZeneca 33% to 36%, Novartis 39% to 40%, GSK 31%, Roche about 35.5% (2025 actual). Sanofi's gap is 4 to 12 points.
  • Why the gap: Regeneron's share of Dupixent profit sits in other operating expenses (profit-sharing expense of EUR 3,486m in H1 2026; other operating expenses EUR 2,168m in Q3 2026 against sales of EUR 14,444m), roughly 15% of group sales in 2026. Excluding it, Sanofi's operating margin would be above its peers'. The gap is therefore a partnership structure, not inefficiency, and it widens as Dupixent grows. Vaccines and general medicines are also lower-margin; R&D intensity (18%) is not the cause (it is lower than AstraZeneca's).
  • Trend: +0.5 to +1 point a year to 2029 from operating leverage on a flat R&D base, mix and divestment; the trend reverses from 2031 because Dupixent's margin after profit-share is still above the group average.
C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

F3533rd of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or other leakage erode EPS?

Judgement: the earnings base is not clean. The gap between business and IFRS earnings is among the widest in the sector, the excluded items include costs that recur every year, and the business EPS definition structurally favours a buy-rather-than-build R&D strategy, which is exactly the strategy Sanofi is now adopting.

  • Size of the gap: H1 2026 IFRS net income EUR 1,957m against business net income EUR 4,765m (41%); FY2025 reported EPS EUR 4.26 against business EPS EUR 7.83 (54%). Drivers: amortisation of acquired intangibles (about EUR 2bn a year), impairments (EUR 1,031m in H1 2026, of which EUR 952m amlitelimab), restructuring (EUR 563m in H1 2026; EUR 1,396m in FY2024).
  • Recurring "one-offs": restructuring has recurred at EUR 1.4bn to 2.2bn a year; on a cash basis it is about EUR 0.8bn to 1.0bn a year, worth about EUR 0.5 to 0.65 of EPS after tax (6% to 7%). Deutsche Bank calls add-backs of restructuring the largest recurring adjustment.
  • Flattering one-offs in 2026: GTN releases and the Sarclisa provision reversal (C-49), about 3% to 4% of 2026 EPS.
  • Capitalisation bias: acquired and in-licensed R&D is capitalised and its amortisation excluded from business EPS, while internal R&D is expensed. The USD 1bn Regeneron upfront, the Blueprint and Dynavax premiums and any future late-stage acquisition therefore hit IFRS earnings and cash, but not business EPS except through financing cost. The Analyst should model acquisition dilution on business EPS only through interest and pre-launch operating costs, and should separately track IFRS and cash to avoid being flattered.
  • Tax and leakage: business tax rate about 20% (21% before Opella; Deutsche Bank says stable); minorities small (EUR 360m); share of associates (Opella, and Cheplapharm from 2027) adds a modest positive. Tax is not an erosion risk; Pillar Two is in the rate.
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

C-6818th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: financial leverage is low and comfortable today (about 0.9x net debt to EBITDA, AA rating, about 85% fixed-rate debt) and amplifies nothing. It becomes material only through a large acquisition, which is the probable path.

  • Today: net debt EUR 12.2bn (September 2026; company definition; FactSet's lease-inclusive figure was EUR 17.2bn at June 2026); net interest expense about EUR 0.5bn a year; consensus total debt falls from EUR 15.7bn (2026) to EUR 10.5bn (2028).
  • With an acquisition: EUR 20bn to 25bn of debt-funded M&A would take net debt/EBITDA to roughly 1.8x to 2.2x and add about EUR 0.7bn to 0.9bn of annual interest at 3.5% (about EUR 0.45 to 0.6 of business EPS, 5% to 6%), offset only by the acquired asset's contribution. Management says leverage should stay around 1x after acquisitions, which implies deals of about EUR 10bn to 15bn unless funded partly by disposals (Opella stake, Cheplapharm stake) or by suspending buybacks.
  • Risk amplification: none at present; a modest one after a large deal, given strong cash generation (EUR 8bn to 12bn of free cash flow a year).

5a

Pricing power

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

F3421st of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: price was a positive contributor of about +1 point to revenue growth in 2026 only because of non-recurring US gross-to-net (GTN) accrual releases; from 2027 price is a steady headwind of about -1 to -1.5 points of revenue growth a year, with a one-off swing of about -2 points in 2027 as the releases fall out. Because price falls almost entirely to profit, it is the single largest reason margins rise only slowly: on our numbers price erosion costs about EUR 0.35bn to 0.45bn of BOI a year after the Regeneron share, roughly 0.7 to 0.9 point of margin a year that volume leverage must first refill.

2026: accounting, not pricing power. GTN releases (prior-period rebate accruals reversed): EUR 100m to 200m on Dupixent in Q2 2026 (Morgan Stanley, 30 July 2026; Deutsche Bank put the Q2 gross-margin benefit at EUR 200m) and about EUR 300m across Dupixent, Lantus and Toujeo in Q3 2026 (Jefferies, 30 October 2026; Morgan Stanley: about half of the 9% sales beat). Q3 2026 Toujeo rose 31.2% and Lantus 18.5%, products in structural decline, which shows the size of the true-ups. We assume about EUR 0.45bn to 0.5bn of releases in total in 2026 (with a possible further release in Q4 that we do not model), about 1 point of 2026 sales growth and, after the Regeneron share on Dupixent's portion, about EUR 0.33bn of BOI. The CFO said on the Q3 call that pricing returns to the historical "slightly negative" pattern in 2027. We treat any Q4 2026 release as upside to 2026 and as an additional 2027 base-effect headwind; a reversal (the mirror image) is possible but has no evidence behind it.

2027 to 2030: what we model by channel (net price contribution to group revenue growth, our estimates):

Channel Share of 2026 sales Assumed net price per year Evidence
Dupixent US commercial about 30% -2% Dupixent's share of biologic-naive starts fell from 80% to 90% to 30% to 40% (Deutsche Bank expert panel, September 2026); Ebglyss covered by all three major PBMs (nearly 95% of commercial plans); Nemluvio preferred first-line at four leading PBMs; experts report aggressive dermatology rebating. AbbVie guides only low-single-digit price headwinds for Skyrizi and Rinvoq, so a price war is not the base case
US government channels under 30% of US immunology Captured in 2026 base December 2025 agreement (Medicaid -61% on average for certain medicines; TrumpRx discounts) is in the 2026 base; Dupixent is not IRA-negotiable before about 2031; the November 2026 CMS rule grouping subcutaneous and intravenous forms matters after the window
Europe (about 15% to 21% of sales) -2% to -3% Germany's mandatory rebate on patented drugs rises from 7% to 15.5% in January 2027 (about -0.2 to -0.3 point of group revenue, once); patented-vaccine price freeze to 2030; joint clinical assessment
China about 6% -3% to -5% NRDL renegotiations; volume-based procurement (insulin glargine bid about 50% below limits in 2021; enoxaparin -63% average in round eight)
Vaccines (about 17%) -1% to -2% Mid-teens flu sales decline guided earlier on "intense pricing competition"; Enflonsia at a similar list price (USD 556) to Beyfortus; CDC contracts priced 30% to 70% below private
General medicines about 18% -4% to -6% (inside the volume decline) Insulin, Plavix, Lovenox price erosion

Net group effect. About -1.0 to -1.3 points a year of revenue growth from price from 2027, plus about -1 point in 2027 alone from the non-recurrence of 2026's releases: a swing of about -2 to -2.5 points between 2026 and 2027. This is embedded in our franchise growth rates (Dupixent +14% in 2027 is volume of about +17%, net price about -2% and a GTN base effect of about -1.5 points).

What it does to margin. About EUR 0.6bn of sales lost to price each year at a profit retention of about 60% to 70% (Dupixent's portion is shared with Regeneron) is about EUR 0.35bn to 0.45bn of BOI, roughly 0.7 to 0.9 point of margin a year. That is why gross margin, which rose to 81.0% in Q3 2026 (+2.1 points, helped by GTN), will not keep rising at that pace: we model about 80% to 81% by 2028, broadly Morgan Stanley's 79.4% plus divestment mix.

Skew and width. For 2027 the skew in price is modestly negative: rebating in dermatology could escalate further if Kymera's oral STAT6 degrader KT-621 reads out well around year-end 2026 (competitors would buy access ahead of the next wave); US midterm results (a more pricing-hostile Congress) and the January 2029 tariff-exemption expiry are tails for 2028 to 2030. The positive tail is further GTN releases (management is using AI tools to record provisions more accurately, per Redburn, which reduces the chance of large adjustments either way). A 1-point swing in group net price is about EUR 0.22 to 0.25 of EPS (about 2.5%), so price is a bigger single lever than Dupixent volume per point.

Peers. No large peer has shown sustained net price increases in immunology; AbbVie held price on Skyrizi and Rinvoq while competitors launched (low-single-digit headwinds); Amgen's Enbrel (CMS price set about 70% below list, net price -22% in Q2 2026) and Humira's private-label conversion (97% of patients switched within a season at CVS from April 2024) show the post-exclusivity shape that matters for terminal value but not for this window.

Where I differ from the first set. C-17 used "about -1% a year from 2027"; we agree on the run-rate but add that the 2026-to-2027 swing is about -2 to -2.5 points of growth, because the first set's -1% omitted the base effect from the 2026 releases, and we quantify that price, not costs, is what holds the margin back.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7555P-0158
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

C5512th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: operating leverage is material (about 1.3 to 1.5 times on business operating income and higher on EPS), dampened on Dupixent by the Regeneron profit share and amplified on wholly owned products. It creates a moderately wide range of 2027 to 2028 outcomes.

  • Observed: FY2025 sales +9.9% at constant rates, business operating income +11.9%, EPS +15% (with buyback); Q3 2026 sales +14.3%, business operating income +19.7%, EPS +22%.
  • Sensitivities (Rothschild & Co Redburn, calculated): ±2 points of volume growth moves next year's business EPS by about ±5.9%; ±0.5 point of operating margin moves EPS by about ±1.8% (about EUR 0.16); a 1-point change in revenue growth moves EPS by about 1.1%; a 5% adverse currency shift moves EPS by about 2.8%.
  • Structure: gross margin about 80%; R&D (18%) and SG&A (22%) are largely fixed in the short run; Regeneron's share takes about half of incremental US Dupixent profit and 35% to 45% ex-US, so a euro of extra Dupixent revenue contributes perhaps EUR 0.35 to 0.45 of operating profit, while a euro of extra ALTUVIIIO, Ayvakit, Lantus or vaccine revenue contributes EUR 0.6 to 0.7.
  • Range: a 3-point swing in 2027 revenue growth (for example 4% versus 7%) produces about a 9% to 10% swing in 2027 business EPS (roughly EUR 9.2 to 10.1 around a EUR 9.7 consensus), before currency. With a EUR/USD swing of 0.05 added, the range widens by a further ±2.6%.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

B+584th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: operating leverage is strong in gross terms but "half-shared" in net terms, and it is partly spent before it reaches the margin. It lifts BOI margin by about 0.5 point a year from 2027 to 2030 (29.3% in FY2026 to about 31.2% in FY2030 on net sales), roughly half the pace consensus implies, and it does almost nothing for the margin in 2027 itself, because 2027 absorbs about EUR 1.3bn of BOI headwinds that are not leverage questions at all. Against the last six years, which saw margin fall from about 30% (2022) to the mid-to-high 20s and then recover, the next four are a period of steady, slow expansion, front-loaded into 2026.

What the leverage is, line by line (our estimates of the contribution kept by Sanofi per incremental euro of net sales):

Source of growth Contribution per EUR 1 of sales Why
Dupixent about EUR 0.45 Gross margin about 88% (Regeneron manufactures); Regeneron takes 50% of US profit and 35% to 45% outside the US; US is 77% of Dupixent (Regeneron Q3 2026: USD 5,064m of USD 6,540m). Cross-check: Q3 2026 Dupixent sales rose about EUR 1.47bn and other operating expenses (mainly Regeneron's share) rose EUR 865m
Launch medicines (ALTUVIIIO, Ayvakit, Rezurock, Sarclisa, Qfitlia, Tzield) about EUR 0.80 High gross margin, small specialist sales forces already in place; some partner shares
General medicines (lost sales) about EUR 0.55 lost Mature products have low promotion cost, so lost sales are lost profit
Vaccines about EUR 0.50 Lower gross margin, Sobi royalty on US Beyfortus stepping up to 30% to 35% by 2028

Why this matters for the model. Dupixent supplies about 60% of revenue growth to 2028 but keeps less than half of each euro, while general medicines (falling 6% to 7% a year plus EUR 1.3bn to 1.6bn of disposals) remove high-contribution sales. The mix of growth, not the fixed-cost base, decides the margin path. A euro of Dupixent growth lifts the margin only slightly (0.45 against a group BOI margin of about 0.30); a euro of launch growth lifts it strongly; a euro of general-medicines decline cuts it.

The fixed-cost base is genuinely being held. R&D was EUR 7.8bn in FY2025 (18% of sales) and is roughly flat in 2026 (Q3 2026 +0.8%); management plans it flat in 2027 and then growing with sales (Deutsche Bank, 18 November 2026). SG&A was EUR 9.5bn (21.9%) and fell 0.9% at constant rates in Q3 2026 on sales up 14%. We hold SG&A about flat in absolute terms to 2030 (the Cheplapharm transfer and the shrinking general-medicines business free commercial resource that launches absorb) and grow R&D +1% in 2027, then +4%, +3%, +2%. Combined R&D and SG&A fall from about 39.7% of net sales in FY2025 to 35.8% in FY2026 and about 31.7% in FY2030: roughly 8 points of fixed-cost leverage in five years.

Where the 8 points go (this is the central insight for the Consumer). Only about 3.5 points reach BOI margin between FY2025 and FY2030. The rest is absorbed by four items that scale with success or are contractual: (a) Regeneron's profit share, which grows with Dupixent (Regeneron's share of collaboration profits rose to USD 2,575m in Q3 2026 from USD 1,456m, partly because the development balance was repaid in Q2 2026); (b) the end of Regeneron development-cost reimbursement (company: about EUR 300m of BOI lost in 2026, about EUR 800m in 2027); (c) net price erosion of about 1% to 1.5% a year from 2027, which falls straight to profit (see P-04); (d) Sobi's rising Beyfortus royalty and the loss of high-contribution general medicines. Partly offsetting: Amvuttra royalties (30% on sales above USD 1.5bn, already reached), gross-margin mix from divesting low-margin plants and products.

The 2026-to-2027 bridge in BOI (EUR bn, our estimates). Start FY2026 BOI 14.24 (29.3%). Headwinds that are not leverage: Regeneron reimbursement -0.5; non-recurrence of the Q2 2026 Sarclisa manufacturing provision reversal -0.2; divested Cheplapharm and Medley products -0.25; general-medicines erosion including the Lantus and Toujeo gross-to-net (GTN) base effect -0.37; Sobi royalty step -0.03; higher depreciation from new plants -0.15; R&D +1% -0.08; acquisition integration -0.05. Total about -1.6, of which about -1.3 is the "three tailwinds reversing" the first set identified. Leverage that offsets: Dupixent (+14% sales, net of the GTN base effect) +1.31; launches (+24%) +1.00; Amvuttra royalty +0.28; other -0.07. Result: BOI EUR 15.13bn (+6.2%), margin 29.6% (+0.3 point). Margin is flat to slightly up in 2027 not because leverage fails but because about 2.7 points of margin headwind (one-offs, reimbursement, disposals) is offset by about 3 points of genuine leverage. Management's statement that margin improves "each and every year through 2030" (Deutsche Bank, 5 November 2026) is therefore consistent with our numbers, but only just in 2027.

The half-year shape (important for predictability). Regeneron reimbursement was still received in H1 2026 and stopped from Q3 2026; the Sarclisa reversal and the first GTN release were in Q2 2026; Cheplapharm transfers start in Q1 2027. So almost all the 2027 headwinds fall in H1 2027. Our H1 2027 BOI is about EUR 6.48bn against about 6.18bn in H1 2026 (+5%), EBITDA margin about 31.0% against 31.1%; business EPS about EUR 4.11 against 3.96 (+3% to +4%). H2 2027 is stronger (EBITDA margin about 34.3%; EPS +8%). The July 2027 print is the visible low point of the forecast window, and a first-half EPS growth rate near zero is plausible if Dupixent decelerates faster than we assume.

2028 to 2030. With the one-offs gone, the leverage shows: BOI +8.6% in 2028 (margin 30.4%), +5.5% in 2029 (30.9%), +2.9% in 2030 (31.2%). Leverage fades in 2029 to 2030 because Dupixent growth slows to about 3% then 0% (competition from long-acting and possibly oral agents from 2029; the 2030 target of about EUR 25bn), R&D resumes growth with sales, and Amvuttra royalties plateau as Alnylam's royalty-free nucresiran approaches (expected launch around 2030).

Against the last four to six years. On FactSet's adjusted EBIT the margin rose from 16.8% (FY2019) to 26.1% (FY2025), but that history mixes Opella, restatements and acquired-amortisation definitions. On the company's own basis, BOI margin reached the 2022 profitability milestone of about 30% (with consumer health), was deliberately reset by the October 2023 reinvestment decision (32% 2025 target withdrawn), and recovered to about 27.7% (FY2025, Morgan Stanley's core basis). The next four years are therefore a recovery to and modestly beyond the 2022 peak, but on a narrower, more concentrated business. The cost programmes of the past (Play to Win delivered EUR 2.7bn of efficiencies, fully reinvested) show that Sanofi's savings historically did not reach the margin; this time the reinvestment is going into acquisitions (capitalised, below BOI) rather than into the R&D line, which is why the margin can rise.

Range of outcomes from leverage alone (2027). A 3-point swing in 2027 Dupixent growth (11% to 17%) moves BOI by about EUR 0.28bn and EPS by about EUR 0.19 (2%); a 5-point swing in launch growth moves EPS by about EUR 0.16; a 1-point swing in group net price moves EPS by about EUR 0.22 to 0.25 (price is the most powerful single operating lever because it is almost pure profit). Combined plausible operating range for 2027 EPS: about EUR 9.2 to 10.1 around our 9.61, before currency (plus or minus EUR 0.23 per 0.05 USD/EUR) and capital allocation.

Where I differ from the first set. C-75 put operating leverage at 1.3 to 1.5 times on BOI; that is right for 2025 to 2026 but overstates 2027 (about 1.2 times on our numbers) and understates 2028 (about 1.4 to 1.5 times). C-78's "+2.5 to 3.5 points of margin between 2025 and 2029" is consistent with our +3.2 points, but about half of it arrives in 2026 and part of 2026's gain is non-recurring, so the forward run-rate is about 0.5 point a year.

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7668P-0255
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?C-6818th of 42full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

D5515th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: financial leverage is low and will stay comfortable; on its own it moves EPS by less than 2% in any year. What makes the balance sheet the second-largest EPS variable in the window is not gearing but the choice of what to do with it. We assume a EUR 10bn debt-funded acquisition in mid-2027, which lifts net debt to about EUR 19.4bn (1.2 times EBITDA) at end-2027 and costs about 1% to 2% of EPS in 2027 and 2028, turning slightly accretive from 2029. The alternative use, a buyback of the same size at today's price, would add about 8% to 2028 EPS. The difference between the two, about 10% of EPS, is larger than any operating uncertainty in the next two years.

Where the balance sheet stands. Company-defined net debt EUR 11.0bn at end-2025, EUR 15.5bn at June 2026 (after the dividend and Dynavax), EUR 12.2bn at September 2026; FactSet's lease-inclusive net debt EUR 17.2bn at June 2026. About 85% fixed rate after swaps; EUR 2.3bn of bonds issued in April 2026 at 2029, 2033 and 2037 maturities; ratings Aa3 (Moody's), AA (S&P, Scope), stable; no rating triggers in financing documents. Net interest about EUR 0.5bn a year. Net debt to EBITDA about 0.8 to 0.9 times.

Our path (company basis, excluding leases). End-2026 about EUR 11.7bn (FCF about EUR 7.45bn on our definition, dividend EUR 4.96bn, EUR 1bn buyback completed April 2026, about EUR 2.15bn of acquisitions, mainly Dynavax). End-2027 about EUR 19.4bn (1.16 times our EBITDA) after the assumed EUR 10bn deal plus about EUR 1.5bn of bolt-ons, less Medley and initial Cheplapharm proceeds. Then deleveraging of about EUR 1.4bn to 2.6bn a year: about EUR 18.1bn (1.0 times) end-2028, 15.4bn (0.8 times) end-2029, 12.9bn (0.65 times) end-2030. Net financial items: EUR -480m (2026), -600m (2027), -620m (2028), -500m (2029), -420m (2030), at about 3.3% on average net debt plus about EUR 0.1bn of other financial costs (lease interest, Sobi royalty liability unwinding), less Cheplapharm preferred dividends from 2028 (Deutsche Bank: up to EUR 800m net present value, about EUR 202m a year over 2028 to 2032, contingent on Cheplapharm's cash and board approval; we include half).

Why we put the acquisition in the base case. The evidence that a large deal is the probable path: the CEO's statement that the group will fund larger, later-stage and commercial business development; the CFO's remit extended to business development (1 September 2026); the R&D head spending about half his time on external sourcing; Jefferies' "Drumroll for a deal" (2 November 2026) and reports of Sanofi weighing Insmed (about USD 27bn); management's own concession that existing assets cannot cover the post-2031 gap; and the December 2026 strategy update. Size: management's guide that leverage should stay around 1 times after acquisitions, against EBITDA of about EUR 16.7bn and FCF after dividends of about EUR 3bn a year, caps a single debt-funded deal at roughly EUR 10bn to 15bn without disposals of the Opella or Cheplapharm stakes. We take EUR 10bn as the central case and model it on the Blueprint precedent (USD 9.1bn for Ayvakit, which runs at about EUR 0.8bn annualised in year two): acquired sales of about EUR 0.3bn in 2027, 1.0bn in 2028, 1.4bn in 2029 and 1.8bn in 2030, BOI contribution of about EUR -0.05bn, +0.15bn, +0.40bn and +0.65bn. The probability we attach to a deal of at least EUR 8bn by end-2027 is about two in three; the Consumer should treat the timing and the asset's contribution as the least predictable inputs in the model.

How it shapes EPS (business basis). Organic EPS: about EUR 9.74 (2027), 10.55 (2028), 11.06 (2029), 11.30 (2030). With the deal: 9.61, 10.45, 11.13, 11.52. Dilution about 1.3% in 2027 and 1% in 2028, accretion about 0.6% in 2029 and 2% in 2030. The dilution is small on business EPS because Sanofi excludes amortisation of acquired intangibles (and capitalised upfront payments) from business EPS; the only charges that reach it are interest and the asset's running costs. On IFRS EPS and on cash returns the same deal looks much worse: a EUR 10bn purchase adds perhaps EUR 0.5bn to 0.7bn a year of amortisation and earns a pre-tax return of about 4% to 6% in its first years (Blueprint precedent), below Sanofi's cost of capital. The Consumer should not let the business-EPS accretion from 2029 be read as value creation.

The buyback counterfactual (the number the market will compare with). EUR 10bn at EUR 84 retires about 119m shares (about 10% of 1,200m); after-tax interest of about EUR 0.26bn costs about 2.3% of earnings; net accretion about +8% to +9% from the first full year, at an earnings yield above 12%. A large buyback is not our base case (management prefers assets; no programme announced since the EUR 1bn completed in April 2026), but it is the upside scenario that a disciplined strategy update could produce.

A larger deal. EUR 20bn to 25bn (an Insmed-scale asset) would take net debt to about EUR 30bn to 35bn (about 1.8 to 2.1 times), add about EUR 0.65bn to 0.8bn of interest, and dilute business EPS by about 4% to 6% in 2027 to 2028 unless the asset is already profitable. It would probably require partial sale of the Opella (48.2%) or Cheplapharm (26.4%) stakes or an equity component to protect the AA rating; at 8.2 times forward earnings, equity is the most expensive currency Sanofi has.

Shares and dividend. Share count held at about 1,200m to 1,203m: employee-plan issuance of about 4m shares a year offset by routine buybacks of about EUR 0.3bn a year (the pattern of 2021 to 2024). Dividend per share EUR 4.33 (for 2026, +5.1%), 4.55, 4.78, 5.02, 5.22: a payout of about 45% to 48% of business EPS, consistent with 31 consecutive increases and leaving room for both a deal and deleveraging. Consensus DPS (4.24, 4.46, 4.66) is about 2% to 3% below ours, because it implies increases of about 3% against a recent pattern of 4% to 7%.

Risk amplification. At 1.2 times peak leverage, with FCF of EUR 8.5bn to 11.6bn a year, financial leverage amplifies nothing material. The balance-sheet risk is not solvency but the rating constraint: a deal above about EUR 15bn without disposals would probably cost the AA rating, which management has named as a constraint.

Cost-out scope NTM
