C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

C-5518th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: there is moderate scope, perhaps 1.5 to 3 points of sales over three years, mostly by holding costs flat while sales grow rather than by absolute cuts; the largest single cost line attached to growth, Regeneron's profit share, is not cuttable at all.

  • FY2025 cost base (reported): cost of sales EUR 13.0bn (29.9% of sales), R&D EUR 7.8bn (18%), SG&A EUR 9.5bn (21.9%), other operating expenses (mainly Regeneron profit share) rising sharply in 2026.
  • R&D: to be flat in 2027 then grow with sales (Fontoura, via Deutsche Bank); the shift from late-stage to more early-stage projects is cheaper per project. Reducible as a share of sales by about 1 to 1.5 points by 2028. Cutting further would deepen the post-2031 problem.
  • SG&A: already cut (headcount -13% since 2023; Q3 2026 -0.9%); the Cheplapharm transfer frees general-medicines commercial resources; AI-enabled processes. About 1 to 1.5 points of sales reducible by 2028 by holding flat. Launch support for any acquired commercial asset would absorb some of it.
  • Cost of sales: transferring three plants and divesting low-margin mature products lifts gross margin; manufacturing efficiency (Modulus plants, AI yield optimisation); offset by new US plants. About 1 point of gross margin over three years.
  • Not cuttable: Regeneron's profit share (rises with Dupixent), Sobi's Beyfortus royalty step-ups, Medicaid and rebate concessions, restructuring cash itself.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

C-5410th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: about 3 to 4 points of sales of efficiency is available over 2026 to 2030, almost entirely by holding absolute costs flat while sales grow rather than by cutting; about 1 point of it has already reached the margin in 2026, and only about 2 further points will reach BOI margin by 2030, because the rest is absorbed by Regeneron's rising share, price erosion and the loss of reimbursement. The timing is front-loaded: 2027 (R&D flat) is the year of largest cost leverage but also of largest non-cost headwinds; 2028 is the year in which efficiency most clearly reaches the margin. Restructuring is a recurring cost of about EUR 0.8bn to 1.0bn of cash a year and should be carried in cash flow and in any "economic" EPS; it is excluded from business EPS.

Line by line, what is cuttable and what reaches the P&L (our estimates):

Line FY2025 What we model Reaches BOI margin by 2030 Timing and confidence
R&D EUR 7.8bn, 18.0% of net sales Flat 2026 and 2027 (about EUR 7.8bn to 7.9bn), then +4%, +3%, +2% R&D falls to about 15.0% of sales; about 1 point net of what it funds 2027 high confidence (management stated); 2028 onward medium: the expanded Regeneron alliance co-funds four long-acting antibodies 50:50 with registration-enabling studies from late 2027, and Deutsche Bank (18 November 2026) reports R&D may grow with business development
SG&A EUR 9.5bn, 21.9% About flat in absolute terms to 2030 SG&A falls to about 16.8%; about 2 points net Medium-high: headcount already down 13% since 2023; Cheplapharm takes 548 employees and three plants; launches need little extra promotion; an acquired commercial asset brings its own SG&A (inside our deal contribution)
Cost of sales Business gross margin about 77.5% (FY2025, Morgan Stanley) Rising to about 80% to 81% by 2028 on mix and divestment of low-margin products and plants About 1 point (largely mix, not efficiency) Medium: Morgan Stanley projects 79.4% by 2028; new US plants (USD 20bn of US investment through 2030) add depreciation (our D&A rises from EUR 1.45bn to 2.0bn)
Regeneron profit share, Sobi royalty, rebates Not cuttable Rise with sales Negative Contractual

How much reaches the lines, and when. Combined R&D and SG&A fall about 4 points of net sales in FY2026 alone (from 39.7% to 35.8%), because sales jump about 11.5% on a flat base: this is the cost efficiency that has already happened and that the 2026 margin (29.3%, from about 27.7%) already shows. From FY2026 to FY2030 they fall a further 4 points. Of those, we estimate about 2 points reach BOI margin (29.3% to 31.2%) and about 2 points are absorbed by Regeneron's profit share rising faster than sales in 2027 to 2028, the EUR 0.5bn reimbursement loss in 2027, price erosion of about 1% to 1.5% a year and Sobi's step-ups. In money: holding R&D and SG&A flat in 2027 is worth about EUR 0.9bn of BOI against cost growing with sales; it is the reason 2027 BOI rises at all.

Restructuring: recurring in practice. Restructuring charges were EUR 1,062m (2019), 1,064m (2020), 820m (2021), 1,336m (2022), 1,030m (2023), 1,396m (2024), 1,138m (2025) and 563m (H1 2026): eight consecutive years at about EUR 0.8bn to 1.4bn. Deutsche Bank (27 April 2026) put Sanofi's cash restructuring at about 10% of FY2025 FCF against 1% to 4% for large European peers. A new CEO, an R&D review (a six-month research post-mortem, Phase 2 to 3 transitions halted), the mature-medicines transfer and a strategy update in December make 2027 to 2028 more, not less, likely to carry charges. We model cash restructuring of EUR 0.9bn (2026), 0.8bn (2027 and 2028) and 0.7bn (2029 and 2030) in FCF. If restructuring is treated as an operating cost, "economic" EPS is about EUR 0.50 to 0.60 below business EPS each year (about 5% to 6%). This is a quality-of-earnings point for the fourth set: the market has long accepted the exclusion, so it is not a consensus risk, but it means business EPS overstates distributable earnings by about 6%.

Precedent check. Play to Win (2019 to 2022) promised EUR 2bn, then 2.5bn, and delivered EUR 2.7bn, fully reinvested; the 2024 to 2025 programme aimed at up to EUR 2bn to fund R&D. So Sanofi has a record of delivering savings and of not letting them reach the margin. The difference now is that management has publicly committed to annual margin improvement, has put a former Nestlé CFO in charge of both costs and deals, and is diverting "reinvestment" into acquisitions that sit below BOI. Peers show the commercial and administrative lines are the most flexible (Roche cut marketing costs 8% in 2020 while raising pharmaceutical R&D 8%; Bristol Myers Squibb cut non-GAAP SG&A 14% in 2025 under a USD 2bn programme); Pfizer's USD 9.7bn programme shows how savings can be invisible in reported opex when reinvested. Sanofi's position sits between: credible, modest, mostly absorbed.

Large cuts. Material absolute cuts (the post-2031 scenario in which Dupixent loses perhaps EUR 15bn to 20bn of sales within three years) are outside the four-year window, and the evidence that Sanofi can flex its cost base down at that speed is absent. Within the window, what matters is that management will likely start pre-positioning (sales-force planning for the long-acting successors; general-medicines exit) from 2029, which we reflect only as holding SG&A flat.

Where I differ from the first set. C-77 estimated 1.5 to 3 points of sales of reducible cost over three years; we see a little more (about 3 to 4 points over four years), but agree that most of it does not reach the margin. C-14 treated restructuring as a recurring operating cost "the model should carry, not exclude": we carry it in cash flow and quantify the EPS effect, but keep business EPS on the company's definition because every forecast the Consumer compares with is on that basis.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4750P-0550
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?D5026th of 42full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

D5014th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: within the four-year window the business is dominated by one reinforcing loop that is slowing (chronic patients accumulating on Dupixent) and two balancing loops that cap what it delivers (Regeneron's profit share, and currency exposure rising with the US share). Together they produce revenue that keeps growing after new-patient share has peaked, and a margin that rises only slowly. The loop that will most move EPS between 2027 and 2030 is not operational but the capital-allocation loop (pipeline failure, therefore acquisition, therefore debt and amortisation), because it decides whether 2028 to 2030 EPS sits about 8% above or about 1% to 6% below the organic path. The reinforcing negative loops the first set feared (reorganisation and attrition; acquisition prices and returns) are real but act mainly on years beyond 2030 and on the multiple, not on the line items in the window.

Loop 1, chronic accumulation (reinforcing, slowing). Effect: revenue up, with a lag. Each year's new Dupixent patients stay on a chronic therapy, so volume grows even while share of new starts falls (from 80% to 90% to 30% to 40% in biologic-naive atopic dermatitis, Deutsche Bank panel, September 2026). Competitors' consumer advertising enlarges the treated market (advanced-therapy penetration about 20% in atopic dermatitis, single digits in COPD). Arithmetic: with about 1.5m patients at end-2026 and net additions falling from about 0.3m a year to about 0.2m, average patients on therapy still grow about 17% in 2027, about 10% in 2028 and low single digits by 2030. This is why our Dupixent growth falls from about +31% (2026, constant currency including GTN) to +14% (2027), +8%, +3% and 0%, and why 2027 Dupixent revenue is more predictable than the share data alone suggest. COPD (pulmonologist-driven, where Dupixent still leads above 300 eosinophils per microlitre) is the loop's main engine in 2027 to 2028; dermatology new-patient share is its main leak. What would break it inside the window: a clean oral STAT6 result from Kymera (KT-621, Phase 2b around year-end 2026) would not cut 2027 revenue but would accelerate rebating; zumilokibart (AbbVie, launch targeted 2029) and head-to-head Rhapsido data (Novartis, 2027) cut the 2029 to 2030 tail.

Loop 2, Regeneron profit share (balancing). Effect: caps BOI growth on Dupixent at about 45 cents per euro. As Dupixent grows, Regeneron's share grows with it (its share of collaboration profits as a percentage of net sales rose to 38% in Q3 2026 from 29%, partly because the development balance was repaid). The more successful Dupixent is, the more of the group's growth is shared. This loop also lifts the US-dollar share of sales (43% FY2023, 50.8% FY2025, 60.4% Q3 2026), which feeds Loop 3.

Loop 3, currency (balancing on reported earnings, rising sensitivity). The cost base is euro-heavy while US sales rise, so each year the EPS sensitivity to EUR/USD increases: about EUR 0.23 of EPS per 0.05 move today, about EUR 0.26 by 2028 on our mix. Hedges (EUR 16.4bn of forward sales at June 2026, of which EUR 13.4bn in US dollars) expire by 2027, so 2027 is partly protected and 2028 is not. We assume EUR/USD flat at about 1.16; the market's tails (1.30 on aggressive Federal Reserve cuts, below parity on a French political shock in 2027) translate to about -EUR 0.65 and +EUR 0.75 of 2028 EPS respectively.

Loop 4, flat R&D and margin (balancing over time). Holding R&D flat raises 2027 margin (worth about EUR 0.9bn against cost growing with sales) but thins the organic replacement pipeline, which feeds Loop 5. Inside the window it is positive for line items; its cost shows after 2030.

Loop 5, pipeline failure, acquisition, leverage and amortisation (reinforcing negative on returns, neutral on business EPS). Late-stage failures (amlitelimab, itepekimab, tolebrutinib, riliprubart, balinatunfib; frexalimab delayed to 2028) force reliance on bought assets in the most crowded part of the market (AbbVie for Apogee about USD 10.9bn; Novartis for Avidity USD 12bn, whose lead asset then failed Phase III in September 2026). Bought assets are priced on peak sales, so returns on capital fall (group ROIC about 10% in 2025), the multiple stays low, and equity cannot fund deals, which pushes deals onto debt. Business EPS hides most of this because acquired-intangible amortisation is excluded. For the line items: net debt and interest rise (P-02), D&A ex-acquired amortisation is unaffected, IFRS EPS diverges further from business EPS (amortisation rising from about EUR 2bn to EUR 2.5bn to 2.7bn a year), and cash returns fall. Historical precedent at single-product-cliff companies is mixed: Roche (2018 to 2020) replaced the Avastin, Herceptin and Rituxan cliff with internally developed launches (Ocrevus, Hemlibra, Tecentriq; CHF 3.9bn of incremental sales in 2020 against CHF 5.1bn lost); AbbVie built Skyrizi and Rinvoq before Humira (organic and partnered, plus Allergan for diversification) and held a 48% adjusted operating margin; Bristol Myers Squibb and Pfizer bought heavily and have not yet shown that the purchases cover their cliffs (Pfizer's USD 4.4bn impairments on Seagen and Global Blood Therapeutics assets in late 2025). Acquisition-led replacement creates value when the asset is commercial or near-commercial and adjacent to existing infrastructure (Sanofi's own Genzyme, Bioverativ/ALTUVIIIO, Blueprint so far) and destroys it when it is early and in a crowded field (Translate Bio, Kiadis, Provention/Tzield).

Loop 6, reorganisation and attrition (reinforcing negative, low evidence quality). Expert calls (2025 to 2026) describe field-force attrition, the elimination of the US paediatric sales force and a slow Tzield relaunch (2025 sales about USD 73m). The loop raises restructuring cash and slows launches. We reflect it only in restructuring cash of EUR 0.7bn to 0.9bn a year and in launch growth (+24%, +18%, +14%, +12%) slightly below what management's EUR 10bn 2030 launch target needs. The commercial engine for Dupixent itself shows no sign of damage.

Loop 7, the expanded Regeneron alliance re-using the Dupixent franchise (reinforcing, but after the window). The four long-acting antibodies (registration-enabling studies from late 2027 or early 2028; possible launch around 2030) would let Sanofi move patients before biosimilars arrive in 2031. Inside the window the loop is a cost (USD 1bn upfront in Q4 2026, capitalised; 50:50 co-funded development in R&D milestones up to USD 7bn, capitalised). Its value appears in the multiple long before it appears in the line items.

Loop 8, low multiple and capital allocation (the switch). At 8.2 times forward earnings, a buyback earns more than 12% on EPS, equity-funded deals are unattractive, and every euro of debt capacity has a visible opportunity cost. If the strategy update or the first large deal disappoints, the multiple stays low and the buyback arithmetic becomes ever more compelling; if a disciplined plan with buybacks emerges, EPS and the multiple rise together (reinforcing). This is why the capital decision is the single largest swing in 2028 EPS (about +8% to -6% around organic).

Net shape of the line items. Revenue: reinforcing accumulation keeps growth at 4% to 6% to 2028 despite falling new-patient share and EUR 1.3bn to 1.6bn of disposals. Margin: balancing loops hold BOI margin expansion to about 0.5 point a year. EPS: organic +8% a year on average 2027 to 2029, then +2% in 2030. Cash and balance sheet: shaped by Loop 5 and Loop 8. Which loops most move EPS 2027 to 2030, in order: capital allocation (Loop 8 and 5, about plus or minus 8%), Dupixent accumulation net of new-patient leakage (Loop 1, about plus or minus 4% by 2029), currency (Loop 3, about plus or minus 3% per 0.05), Regeneron's share (Loop 2, deterministic, already in every model).

6c

Case simplicity

7 answers
Judgement61C-7950D-0266D-0356D-0457D-0566D-0660
Group judgement

Risks

–61

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The financial forecasts for the next 18 months are predictable to an unusual degree for a company with this much to resolve, and what is unpredictable is not operational. The work of the first two sets is congruent: one story, numbers that reconcile under independent recalculation, and disagreements that the evidence settles. The forecasts rest on strengths Sanofi has shown repeatedly (Dupixent volume, launch execution in rare disease, cost-holding, conservative guidance) and ask for a repeat of 2026's underlying performance, not for anything new. The drivers are few, large and unusually well observed from outside the company, so progress can be followed week by week for the main product and quarter by quarter for almost everything else. The shape of the period is known in advance: a deceptively comfortable first quarter of 2027, a trough in the second quarter published in late July 2027, and recovery from the second half as the comparisons clear.

Three things limit that predictability, and the memo should name them precisely.

  1. A management decision, not a market, sets the widest part of the cone. Whether Sanofi buys a company the size of Insmed, a smaller asset, or its own shares moves 2028 EPS by more than every operating uncertainty combined, and the second set's EUR 10bn case is now the average of two quite different outcomes. The decision is weeks to months away.
  2. Net price is the one powerful driver that cannot be observed. It is largely contracted for 2027, which helps; it is exposed in the 2028 round; and its accounting expression, gross-to-net adjustments, has a history of favourable surprises that the forecasts prudently exclude.
  3. Concentration is rising inside the forecast window, with Dupixent approaching half of sales as its patent expiry approaches. This does not reduce the predictability of the next 18 months; it is why the market has declined for a year to pay for that predictability, and why the multiple has been moved by the sector and by questions about 2031, not by rising estimates.

For the memo's judgement on riskiness: the forecasts themselves are low-risk and simple to follow; the case is not, because its valuation outcome depends on an acquisition whose size, price and quality are unknown and on a terminal-value debate that 18 months of delivery will not settle.

C-79

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

D5025th of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the perimeter is narrowing in business lines (one Biopharma segment after Opella; three priority areas; mature tail outsourced) while revenue concentration on one product and one country is rising. That makes 2026 to 2028 more predictable and the years after 2030 less predictable.

  • Concentration: Dupixent about 40% of 2026 sales (about EUR 20bn of about EUR 51bn) and more than half of business operating income after the profit share, on our estimate; the US 60% of Q3 2026 sales; the three largest US wholesalers 42% of H1 2026 sales.
  • Diversity that remains: vaccines (about 17% of sales, more than 150 rabies-endemic countries, global public buyers), rare disease (Genzyme base), launches (about EUR 5.5bn annualised), general medicines (declining), operations in many countries (China EUR 762m a quarter).
  • Predictability: the forecasting question reduces to four variables: Dupixent volume and US net price, the EUR/USD rate, the scale and timing of M&A, and vaccine demand. That is a narrow, modelable set for the next 18 months; it becomes a single binary-like variable (the slope of Dupixent's erosion against the size of the acquired bridge) after 2030.
D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

–66

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: for the next 18 months the expected change is plausible and rests almost entirely on strengths Sanofi has demonstrated repeatedly: growing Dupixent volume, executing rare-disease launches, holding costs, and guiding conservatively. The strengths it would need to acquire (earning a return on a large acquisition; a productive research engine) are not needed to deliver the forecasts before 2029. Inside the 18-month window they appear only as a risk, that a large transaction dilutes earnings, and never as a requirement. From 2029 the position reverses: the forecasts begin to require them. The thesis is therefore well-founded for the horizon this set is asked about, and progressively less so beyond it.

The strengths the 18-month forecasts require, and the record for each.

Strength required What the forecasts ask of it Demonstrated?
Dupixent volume +14% sales in 2027 after about +31% in 2026 Yes, strongly. More than 1.5m patients; total and new prescriptions still rising in the oldest indication; about 80% of patients stay on therapy in surveyed practices; a successful COPD launch with broad commercial and Medicare coverage (Jefferies, 30 October 2026); consensus has underestimated it for two years. Atopic dermatitis penetration is still only high-teens to 20% against 40% to 50% in psoriasis, the nearest precedent, where several blockbusters grew together
Launch execution Launch medicines +24% in 2027 from about EUR 5.2bn Yes for the products that matter: ALTUVIIIO reached EUR 1.2bn in 2025; Ayvakit +46% and Rezurock +25% to 43% in recent quarters; the group grew 31% in the third quarter of 2026. No for Tzield (EUR 23m in the second quarter of 2026; up to 43% of eligible families refuse the 14-day infusion course) and slow for Qfitlia. Deutsche Bank's prescription tracker points to Ayvakit below consensus. The forecast needs the strong products to carry the weak ones
Holding costs R&D +1% and selling and administrative costs flat in 2027 Yes, for two years: third-quarter 2026 R&D +0.8% and selling costs -0.9% on sales +14%; headcount down 13% since 2023. The older record is the opposite: the Play to Win programme delivered EUR 2.7bn and reinvested all of it
Conservative guidance A first 2027 guide that is then met or beaten Yes: delivery exceeded the initial guide in each of the seven years 2020 to 2026, by 2 to 7 points of EPS growth

The test that matters most. As set out in D-01, 2027 needs a second consecutive year in which underlying BOI grows about twice as fast as underlying sales. The business demonstrated exactly this in 2026, using the same levers that are planned for 2027 (a flat cost base, launch mix, royalty income). So the requirement is a repetition, not a step up. It is, however, a requirement with no slack: one point of unplanned growth in the EUR 17.4bn of R&D and selling costs is about EUR 0.17bn, or 1.1% of BOI.

Why cost-holding now reaches the margin when it did not before, and why that is only partly a strength. The second set observed that the "reinvestment" is now going into acquisitions and licences, which are capitalised and whose amortisation is excluded from BOI, and not into the R&D line. I agree, and draw the consequence for this question: part of the demonstrated margin improvement is a change in where the cost of renewing the portfolio is recorded, not a reduction in that cost. On business EPS the strength is demonstrated. On cash and on IFRS earnings it is not (the USD 1bn Regeneron upfront, up to USD 7bn of milestones, the Blueprint and Dynavax premiums). The memo should claim the first and not the second.

The strengths the thesis does not yet have.

  • Earning a return on large acquisitions. Sanofi's own record: Genzyme and Bioverativ built real franchises but Bioverativ and Ablynx were neutral to business EPS for several years; Translate Bio was written off; Provention's Tzield has disappointed; Blueprint is growing, and Sanofi cut about a third of its workforce within a year, a faster integration than before. Peer precedents assembled by AlphaSense point the same way: rebuilding by acquisition dilutes margins before it helps (AstraZeneca's core margin fell from 31% to 26% in 2017 to 2018 before recovering above 30% after 2020); AbbVie's earnings outgrew its Pharmacyclics debt within two years because the asset was commercial and growing; Bristol Myers Squibb and Pfizer have not yet covered their cliffs and both de-rated; Novartis impaired USD 1.94bn of its USD 12bn Avidity purchase seven months after closing. The pattern is that the asset's commercial stage decides the outcome. Insmed, if it is the target, is commercial-stage, which is the more favourable kind, at a price that leaves little room.
  • A productive research engine. Not demonstrated; the new governance cannot show results before 2028, and peer experience is that a rebuild takes five to seven years.

How much of each year's forecast depends on undemonstrated strengths. In the second set's numbers the acquired asset contributes EUR -0.05bn of BOI in 2027, +0.15bn in 2028, +0.40bn in 2029 and +0.65bn in 2030. So none of the 2027 growth and about 1% of 2028 BOI depends on it; by 2030 it is about a fifth of the cumulative BOI growth from 2026. The internal pipeline contributes nothing in any year. This is the cleanest way to state the answer: the next 18 months rely on demonstrated strengths for all of their growth; years three and four rely on an undemonstrated one for a growing minority of it.

Where I differ from the earlier sets. Neither is wrong, but both describe 2027 mainly as a year of headwinds absorbed. The more useful description for judging plausibility is that 2027 asks for a repeat of 2026's underlying performance with price turning from a help to a hindrance. Price is the one input for which the demonstrated record is unfavourable (C-17), and it is the reason the plausibility is high and not very high.

D-03In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?–56full answer under 2a Internal risks
D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

–57

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the cone for FY2027 business EPS is about EUR 9.0 to EUR 10.3 around a centre of EUR 9.61, that is about 6% below to 7% above. Operations alone account for about plus or minus 4%; currency and corporate action, in roughly equal parts, account for the rest. The operating skew is neutral to slightly positive, the corporate skew is negative for earnings inside 18 months, and the two roughly cancel, so the base case sits close to the probability-weighted mean. The case is highly predictable in revenue, well predictable in operating profit, and moderately predictable in EPS. Its shape within the period (a trough in the first half of 2027, most visibly in the second quarter) is more predictable than its level.

The cone in three layers (FY2027 business EPS, EUR).

Layer Plausible range Skew What sets the width
Operations, at constant currency and with no transaction About 9.35 to 10.10 around 9.74 Neutral to slightly positive Dupixent growth of 11% to 17% (plus or minus 0.19); launch growth plus or minus 5 points (0.16); group net price plus or minus 1 point (0.22 to 0.25); Amvuttra royalty and vaccines (0.07 to 0.08 each). These do not all go wrong together; combined they give about plus or minus 0.36
Currency About -0.4 to +0.3 for EUR/USD between 1.25 and 1.10 Unknown; symmetrical on the evidence EUR 0.23 per 0.05 (company disclosure); EUR 13.4bn of forward dollar sales expire during 2027, so 2027 is partly protected and the first half of 2028 is not
Corporate action About -0.4 to +0.4 Negative for earnings A buyback of EUR 5bn to 10bn adds 0.2 to 0.4 in 2027; a EUR 10bn acquisition takes off about 0.13; a transaction of EUR 30bn or more closing in mid-2027 takes off about 0.2 to 0.4 in 2027 and about 0.4 to 0.6 in a full year

An empirical check on the width. The market's own record of forecasting this company one year ahead matches the cone. FactSet's consensus for FY2026 has moved from a low of EUR 8.41 to an expected outcome near EUR 9.05 (about +8%); FY2025 ended at EUR 7.83 after consensus had ranged from EUR 7.71 to EUR 8.67; consensus for FY2027 has already ranged from EUR 8.94 to EUR 10.44; and individual brokers today span EUR 8.49 to EUR 10.07. A cone of 6% to 7% either side is what Sanofi has actually delivered against expectations, so I regard the second set's range as well-founded, not as a guess.

Three refinements to the second set's cone, from the newer evidence.

  1. US net price for calendar 2027 is largely settled already; the price risk has moved into 2028. The 2027 formularies are published: Express Scripts keeps Dupixent on its specialty tier, CVS Caremark keeps it preferred beside Ebglyss and Nemluvio, and the AlphaSense report found no exclusion. Price for 2027 is therefore narrower than the one-point swing the second set allowed. The exposure is the contracting round for 2028, negotiated during 2027, when three things coincide: Express Scripts, CVS and OptumRx are all moving to fee-based models that weaken the "rebate wall" which protected the incumbent; Ebglyss has reached nearly 95% of commercial plans; and oral and long-acting data will be accumulating. This narrows the cone for FY2027 and widens it for the first half of 2028, the last six months of the 18.
  2. Gross-to-net adjustments are a two-way source of quarterly surprise of roughly EUR 100m to 300m, and their history is of releases. EUR 155m, EUR 282m and EUR 667m were released in 2023, 2024 and 2025; a negative true-up occurred in general medicines in the second quarter of 2023. The forecasts assume none in 2027, in line with management's guide to "slightly negative" pricing and its use of new tools to provision more exactly. If the habit of over-provision persists even in part, 2027 sales are EUR 0.2bn to 0.4bn higher than modelled. This is the main reason I put the operating skew at slightly positive. The annual report in February 2027 will show the 2026 release and the year-end provision (EUR 5.9bn at end-2025, up from EUR 4.8bn) and will largely settle it.
  3. The corporate layer has become more skewed since the second set wrote. My probabilities for the period to end-2027, after the Insmed report: a transaction of about EUR 25bn or more, about 35%; one of EUR 8bn to 15bn, about 30%; bolt-on purchases only, about 20%; bolt-ons with a buyback of EUR 5bn or more, about 15%. Weighted, these take about EUR 0.10 off organic FY2027 EPS and about EUR 0.12 off FY2028, which is almost exactly what the second set's single EUR 10bn case takes off (EUR 0.13 and EUR 0.10). So the base case is right as an average but is no longer the single most likely outcome; the distribution has two humps, a large dilutive transaction and a small or no transaction, with the base case between them. The memo should present the EUR 10bn case as a weighted centre, and show the Insmed-scale case (business EPS about 4% to 6% lower in 2028, net debt about 2.3 to 2.5 times EBITDA, probably the end of the AA rating) beside it.

The shape inside the 18 months is the most predictable feature. Almost every item that separates 2027 from 2026 has a known quarter. The second quarter of 2026 contained about EUR 0.6bn of BOI that will not recur (the final Regeneron reimbursement of EUR 291m, the EUR 200m Sarclisa reversal, and a gross-to-net release), against quarterly BOI of about EUR 3.1bn. The first quarter of 2026 contained only the reimbursement. The third quarter of 2026 contained the largest gross-to-net release (about EUR 300m of sales). Carrying the second set's bridge to quarters, on my own arithmetic: business EPS grows by a mid-to-high single-digit percentage in the first quarter of 2027, is flat to slightly down in the second quarter, and recovers to about +8% in the second half; the first half as a whole is +3% to +4%, with a plausible range of about -2% to +8%. The first half of 2028 then has clean comparisons and the forecasts show BOI growth of about 8% to 9%. The result published in late July 2027 is therefore the point of weakest reported growth in the whole 18 months, and the result published in late April 2027 will look deceptively comfortable.

How predictable, in one line each. FY2026: within 1% (one quarter left; guidance raised on 30 October 2026). FY2027 revenue at constant currency: within 3%. FY2027 organic EPS at constant currency: about two chances in three of landing within 3% of EUR 9.74, and about 85% within 5%. FY2027 reported EPS including currency and transactions: about 60% within 5% of EUR 9.61. What lies outside the cone, at low probability and large size, is unchanged from the first set (C-81): a Dupixent safety signal or a supply interruption.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

–66

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: helpful, and more helpful than Sanofi's own disclosure alone would suggest, because the drivers of this case are reported by other parties earlier, more often and in more detail than by Sanofi. Over the next six months the volume engine and the known 2027 headwinds can be followed closely. Two drivers cannot be followed at all until the company chooses to speak: the gross-to-net line and capital allocation. One important reading, the trough in the second quarter of 2027, falls just outside the six months.

What can be followed, and from whom.

Driver Sanofi's disclosure Outside disclosure that is earlier or finer
Dupixent volume Quarterly sales by region Weekly US prescriptions (Deutsche Bank publishes a weekly tracker); Regeneron reports US and international sales in dollars and its profit share, at the same time as Sanofi
Regeneron's share of profit "Other operating expenses" each quarter Regeneron's reported share (38% of combined sales in the third quarter of 2026)
Royalty income Not broken out each quarter Alnylam reports Amvuttra sales the day before Sanofi; the royalty tiers are public (30% above USD 1.5bn of annual sales); Alnylam gives 2027 guidance in mid-February 2027
US Beyfortus Quarterly sales Sobi reports its Beyfortus royalty two or three days before Sanofi; its shortfall in October 2026 foreshadowed the 39% fall in US Beyfortus sales
Competitors for new patients None Lilly (Ebglyss) and Galderma (Nemluvio) report their sales before Sanofi does; published formularies show 2027 access
2027 headwinds Quantified by the company: disposals (about EUR 1.3bn of sales), the end of reimbursement, pricing direction, R&D flat; a detailed note to analysts before each quarter Brazil's competition authority (it recommended approval of the Medley sale with remedies in November 2026)
Currency Explicit sensitivity by currency Spot rates

The consequence is that most of the change in BOI from one quarter to the next can be estimated before Sanofi reports. That is why the large surprises of 2026 came from the items no outside party reports: gross-to-net releases and the timing of costs.

What cannot be followed.

  • Net price. Sanofi gives no quarterly figure for gross-to-net adjustments and none by product; the amounts in 2026 are brokers' estimates made after the event. The only hard disclosure is annual (prior-period adjustments and year-end provisions in the annual report, next due in February 2027). GSK, by comparison, publishes a full reconciliation from gross to net. Rebate terms are confidential; even Regeneron sued in 2025 for access to the contracts.
  • Capital allocation. There is no disclosure until a decision is announced. The strategy update in early December 2026 is the first scheduled occasion.
  • The conversion of launch sales into profit. No franchise margins are given, and the launch group's growth rate includes the acquired Ayvakit.
  • Cash and working capital between half-years. Full statements are half-yearly; the quarterly releases give sales, a business income statement and net debt.
  • The meaning of "margins improve every year to 2030". It was said to one broker, with no number and no stated definition.

The six-month calendar, with what each item settles.

  • Early December 2026, strategy update: the leverage and rating language (whether "about 1 time" and AA survive), any return hurdle for acquisitions, any buyback, any mid-term margin or EPS framework.
  • Around year-end 2026, Kymera's KT-621 Phase 2b result: a share-price and 2028-contracting event, not a 2027 earnings event.
  • 28 January 2027, full-year results: whether Dupixent reaches about EUR 5.5bn in the fourth quarter, which keeps the 2027 forecast on course; whether a further gross-to-net release is acknowledged; the wording of the first 2027 guidance (I-01).
  • February 2027, annual report: the size of 2026's prior-period rebate releases against EUR 667m in 2025, and the year-end provision. This is the single most informative document on the quality of the 2026 base and it receives little attention.
  • Mid-February 2027, Alnylam's guidance: the 2027 royalty.
  • Weekly: US Dupixent prescriptions; the second set's warning level is growth below about 12%.
  • Late April 2027, first-quarter sales: the first quarter with the Cheplapharm transfer under way; expected to look comfortable for the reason given in D-04.

So within six months the Consumer will have seen the capital decision or at least its framework, the first official view of 2027, two readings of the volume engine and the one annual reading of gross-to-net. The reading that tests the forecasts hardest, the second quarter of 2027, comes about eight months from now.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

–60

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: clear on the revenue side of every driver, and clear all the way to EPS for one year ahead, because the company gives growth ranges, product ambitions, currency sensitivities and the main 2027 adjustments in numbers. It is unclear in the middle of the chain: the conversion of revenue into profit is disclosed for only one driver, Dupixent, and that through the partner's accounts; nothing the company says about margins beyond the current year can be traced to a line. The external transmission is clear for currency, royalties and the Regeneron share, and opaque for the one external force that matters most to earnings, the passage from payer behaviour to net price.

Statements that trace cleanly to a line and to EPS.

  • 2026 guidance (sales growth in the low teens and business EPS growth in the mid-teens at constant exchange rates; currency about -1% on sales and -1% to -2% on EPS; tax about 21%). Every step to EPS is given.
  • Dupixent to about EUR 25bn in 2030. Product sales are reported quarterly by region. Regeneron's share (half of US profit; 35% to 45% outside the US) appears in other operating expenses and can be checked against Regeneron's own accounts. From this the second set derived about 45 cents of BOI per incremental euro; the third quarter of 2026 bears it out (Dupixent sales up about EUR 1.47bn, other operating expenses up EUR 0.87bn, part of which was the end of reimbursement).
  • The end of Regeneron's reimbursement. Quantified by year by the company and visible quarter by quarter in Regeneron's accounts: about EUR 0.5bn less BOI in 2027 than in 2026, all in the first half.
  • Royalty income. About EUR 1bn in 2026 against about EUR 0.5bn in 2025 (company); public tiers; no cost attached, so it passes to BOI in full.
  • R&D flat in 2027, then growing with sales. A single line.
  • Currency. EUR 1,069m of sales and EUR 0.23 of business EPS per 0.05 move in the dollar rate.

Statements that trace only part of the way.

  • The disposals. The sales effect is given (about EUR 1.3bn in 2027). The profit effect is not; the only figure is Deutsche Bank's EUR 440m of EBITDA on the Cheplapharm portfolio, and the earlier sets used lower numbers (D-01). What Sanofi receives in return does not come back through the same line: the Cheplapharm stake will be accounted for by the equity method and, under Sanofi's stated rule, associates not managed as part of the business are excluded from business EPS, as Opella's already is. The preferred dividends (up to about EUR 202m a year from 2028) and earn-outs (about EUR 93m a year) depend on Cheplapharm's cash and distributable reserves; Fitch expects its free cash flow to be subdued from 2028, and Deutsche Bank does not capitalise them. On this evidence I disagree with the second set's inclusion of half of the preferred dividends in business earnings from 2028, and have removed them from the inputs (net financial items become EUR -720m, -600m and -520m in FY2028 to FY2030). The effect is about EUR 0.07 a share, 0.6%. If they are paid and are counted, they are upside.
  • The 2030 ambitions for launches (about EUR 10bn) and vaccines (about EUR 9bn). Sales trace; margins do not. The three ambitions together cover about three-quarters of the sales the forecasts show for 2030, at constant exchange rates, and say nothing about profit.
  • "Pricing slightly negative in 2027." A direction without a number, and the gross-to-net line through which it will act is not disclosed.

Statements that do not trace.

  • "Operating margin will improve each and every year through 2030." No number, no definition, said to one broker. On the forecasts the 2027 improvement is 0.3 point, small enough to be lost in the definition, and a large acquisition would reverse it. It is a statement of intent.
  • Acquisition capacity. Expressed as capacity (EUR 15bn early-stage, EUR 20bn to 25bn late-stage) and as a leverage guide; neither is a commitment, and the business-EPS definition means the largest cost of an acquisition, amortisation, never reaches the reported measure.
  • "Only a moderate revenue impact" when Dupixent loses exclusivity. Unquantified and contradicted by brokers' models (Redburn assumes ten biosimilar entrants at discounts of 60% to 80%).

A timing point the trace reveals. Management said that royalty income fully offset the end of reimbursement in 2026. That was true (each about EUR 0.5bn). It does not repeat: in 2027 the further loss of reimbursement is again about EUR 0.5bn, concentrated in the first half, while royalty growth is at best about EUR 0.3bn spread over the year, and the royalty rate restarts at its lowest tier each January. An offset that was complete in the second half of 2026 is less than half complete in the first half of 2027. This is one of the specific reasons the first half is the trough.

External transmission.

Value-chain dynamic Path to EPS Clarity
Dollar against euro Sales and costs, with a published sensitivity High
Regeneron's share; Sobi's royalty on US Beyfortus (rising to 30% to 35% by 2028) Contractual percentage of a reported line High
Alnylam's sales, payer restrictions and the 340B reimbursement cut from January 2027 Amvuttra sales to a tiered royalty to BOI High in mechanism, medium in amount (Alnylam has not quantified its 340B exposure)
Payer and pharmacy-benefit-manager decisions Formulary position to rebate to net price to sales to shared profit Low: positions are public, rebates are not; the effect appears months later as a gross-to-net adjustment that is itself undisclosed
US public purchasing of vaccines and the childhood schedule CDC orders to Beyfortus and paediatric vaccine sales Medium: visible afterwards, lumpy, subject to litigation
US pricing policy (Medicaid alignment, Medicare redesign, international reference models from 2027) Mandatory discounts within gross-to-net Low to medium
Competitor clinical results (KT-621, zumilokibart, Rhapsido) New-patient share to rebates to net price, with a lag of a year or more Low inside 18 months; none reaches 2027 earnings directly

The pattern is that the external forces with a clear path are the ones already in every model, and the force with an unclear path, net price, is the one both earlier sets identified as the most powerful single lever on earnings (one point is about 2.5% of EPS).

C-82

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

B404th of 42

Layer 1 · Risks

The Investment Analyst's heading What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

Judgement: the near-term share price is more exposed to perception events than to fundamental ones, and three of them are dated within six months.

  • KT-621 Phase 2b (Kymera), around year-end 2026: a clean, Dupixent-like result would be read as "oral Dupixent" and hit Sanofi's terminal value regardless of 2027 earnings.
  • The December 2026 strategy update and any accompanying deal: a high-priced acquisition is the most likely trigger of a sharp de-rating; conversely, a disciplined plan with buybacks could re-rate the shares.
  • Full-year results and first 2027 framework (28 January 2027): the optics of 2027 (GTN normalisation, EUR 1.3bn divested sales, Regeneron reimbursement loss, possibly stronger euro) could make 2027 look like a sharp slowdown even if underlying growth is healthy.
  • Sector flows: the Fitness for Fighting digest notes the Pharma/biotech Sector's October relative strength came entirely on index down days (money moving, reversible within weeks); Sanofi's short interest (1.2% of free float, double the peer median, up 0.7 points year to date) and put/call ratio (about 2x) show hedging demand. A rotation back to cyclicals would hit the group regardless of Sanofi's numbers.
  • Read-across from peers: AstraZeneca's AVANZAR and Novartis's pipeline failures moved the group; Lilly's and AbbVie's immunology data at congresses move Sanofi.
  • Politics: US midterm results and pricing rhetoric; the French presidential election (2027) through the France country risk premium; an L'Oréal stake placement (overhang).

Track record (guidance raise/sell-side upgrades) ~L2-3Y

C-61How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?F4839th of 42full answer under 4b Management quality
C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

B+685th of 42

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: under the current CFO (since April 2024) and CEO (since May 2026), the pattern is consistent: conservative initial guidance, raised after strong quarters, with delivery at or above the top of the raised range. This is close to the Wisdom Library's "sandbagging precision" archetype, but on a short record, and the earlier record includes one major negative reset.

  • 2025: initial "low double-digit" business EPS growth (before buyback); sales raised in July 2025 to the upper end of high single digit; delivered sales +9.9% and EPS +15% (12.2% before buyback).
  • 2026: initial high-single-digit sales with EPS "slightly faster"; raised in July (sales about 10%) and October (sales low-teens, EPS mid-teens); currency guidance improved.
  • 2022 (previous CEO): guided 15% EPS growth; delivered 17.1%.
  • Negative reset: October 2023, FY2024 guidance below consensus and the 32% 2025 margin target abandoned to fund R&D (the "investment" was later partly written off).
  • CEO behaviour: Garijo raised guidance twice in her first six months while cutting pipeline ambitions, which is the right combination (financial guidance evidence-anchored, scientific claims deflated). At Merck KGaA she missed long-term revenue targets, so her behaviour on mid-term targets is less reliable than on annual guidance.
C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

C+6410th of 42

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: over one year, consistent and large beats; over three years, mostly beats with two profit misses; over five years, one major negative reset (October 2023) in an otherwise beat-or-meet record. In a strong-Dupixent environment like today's, Sanofi beats.

  • Last 12 months: Q4 2025 results led to a "guidance in debate" reaction (Morgan Stanley) but full-year 2025 landed at the top of guidance; Q1 2026 double-digit growth; Q2 2026 sales +7% and EPS +10% against consensus; Q3 2026 sales +9% and EPS +26% against consensus; guidance raised twice.
  • Three years: Q4 2023 sales and profits missed (Jefferies); 2024 results and 2025 guidance in line (Bernstein); Q3 2024 beat driven by vaccines; Q1 2025 sales +3%, business operating income +8%, EPS +5% against consensus; Q2 2025 sales beat but profit short (Jefferies); Q3 2025 sales ahead and profits beat.
  • Five years: 2022 EPS growth of 17.1% against 15% guided; October 2023 guidance cut and margin target withdrawn.
  • Quality caveat: the size of the 2026 beats is partly accounting (GTN releases, the Sarclisa reversal) and cost timing (R&D and SG&A 6% to 7% below consensus in Q3); the Analyst should not extrapolate a 26% beat rate.
P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

B605th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the near-term forecasts deserve high weight and a slight upward lean; the medium-term forecasts deserve moderate weight and, on margins and the Dupixent tail, a downward lean. Sanofi's record splits sharply by horizon and by type of promise: it reliably meets and beats annual financial guidance and Dupixent volume expectations; it has repeatedly missed mid-term margin targets, pipeline timelines and the vaccines ambition.

The record, by type.

  • Annual guidance (reliable, conservative). 2022: guided about 15% EPS growth, delivered 17.1%. 2025: initial low-double-digit EPS growth, delivered +15% (12.2% before buyback), sales +9.9% at the top of a raised range. 2026: high-single-digit sales growth with EPS "slightly faster" in January, raised in July (sales about 10%) and October (sales low-teens, EPS mid-teens). One major negative reset: October 2023 (FY2024 guided below consensus; 32% 2025 margin target withdrawn).
  • Quarterly delivery (strong, partly accounting). Q2 2026 sales +7% and EPS +10% against consensus; Q3 2026 sales +9% and EPS +26%. Roughly one third to one half of the Q3 sales beat was GTN releases, and R&D and SG&A ran 6% to 7% below consensus.
  • Dupixent volume (consistently underestimated). The 2030 target was raised from EUR 22bn to about EUR 25bn; consensus revisions have been upward for two years; Q2 and Q3 2026 each beat by about 10% (about 5% to 6% underlying after GTN).
  • Mid-term targets (unreliable). 32% 2025 margin (abandoned); 2023 R&D day pipeline timelines (amlitelimab, itepekimab, tolebrutinib, frexalimab: failed or delayed); vaccines 2030 ambition cut from more than EUR 10bn to about EUR 9bn; Tzield relaunch weak. The new CEO's prior company (Merck KGaA) missed its EUR 25bn 2025 revenue target.

Weights and leans for each forecast horizon (our judgement):

Horizon Weight Lean against our base Why
FY2026 (one quarter left) Very high Slightly up (EUR 9.05 could be 9.10 to 9.15) Guidance has been conservative; Q4 implied flat on the year despite +10% sales; further GTN releases possible
H1 2027 High on revenue, medium on EPS Revenue up, EPS neutral Dupixent volume has beaten for two years; but the H1 headwinds (reimbursement, Sarclisa and GTN base, disposals) are mechanical and known
FY2027 High on revenue, medium-high on EPS Revenue slightly up; EPS neutral to slightly down if a deal closes early Organic EUR 9.74 sits on consensus; the deal and FX are the swing
FY2028 Medium Revenue neutral; margin slightly down Launch growth needs Tzield, Qfitlia and Ayvakit to keep compounding; R&D may grow faster than "with sales" once registration studies start
FY2029 to FY2030 Medium-low Down on Dupixent tail and royalties; up only if the acquired asset outperforms Competitor launches from 2029, tariff-exemption expiry (January 2029), nucresiran from about 2030, and Sanofi's record on mid-term targets

What the record implies for the next two guidance events. The first 2027 framework (results on 28 January 2027) will very likely be at constant exchange rates, exclude acquisitions not closed, and frame 2027 cautiously (pricing slightly negative, divestments, Regeneron reimbursement). On the pattern since April 2024 under the current CFO, an initial EPS growth range in the mid-single digits, raised during the year, is more likely than a framing above high-single digits. Our organic EUR 9.74 is +7.6% reported on our 2026 base; a "mid-to-high single digit" guide would be consistent. The direction of the lean is therefore: trust the revenue engine more than consensus does in 2027; trust management's margin claims only for 2027 to 2028; discount any 2030 numbers by about 5% for Sanofi's mid-term record.

Where I differ from the first set. C-68 expects the first 2027 guide "probably below the consensus 7.8%" EPS growth. We agree on the framing but note that, measured against our 2026 base of EUR 9.05 rather than the FY2025 base, consensus EUR 9.70 is +7.2%, and the company-compiled consensus quoted in early November (about EUR 9.2) is only about +2%. A cautious guide may land at or above the consensus the company itself circulates, even if below the FactSet median. The Consumer should check which consensus the company's January framing is measured against.

Forecasts and upgrades

7

Forecasts with good growth rates and higher growth/margins than past

–not yet rated

Memo topic 6, : the Rater's score

7a

Growth rate

4 answers
Judgement56C-4960P-0850P-1058
Group judgement

Financial forecasts

B565th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Sanofi's financial forecasts are better than its multiple implies and less dynamic than its 2026 numbers suggest. The engine is volume from an accumulating chronic patient base on Dupixent and from rare-disease launches, which together supply about 80% of gross revenue growth and are the most predictable part of the case to 2028. The brakes are structural: Regeneron takes about half of every incremental Dupixent euro; price erodes about 1% to 1.5% a year once 2026's accounting releases fall out; general medicines and disposals remove high-contribution sales. Net, BOI margin rises about 0.5 point a year, business EPS grows about 6% to 9% a year from 2027 to 2029 and about 3% in 2030, and FCF reaches about EUR 11bn to 12bn by 2029 to 2030. The first half of 2027 is the low point of reported growth, because the reimbursement loss, the Sarclisa and GTN base effects and the disposals all land in it. Our operating view sits within about 1% of consensus EPS for 2026 to 2028; the material difference is capital allocation, which consensus ignores and which we judge more likely than not to take the form of a debt-funded acquisition that dilutes business EPS slightly in 2027 to 2028 and returns less than its cost of capital. The alternative, a buyback at an earnings yield above 12%, would add about 8% to EPS. That switch, decided by management within the next six to twelve months, is the largest single swing in the forecasts and the most important thing for the next Analyst to test.

C-49What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?C+6013th of 42full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

D5016th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: below BOI, the next four years are quieter than the last six for business EPS (no Opella deconsolidation, no EUR 5bn buyback, a stable tax rate), so business EPS will track BOI closely: growth of about 6% to 9% a year from 2027 to 2029 on BOI growth of 6% to 9%. The two below-the-line items that matter are interest on acquisition debt (about -1% to -2% of EPS in 2027 to 2028) and the share count (flat, unless the strategy update brings a buyback). For IFRS net income and for free cash flow the picture is weaker than business EPS suggests, because excluded items (acquired-intangible amortisation, restructuring, capitalised upfronts and milestones) are rising with the buy-the-bridge strategy.

Item by item, against the past:

Business EPS path (EUR): 9.05 (2026, +15.6% on 7.83), 9.61 (+6.2%), 10.45 (+8.7%), 11.13 (+6.4%), 11.52 (+3.5%). Business net income: about EUR 10.9bn, 11.6bn, 12.6bn, 13.4bn, 13.9bn. Against the last six years, when business EPS was essentially flat from EUR 8.26 (2022) to 7.12 (2024, restated without Opella) and then rose to 7.83, the window delivers a compound growth of about 8% a year from 2025 to 2030, of which about 2.5 points come in 2026 from items that do not recur.

IFRS (reported) EPS. FY2025 IFRS EPS was EUR 4.26 against business EPS of 7.83, a gap of EUR 3.57 inflated by impairments (EUR 952m on amlitelimab in H1 2026 shows the risk recurs). Excluding impairments, the structural gap is about EUR 2.0 to 2.3 a share (amortisation about EUR 2bn, restructuring about EUR 1bn, less tax), rising slightly with acquisitions. IFRS EPS of about EUR 6.5 to 7.0 in 2027 to 2028 is a reasonable expectation absent impairments; the R&D review makes further impairments in 2027 likely, which the Consumer should treat as a known unknown for IFRS but not for business EPS.

Free cash flow (our definition: after interest, tax, lease payments, restructuring cash and all capital spending including intangibles; before acquisitions, disposals and dividends): EUR 7.45bn (2026), 8.55bn, 9.45bn, 11.0bn, 11.6bn, about 68% to 85% of business net income. Against consensus FCF (EUR 7.6bn, 8.8bn, 10.4bn, 12.2bn), ours is about EUR 0.2bn to 1.2bn lower, mainly because we deduct restructuring cash and the interest on acquisition debt, and because we include capitalised in-licensing (the USD 1bn Regeneron upfront in 2026; milestones thereafter) in capital spending. Bridge to the company's own FCF: the company's definition excludes large intangible acquisitions above a threshold (the Regeneron upfront) and includes disposal proceeds; cash tax is also lower than the business tax charge because amortisation and restructuring are partly tax-deductible (about EUR 0.6bn a year, which we include). On the company's basis 2026 FCF should be about EUR 9bn (nine months: EUR 6,993m), about 18.5% of sales; the "at least 20% of sales" ambition is reached on our numbers only around 2029.

Working capital. Net working capital (receivables plus inventories less payables) was 27.7% of trailing sales at June 2026 and 25.8% at December 2025 (year-ends are seasonally lower). The inventory programme (about 30 fewer days in 2025, a further 30 targeted for 2026) is offset by receivables rising with US sales and wholesaler concentration (42% of H1 2026 sales). We model 24.5% at end-2026 to 2028 and 24.0% in 2029 to 2030, a cash outflow of about EUR 0.2bn to 0.75bn a year as sales grow, not the EUR 0.5bn to 1bn inflow the first set (C-52) assumed for 2026 to 2027. I disagree with the first set here: the 2025 inflow (EUR 2.1bn) came from one-off inventory days; the H1 2026 outflow (EUR 412m) shows that receivables growth now dominates, and the Cheplapharm deferred inventory payment (over EUR 500m spread over three years) is a receivable, not a release.

What would change the picture. A buyback of EUR 5bn to 10bn (+4% to +9% EPS from the first full year); a larger deal (EUR 20bn to 25bn: -4% to -6% for two years); a successful method-of-use patent defence (no effect inside the window); disposal of the Opella stake (cash of perhaps EUR 4bn to 6bn on the April 2025 valuation, roughly neutral on business EPS if used to repay debt).

The next 18 months by half-year (net sales growth; EBITDA margin): H2 2026 +11.8%, 33.3%; H1 2027 +6.1%, 31.0%; H2 2027 +4.1%, 34.3%. Business EPS: H1 2027 about EUR 4.11 (+3% to +4%), H2 2027 about EUR 5.50 (+8%). H2 is seasonally stronger (flu vaccines, Q3 Dupixent seasonality, Q4 cost timing); H1 2027 carries the reimbursement loss, the Q2 2026 Sarclisa and GTN base effects and the start of the Cheplapharm transfer.

What the base case rests on, in order of importance, with what would make us change it:

  1. Capital allocation. A EUR 10bn debt-funded deal in mid-2027. Change: a strategy update in December 2026 that announces a buyback (EPS +4% to +9%) or a deal of EUR 20bn or more (EPS -4% to -6%).
  2. Dupixent volume. +14% in 2027 and +8% in 2028. Change: US weekly prescription trackers showing total prescriptions growing below about 12% into early 2027, or COPD uptake slowing; a strong KT-621 Phase 2b result accelerating rebates.
  3. Launch medicines. +24% and +18%. Change: ALTUVIIIO or Ayvakit decelerating below +20%.
  4. Currency. EUR/USD 1.16 flat. Every 0.05 is about EUR 1.07bn of sales and EUR 0.23 to 0.26 of EPS.
  5. Price. About -1.2% a year from 2027 plus the 2026 base effect. Change: Q4 2026 GTN line; 2027 PBM formulary decisions in atopic dermatitis.
  6. Cost discipline. R&D flat in 2027. Change: the strategy update re-accelerating R&D or the Regeneron alliance's registration-enabling studies starting earlier and larger.

For the third set (predictability): 2026 is almost certain within plus or minus 1%. 2027 revenue is predictable within about plus or minus 3%; 2027 EPS within about plus or minus 7%, with half of that width coming from currency and capital allocation, not operations. The drivers of the difference between the next four years and the last six are concentrated: Dupixent and launches supply about 80% of gross revenue growth; the Regeneron mechanics, price and the capital decision explain most of the margin and EPS path. For the fourth set (reflection in expectations): the operating view is close to consensus; the debate is whether consensus is "full" for 2027 (we think it is fair organically, but measured against a company-compiled consensus near EUR 9.2 it is beatable), and whether the market has priced a deal (consensus has not modelled one).

Margins/growth vs. past

C-53How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?F5033rd of 42full answer under 4c Financial profile
C-78

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

F5832nd of 42

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: there is plausible scope to lift the operating margin by about 2.5 to 3.5 points between 2025 and 2029 (to roughly 28% to 29%), coming from a few big areas, consistent with consensus and with peer precedent, and congruent with the strategy. Beyond 2029 the same levers reverse.

  • Big areas: (1) operating leverage on Dupixent and launch volume against a flat R&D base; (2) portfolio mix after exiting about EUR 1.3bn of low-margin mature products; (3) Amvuttra royalties (about EUR 1bn a year at the 30% tier) partly replacing the lost Regeneron reimbursement; (4) working capital (cash, not margin).
  • Peer precedents: Novartis lifted core operating margin from 36% (FY2023) to 40.1% (FY2025), two years early, after the Sandoz spin; AstraZeneca from about 31% to 34% while investing heavily; GSK targets 31% by late 2026. A 3-point improvement in four years for Sanofi is well within precedent.
  • Congruence: management's statement that margins improve every year to 2030 and the plan to keep R&D flat in 2027 are consistent; the only incongruence is the M&A pivot, which, if executed at scale, brings pre-launch costs and interest that dilute the margin and EPS path for two to three years.
C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

C+6410th of 42

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: it has not decelerated; it has accelerated to the fastest rate in two decades. But the headline overstates the engine (rebate releases), and the leading indicator of the engine's input force (Dupixent's share of new patients) is slowing. Deceleration of the rate is a 2027 event, already scheduled.

  • Rate versus Sanofi's normal: constant-currency growth of about 5% (2023), about 11% (2024 restated), 9.9% (2025) and low-teens in 2026 (Q2 +17.8%, Q3 +14.3%) against a long-run normal of roughly 2% to 4% (revenue was essentially flat in absolute terms from the 2004 Aventis merger to about 2020).
  • Engine: Dupixent +34.8% at constant rates in Q3 2026 including GTN releases; Morgan Stanley's underlying Q2 beat was about 11% rather than 15%. On our reading underlying Dupixent volume growth is running in the mid-20s percent, still very strong.
  • Input force: the type-2 market is still expanding (penetration about 20% in atopic dermatitis, single digits in COPD), but Dupixent's capture of new biologic-naive patients has fallen (about 80% to about 30% in a dermatology panel). Volume growth from the installed base continues for several years even as new-patient share falls, so this shows in revenue only gradually, first as rising rebates.
  • Warning signs to track: Dupixent growth below 10% (Redburn's stated threshold for "severe competitive problems"), Deutsche Bank's weekly prescription tracker, US net price per patient, and the Q4 2026 GTN line.

Forecasts and upgrades

8

Probably upgrades this financial year to

–not yet rated

Memo topic 7, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

–55

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Our assessment is, for the most part, already reflected. The gap between what we expect and what guidance, estimates and the narrative contain is small in size, mildly favourable in direction for the next two to three earnings events, and dominated by one item that is in the narrative but in nobody's numbers: the acquisition.

What is reflected. The operating forecasts for 2026 to 2028 lie between the consensus mean and median. The 2027 headwinds, the first-half weakness, the competitive erosion of new-patient share, the patent expiry and the likelihood of a large transaction are all articulated by the sell-side and, on the evidence of the price, accepted by investors. The valuation, 8.2 times 24-month-forward earnings, at the 9th percentile of five years and the 2nd of ten, reflects the negative part of our assessment in full.

What is not, in order of importance.

  1. The transaction is in the narrative and not in the estimates (unfavourable for estimates, uncertain for the shares). A completed purchase on the scale reported would cut consensus for 2028 by about 3% to 5%; a disciplined outcome, a smaller asset or a buyback, would do the opposite. The shares have begun to discount the first (-2.9% on the report). This cuts both ways for an investment case: the downward revision is foreseeable, but so is the relief if the strategy update in early December 2026 shows restraint.
  2. Guidance is more conservative than the narrative credits (favourable). Seven first guides in seven years were beaten by 2 to 7 points; our forecast for 2027 assumes the smallest beat on record. The first 2027 guide will look cautious in growth and be in line in euros, and the raise will probably come later in the year than usual.
  3. Gross-to-net releases are treated by everyone as one-off and have in fact recurred and grown for three years (favourable, moderate confidence). If they recur in 2027 even in part, sales and estimates rise by an amount no model contains. The annual report in February 2027 is the test.
  4. The consensus mean is held down by estimates that the 2026 result will force up (favourable, small, near-term).
  5. The quarterly shape of 2027 is known only in outline (neutral for the numbers, relevant for timing). The comfortable first quarter and the weak second quarter are a reason to expect the flow of revisions to be positive into April, at risk in July and positive again from October.

Read across to the return the model shows. The model's 18-month return of about 84% to 89% is almost wholly the guideline fair multiple of 13.59 times: 64.6 points come from the multiple, 8.5 from earnings and 5 to 11 from dividends. The fair multiple is set by Viewforth's guideline, and my part is only to say how it sits against this set's findings. It equals the 90th percentile of the stock's ten-year range and is above the highest multiple of the last five years (12.85 times); the stock has spent the last two years around 9 times. Everything in the third and fourth sets describes a company whose earnings for 18 months are predictable and already known, and whose multiple has not responded to a year of rising estimates because the market is pricing 2031 and the acquisition. On those findings I would describe the stock as turning up in earnings and estimates but not yet in valuation, and I would treat the return at the guideline multiple as the outcome if the terminal-value question were resolved favourably, not as the central expectation. For orientation, using the model's own earnings and one dividend to the end of 2027: at an unchanged multiple the return is about 14%; at the five-year median of 9.9 times about 35%; at the five-year low of 7.3 times about 1%. That asymmetry, a positive return even without any re-rating and a roughly flat one at the trough multiple, is in my view the fairest statement of what the fourth set supports: the predictable earnings and the dividend carry the investor while the question of the multiple is decided by events that begin in early December 2026.

What would change this judgement. Favourably: a strategy update that keeps the leverage guide, sets a return hurdle and includes a buyback; a 2026 annual report showing another large prior-period release with provisions still rising; a first-quarter 2027 with Dupixent growing faster than 17%. Unfavourably: a purchase at or above the reported scale financed by giving up the AA rating; a strong KT-621 result followed by evidence of deeper rebating in the 2028 formularies; US Dupixent prescriptions growing less than about 12%.

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

–62

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: on the operating business, raises are about four times as likely as cuts over the six earnings events to April 2028. The most probable sequence is: 2026 delivered at or above the top of its range in January 2027; a first 2027 guide that reads as cautious but in euros brackets the consensus mean; no change in April; a raise that comes later in the year than Sanofi's habit, October 2027 more likely than July; 2027 delivered above the first guide; and a 2028 guide at or just under consensus. The only probable route to lower guidance is a change of perimeter: a large acquisition completed during 2027 and restated into the guide. Mid-term, the Dupixent ambition is more likely to be raised than held, the vaccines ambition more likely to be cut than raised, and the leverage guide is the "target" most likely to be given up.

2023 Low single digit July (mid single digit) 5.4%
2024 Low single-digit decline Twice +4.1%
2025 Low double digit before buyback Sales raised in July 12.2% before buyback, 15% with it
2026 Sales high single digit, EPS "slightly faster" July, October Guided mid-teens

Seven years out of seven, delivery exceeded the first guide, by 2 to 7 points. Wherever the date is recorded, the first raise came with the half-year results in July and never with the first quarter. The October 2023 episode (a guide for 2024 set below consensus and a margin target withdrawn) is the one adverse precedent, and it was a decision to spend more, not a failure to deliver.

Event by event.

A cost catch-up after a third quarter in which R&D and selling costs ran 6% to 7% under consensus is the route to "within"

Across 2027 as a whole: at least one raise about 60%; no change and delivery in the upper half about 28%; an operating cut about 12%. Separately, about a 30% chance that guidance is restated lower in 2027 or early 2028 for a completed large acquisition (the guide excludes transactions until they close); the market would read that as a transaction effect, but it is how a lower number would most probably appear.

Why the January guide will probably be misread. Consensus growth for 2027 is 6.9% on the mean and 7.8% on the median, measured from consensus for 2026 (EUR 8.89 mean, EUR 9.00 median). A guide of roughly 5% to 7% will look one to two points short of that. But it will be applied to an actual 2026 that I expect to be EUR 9.05 or a little more, which is 1.8% above the consensus mean for 2026. In euros, 5% to 7% on EUR 9.05 is EUR 9.50 to EUR 9.68: the consensus mean at the bottom and the median at the top. The growth rate will look cautious; the level will be in line. This is the practical consequence of point 4 in the opening section, and it replaces the second set's idea of an easy bar near EUR 9.2.

What our own forecast implies about the guide. Our organic EUR 9.74 is growth of 7.6%. If management guides 5% to 7% and then beats by the smallest margin in its seven-year record (2 points), it delivers about 8%, which is our forecast. If it beats by its typical margin (4 to 6 points), it delivers EUR 9.95 to EUR 10.15, the top of the brokers' range. So either management will guide lower than I expect, or the forecasts of record are conservative against management's own habits. I think the truth is some of each, and it is the main reason I regard the guidance gap as favourable.

Drivers, in order of weight. For raises: Dupixent volume ahead of plan (COPD and urticaria; two years of beats); gross-to-net true-ups recurring; costs again under plan; royalty income; launch medicines. For reductions: escalation of US rebates in the 2028 contracting round or after a strong KT-621 result; vaccines (Merck's Enflonsia now inside the public Vaccines for Children programme, which covers about 45% of US infants; flu immunisation rates down 12% last season; the childhood-schedule litigation); a weaker Amvuttra royalty; the launch group slowing; and the costs of integrating an acquisition. Currency is guided separately and does not change the constant-rate guide, but it changes what is reported: at EUR 0.23 per 0.05, it can outweigh any raise.

How this compares with what is reflected. Brokers expect conservative framing from the sector in general (Morgan Stanley expects the same of AstraZeneca for 2027; Novartis has just lowered its sales growth target to 2031 to 5%). I found no broker spelling out the later-than-usual timing of the first raise in 2027 or the base effect that will make the January guide look weaker than it is.
