I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

–53

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: on operations, consensus for FY2027 and FY2028 is more likely to be held within about 2% than to move decisively either way, because our forecasts sit between the consensus mean and median. Beneath that flat average three things will happen. The mean will be pulled up over the next three months by a group of low and probably stale estimates; the high group will be trimmed around the half-year results in July 2027; and the first clean upgrade cycle begins only when the 2026 comparisons have passed, from October 2027 into 2028. The largest prospective revision is not operational: a completed large acquisition would cut consensus for 2028 by about 3% to 5% in one step, and a buyback would raise it by 4% to 8%; no broker's numbers contain either. For FY2029 consensus is more likely to be trimmed than raised.

Where consensus stands against our forecasts (business EPS, EUR, company share count).

FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029
FactSet median (brokers) 9.00 (18) 9.70 (19) 10.58 (18) 11.35 (12)
FactSet mean 8.89 9.50 10.37 11.28
Ours with the assumed acquisition 9.05 9.61 10.38 11.06
Ours without it 9.05 9.74 10.48 10.99
Ours without it, against median and mean +0.6% and +1.8% +0.4% and +2.5% -0.9% and +1.1% -3.2% and -2.6%

The mean is the figure the dashboard's revision measures follow (EUR 9.48 for FY2027). On that measure our operating view is 1% to 2.5% above consensus for FY2026 to FY2028; on the median it is level. For FY2029 we are about 3% below on both.

The structure of consensus matters more than its average. Brokers fall into two groups for FY2027. Of the fifteen whose estimates FactSet shows individually, seven lie between EUR 8.49 and EUR 9.17 and eight between EUR 9.51 and EUR 10.07, with nothing between. The low group is probably out of date for the year in progress: five brokers hold FY2026 estimates of EUR 8.52 to EUR 8.69, which, with about EUR 7.51 already reported for nine months, would require fourth-quarter EPS of EUR 1.0 to EUR 1.2 against about EUR 1.5 a year earlier. Nothing in guidance or in the run of results supports a fall of 20% to 35%. When the 2026 result is published, those brokers must raise 2026 by 4% to 6% and will probably carry part of that into 2027. That is a mechanical source of positive revision breadth unrelated to new information.

Event by event (operations only).

Period FY2027 consensus Net breadth of revisions Why
Now to 28 January 2027 Mean drifts up 0% to 1% Mildly positive Low group catches up; little news otherwise; the strategy update changes long-term assumptions more than 2027
28 January 2027 and the weeks after Mean flat to +1%; median flat to -1%; spread between brokers narrows Positive by count 2026 beats the mean by about 2%; the guide brackets the mean; brokers at EUR 9.9 to 10.1 are looking for growth of 10% to 12%, above any likely guide, and start to trim
April 2027 Flat Neutral to mildly positive A comfortable first quarter
July 2027 Mean flat to -1%; median down 1% to 2% Negative risk First-half EPS up only 3% to 4% leaves the high group needing second-half growth of 14% to 17% against our 8%. This is the one date at which I expect more cuts than raises
October 2027 to April 2028 Up 1% to 2% for FY2027; FY2028 held to slightly up Positive Comparisons clear; BOI growth of 8% to 9% in 2028 on an undisputed base; a guidance raise becomes likely

Over the 18 months, for the operating business: FY2027 consensus mean ends within 2% of today's EUR 9.50 about 60%, higher about 25%, lower about 15%. FY2028 (mean EUR 10.37): within 2% about 50%, higher 25%, lower 25%.

Three features of how this consensus moves.

  1. It moves in steps on results days. Of the 5% rise in FY2027 EPS estimates over twelve months, 4 points came in the last month, after the third-quarter results. Between results it is flat, because the items that surprise (gross-to-net releases, the timing of costs) are not observable in advance while the items that are observable (prescriptions) are already in the numbers.
  2. Sales upgrades reach EPS one for one. Over twelve months FY2027 sales estimates rose 5% and EPS estimates 5% (D-03). Further Dupixent beats will raise EPS estimates in proportion, not by more.
  3. Estimates have risen while conviction has not. The share of buy ratings on FactSet's definition is 31%, down 3 points in a month in which EPS estimates rose 4% and price targets 6%. Analysts are marking numbers up without recommending the shares more strongly, which is consistent with a multiple that has not responded (D-07).

The corporate step. Consensus debt falls every year (EUR 15.7bn, 12.9bn, 10.5bn), so it contains neither a large acquisition nor a buyback. On my probabilities (D-04): about 35% that a transaction of EUR 25bn or more is completed, cutting 2028 consensus by about 3% to 5% (interest of about EUR 1bn before tax, less the asset's early profit; Jefferies expects an Insmed purchase to become accretive to business EPS within two years, which means dilutive first); about 30% for a smaller transaction, cutting about 1%; about 15% for a buyback of EUR 5bn or more, adding about 4% to 8%. Brokers will not move until a transaction closes, so this revision is delayed as well as large. Because business EPS excludes the amortisation of what is bought, the cut on the sell-side's measure will understate the economic cost, which is why the share price reacted (-2.9% on the report) before any estimate did.

Eighteen months to three years out. FY2029 consensus (EUR 11.35 from twelve brokers; revenue from ten) is thinly based and about 3% above us. I expect it to be trimmed, about 50% likely against 35% held and 15% raised, as the events of 2029 come into view: AbbVie's long-acting zumilokibart, whose Phase III starts now and whose launch is targeted for 2029; the expiry of the tariff exemption in January 2029; the first effect of Alnylam's royalty-free successor on royalty income (launch targeted for 2028 in the smaller indication); and R&D resuming growth as the long-acting Regeneron antibodies enter registration studies. Morgan Stanley's base case of 5% revenue and 7% EPS growth a year from 2027 to 2030, and Bernstein's 5.9% a year to 2031, already sit below what FY2029 consensus implies.

Where I differ from the earlier sets. The first set called 2027 consensus "full" with the larger skew to the downside. On the mean, which is what the revision data measure, I find it slightly low for 2026 and 2027, and I expect the first move to be up, for the mechanical reason above. The first set's caution belongs to the median and to July 2027, not to the next three months.

11d

Current multiple

–not yet ratedMemo topic 11, : the Rater's score1 answer
D-0752
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

–52

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: on daily moves over the latest six months, 38% of Sanofi's price variance is explained by the peer group and 62% is its own; the two-year average is about 31% explained. The sensitivity to the sector has risen sharply, from a beta of 0.6 in the spring of 2026 to 1.1 now, the highest of the two years. My reading is that the recent change in the multiple has been mostly sector-driven, while its level, the discount to peers, is company-driven. Over the next 18 months the size of the discount will be decided by company events, and the direction of the price in any given month will increasingly be decided by the sector.

The evidence.

  • Latest six months (1 June to 23 November 2026): correlation with an equal-weighted basket of 33 European pharma and biotech peers 0.61; beta 1.10; share of variance explained 38%. Sanofi returned +13.2% and the basket +13.1%.
  • Two years of rolling six-month readings: the explained share has ranged from 18% to 60%, with a median of 29%. It peaked at 60% in the window ending April 2025, the US tariff and pricing shock that hit every drugmaker together. It was lowest, about 20%, in the windows from October 2025 to May 2026, when Sanofi's own news dominated (late-stage failures, the decision not to renew the chief executive, the new appointments). Since June 2026 it has climbed back to 38%.
  • Which peers. Sanofi moves most with GSK (correlation 0.62; GSK alone explains as much as the whole basket), then AstraZeneca (0.54), Roche (0.53), UCB (0.50) and Novartis (0.48). It moves hardly at all with the small and mid-sized names that fill the basket. Against a basket of large European drugmakers only, the explained share would be higher than 38%; I would put the truly industry-driven share of daily moves at about 40% to 50%. The closest companion is the other large, cheap, patent-cliff-facing European stock, which says the common factor is "cheap defensive pharma", not healthcare in general.

Separating the multiple from the price.

  • Over twelve months the multiple did not follow the sector. Sanofi's price rose 2.9% while consensus FY2027 EPS rose about 5%, so the forward multiple fell slightly, in a year when Novartis rose 24% and Roche 43% and both were re-rated. Rising estimates were not rewarded. The discount to peers (about 30% to 45% on brokers' figures; 8.2 times 24-month-forward earnings, at the 9th percentile of its own five years) widened for reasons that belong to Sanofi: the pipeline failures, the dated patent expiry, and the question of what management will buy.
  • Over six months the multiple rose with the sector. The price rose about 13% and estimates about 5%, so roughly 60% of the gain was multiple, and the basket rose by the same amount. The first set recorded that the sector's relative strength in October 2026 came entirely on days the wider market fell, that is, from money moving into defensive shares. Sanofi, at a beta of 1.1, has been a slightly geared way of owning that movement.
  • The single clearest company-specific move in the period went the other way: -2.9% on 19 November 2026, the day of the Insmed report.

What this means for the case. First, the recent recovery in the share price is weaker evidence of a re-rating than it looks: it is mostly the sector, and by the first set's account a reversible kind of sector move. A rotation back out of defensives would take Sanofi down by slightly more than its peers whatever its results. Second, the part that is Sanofi's own, the discount, has not yet started to close, and the events that could close or widen it are dated and near (the strategy update, a transaction, the 2027 guidance). Third, for predictability: the fundamental forecasts are predictable (D-04) but the share's response to them has been weak for a year, so the delivery of forecast earnings is not by itself a reason to expect the multiple to move. On the past year's evidence the multiple responds to the terminal-value question, not to the next 18 months of earnings.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

F4239th of 42

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector have typically generated outperformance and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

Judgement: in large-cap pharma, sustained share-price outperformance has come from a credible new product cycle that the market can see replacing the old one (earnings upgrades plus a lengthening growth runway), and underperformance from a dominant product's patent cliff without a visible replacement. Sanofi currently fits the second pattern on the long view and a short-term variant of the first ("worry clearing at a low multiple").

  • Outperformance precedents: AstraZeneca 2013 to 2024 (Soriot reinvested, cancelled buybacks, delivered pipeline; ten-year TSR 307% against 197% for peers); Lilly (GLP-1); Roche 2018 to 2020 (overcame the Avastin/Herceptin/Rituxan cliff covering half of sales by launching Ocrevus, Hemlibra, Tecentriq and Vabysmo); Novartis 2023 to 2025 (Sandoz spin, margin to 40%, two years ahead of plan).
  • Underperformance precedents: Novo Nordisk 2025 to 2026 (growth franchise faltered, -24% over twelve months); Novartis September 2026 (HORIZON and HARBOR failures removed USD 8.9bn of risked peak sales); Sanofi itself after the Lantus cliff (2015) and in October 2023 (margin target abandoned); Pfizer and Bristol-Myers before acquisitions; Bayer after Monsanto (large M&A destroying value).
  • Rules of thumb that apply: (1) the market starts discounting a cliff about five years ahead, so Sanofi (cliff in 2031) is in the discounting window now; (2) a stock at the bottom of its own multiple range with rising estimates re-rates only when the long-term worry is addressed (Sector Digest: "a known, capped cost earns a higher multiple than an open-ended one"); (3) a large acquisition by a cliff-facing company typically de-rates the acquirer on announcement unless the asset is commercial and clearly accretive; (4) gross-to-net-driven beats are "false friends" (Risks report's winning ACH profile).
  • Fit: near-term, Sanofi fits the "cheap, rising estimates, policy worry cleared" pattern that produced Novartis's and Roche's 2025 to 2026 runs (+24% and +43% over twelve months) and has not yet re-rated (+2.9%). Long-term, it fits the "dominant product cliff, thin replacement" pattern unless the bridge is bought well. The Roche precedent is the bull's template; the Sanofi-after-Lantus precedent is the bear's.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

A601st of 42

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: about 15% to 20% on plausible trough assumptions, and about 25% in a severe case; the starting multiple is already near its historical floor, which limits the downside compared with almost any other large-cap pharma stock.

  • Starting point: EUR 83.79; 24-month-forward P/E 8.16x (five-year range 7.28x to 12.85x, median 9.86x; 9th percentile of five years; 2nd percentile of ten years on the dashboard); current P/E 8.5x; dividend yield about 4.9%.
  • Trough multiple: the five-year low of about 7.3x (the market's own floor); Jefferies' tactical downside uses 7x 2027 earnings (EUR 70); Morgan Stanley's bear case EUR 71 (9x 2026 on a lower EPS).
  • Arithmetic on FY2027 consensus EPS of EUR 9.70: at 7.3x, EUR 71 (-15%); at 7.0x, EUR 68 (-19%). If FY2027 EPS is cut 5% (GTN normalisation, euro strength or acquisition dilution) to about EUR 9.2: at 7.3x, EUR 67 (-20%). Severe case (EPS EUR 8.8, 7x): EUR 62 (-26%).
  • Support: the dividend (a yield of about 6.5% at EUR 65), free cash flow yield of about 8.7% on 2027 consensus, and the option to buy back stock; Redburn's zero-pipeline DCF of EUR 98 suggests the current price already assumes the pipeline contributes little.
  • Upside for symmetry: a return to the five-year median of about 9.9x on EUR 9.70 is about EUR 96 (+14%), close to the consensus target (mean EUR 97, median EUR 99.5).

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

F4042nd of 42

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The market narrative for the next 18 months will be written by capital allocation (the strategy update and any acquisition) and by the 2027 earnings framing, not by Sanofi's own pipeline, which offers few novel, well-founded steps before 2029. The cheap valuation means a disciplined capital plan could re-rate the shares; an expensive deal could extend the de-rating. For the second-set Analyst, this means the operating model should be built to accept a capital-allocation scenario switch, because that switch, more than any trial, will drive the 2027 to 2029 EPS path.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–68

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: highly congruent in structure and in arithmetic. The two sets tell one story, each part of which explains the next, and the numbers reconcile. They jar in six places. Four I can settle on the evidence, one has been overtaken by events, and one is open: the size of the acquisition against management's own leverage guide. That open point is also the largest source of unpredictability in the forecasts, so the incongruence is not a flaw in the earlier work; it is a real feature of the company at this date.

Why the story holds together. Each foundational finding of the first set reappears in the second set as a specific number, and the direction always matches:

  • Under-penetrated chronic disease and a patient base that accumulates (first set, C-01 and C-47) become Dupixent growth of +14% in 2027 and +8% in 2028 despite the share of new patients falling from 80% to 90% to 30% to 40% (second set, P-05). The AlphaSense report on US prescriptions confirms both halves at once: total and new-to-brand prescriptions in atopic dermatitis are still rising (Jefferies, 30 October 2026) while share of new starts falls.
  • The explanation of the low margin as a partnership structure, not inefficiency (C-53), becomes the "half-shared" leverage of about 45 cents kept per incremental Dupixent euro and a margin gain of only about 0.5 point a year (P-01). Regeneron's accounts corroborate it: its share of profit rose to 38% of combined sales in the third quarter of 2026 from 29%.
  • No pricing power and accounting-driven price in 2026 (C-17, C-45) become a price contribution of about -1.2 points a year plus a base effect in 2027 (P-04).
  • A conservative, repeatedly raised annual guidance record but a poor record on mid-term targets (C-61, C-70) becomes high weight on 2026 and 2027 and a discount on 2030 (P-06).
  • A failed organic pipeline and a deal-oriented finance chief (C-11, C-18, C-86) become the capital-allocation switch (P-02).

The arithmetic is internally consistent. I checked three things. The second set's 2026-to-2027 BOI bridge sums to its result (EUR 14.24bn, less EUR 1.63bn of headwinds, plus EUR 2.59bn from Dupixent, launches and royalties, less EUR 0.07bn of other items, is EUR 15.13bn). Its half-year inputs sum to its year (first-half 2027 at 31.0% and second-half at 34.3% give 32.8% for the year). And the Financial Analyst's model, built independently from the inputs, reproduces the second set's net income to within 0.2% in every year. A forecast that survives independent recalculation is more predictable than one that does not.

Where the sets jar, and how I resolve each.

  1. What the fourth quarter of 2026 implies (first set: a 9% fall; second set: roughly flat). The second set is right; the difference is the share count, and the company's 1,199m settles it. The same error then re-entered through the model in the opposite direction (previous section).
  2. Whether 2027 consensus is "full with a negative skew" (first set, C-49) or "fair and close to symmetrical" (second set, P-09). They are the same arithmetic in different words. Growth of 7.6% from EUR 9.05 to the second set's organic EUR 9.74 is the same thing as the first set's 11% to 12% from a "cleaned" base of about EUR 8.65. The second set then shows where that growth comes from line by line. My own restatement of the second set's bridge makes the requirement concrete: excluding the non-recurring items in both years, disposals and the acquisition, 2027 needs BOI to grow about 17% on sales growth of about 8%, roughly two times; 2026 delivered about the same ratio on the same basis. So 2027 requires a second consecutive year of the 2026 pattern. That is "full" in the sense that nothing may slip, and "fair" in the sense that nothing new has to happen. The recurring pattern of gross-to-net releases (point 5 of the opening section) also weakens the first set's idea that a clean base can be identified at all.
  3. Working capital (first set: an inflow of EUR 0.5bn to 1bn; second set: an outflow of EUR 0.2bn to 0.75bn). The second set is right in direction: the first half of 2026 showed an outflow and net working capital at 27.7% of sales against 26.0% a year earlier. I add a caution: the second set's 24.5% at end-2026 would be the lowest year-end ratio in six years (the range is 24.6% to 25.8%), and each point is about EUR 0.5bn of free cash flow. This is a cash-flow risk of that size in FY2026, not an earnings risk; the fall of EUR 3.3bn in net debt during the third quarter supports the second set, so I left the input.
  4. What the mature-medicines disposal costs (first set: 1% to 2% of 2027 EPS; second set: EUR 0.25bn of BOI in 2027). The newer AlphaSense report gives Deutsche Bank's figure of about EUR 440m of EBITDA on the EUR 1.2bn of sales going to Cheplapharm, and Sanofi's rule that the results of associates not run as part of the business are excluded from business EPS. So at full run-rate in 2028 the disposal removes about EUR 0.45bn to 0.47bn of BOI including Medley (about 3% of business EPS), and what Sanofi receives in exchange (a 26.4% stake valued at EUR 1.36bn, EUR 390m of cash, deferred inventory payments, contingent preferred dividends and earn-outs) arrives almost entirely outside business EPS. Both earlier sets understated this a little. The second set's total over 2027 and 2028 is within about EUR 0.05bn of mine, with slightly too little in 2027 (perhaps EUR 0.1bn, 0.7% of EPS), which I treat as a small downside skew and not as an input change; its inclusion of half the preferred dividends in business earnings from 2028 I have removed.
  5. The size of the acquisition (first set: EUR 10bn to 25bn at 1.5 to 2.0 times leverage; second set: EUR 10bn at 1.16 times, because management guides to about 1 time). The second set's reasoning was sound on what management has said. The Insmed report, if accurate, describes a transaction three times that size at about 2.3 to 2.5 times, and Bernstein has floated relaxing the rating guardrail from AA to A. Management's stated discipline and its reported intention cannot both be true. This is unresolved until the strategy update in early December or a transaction announcement. I keep the second set's EUR 10bn as the central input because it is close to the probability-weighted outcome (D-04), and I state the alternatives explicitly there.
  6. Overtaken. The first set counted the Nurix oral STAT6 degrader as a partial hedge (C-36, C-20); it has been terminated. This does not touch the forecasts to 2028 but removes one of the first set's few positives for the years after 2029.

One thing that is congruent but cannot be tested. The second set's "single most important insight", that launch medicines supply more BOI growth than Dupixent from 2027 (about EUR 3.6bn against EUR 2.5bn to 2030), follows correctly from its own estimate that Sanofi keeps about 80 cents of each launch euro against 45 cents of each Dupixent euro. It sits a little awkwardly beside the first set's wholly Dupixent-centred account, and the 80 cents is an estimate with no disclosure behind it. At 65 cents, 2027 BOI would be about EUR 0.19bn lower (1.2% of EPS). The memo should present it as a reasoned estimate.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

–50

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: comprehensively. Almost every dynamic in our assessment is articulated by the sell-side, most of them by several houses and for many months, and the negative ones are the best known of all. The patent expiry, the failed pipeline, the falling share of new patients, the 2027 headwinds and the prospect of a large acquisition are the consensus account of Sanofi, and they are in a multiple at the 2nd percentile of ten years. What the narrative lacks is small and mostly a matter of precision: the quarterly shape of 2027, the one-for-one passage of sales into earnings, the fact that the launch medicines and not Dupixent supply the larger share of profit growth, and, the only point of real substance, that favourable gross-to-net adjustments recur every year. I have no direct evidence of investor commentary; what can be inferred from prices and positioning is a market that knows the story, does not dispute the next 18 months, and is waiting for the acquisition.

The evidence base and its limit. The AlphaSense report claims full coverage but in fact draws on Morgan Stanley, Jefferies, Rothschild & Co Redburn, Deutsche Bank and Bernstein, five of about nineteen contributing brokers, though the five most widely read. It found nothing from investors themselves. The table below is therefore sell-side; investors follow after it.

Dynamic in our assessment How well known on the sell-side Since Is our view different?
The 2027 step-down (gross-to-net, Sarclisa reversal, end of reimbursement, EUR 1.3bn of disposals) Very well known; each item quantified by management and by at least three brokers July to October 2026 No. Morgan Stanley even argues the step-down may be shallower than feared
That it is concentrated in the first half of 2027 Known in outline (Deutsche Bank expects "a visible deceleration in the first half") November 2026 In precision only: no broker in the evidence sets out the quarters, a comfortable first quarter and a second quarter in which EPS may fall
Dupixent's falling share of new patients beside rising total volume Very well known (Deutsche Bank's dermatologist panel; Jefferies; Morgan Stanley) September 2026 No
Sanofi keeps about half of each incremental Dupixent euro The contract terms are universally known; Morgan Stanley and Redburn note that partner payments rise with sales Years In consequence only: the 45 cents is not quantified anywhere I found, nor the observation that sales upgrades have reached EPS one for one
Launch medicines supply more of the profit growth than Dupixent One house (Redburn: launches "must supply a disproportionate share of incremental profits") November 2026 Yes, in emphasis; it rests on our own estimate of launch margins
Price, not cost, limits the margin Partly: management's "slightly negative" price is widely repeated; brokers name the Regeneron share as the limit October 2026 Yes, in emphasis: that one point of price is about 2.5% of EPS and outweighs a point of volume is not said
Gross-to-net releases are non-recurring Universally asserted July 2026 Yes. The annual series (EUR 155m, 282m, 667m) and peers' experience say they recur. No broker in the evidence cites it
A large acquisition is probable; its size and financing Very well known and the most discussed topic of November 2026: Jefferies ("One deal is all it takes", 6 August; "Drumroll for a deal", 2 November; a note and a client call on Insmed, 19 to 23 November); Bloomberg, 19 November; Redburn's leverage arithmetic; Bernstein on the rating August 2026 No in the narrative. Yes in the numbers: no estimate includes it
A buyback at this multiple would add far more to EPS Known (Jefferies: "substantial double-digit" accretion) but dismissed, because it does not address 2031 2026 No
The gap between business and IFRS earnings; restructuring every year Known, old and accepted (Deutsche Bank's annual review of earnings quality) Years No; not a live debate
The rising dollar share and currency sensitivity Known; the company publishes the sensitivity. The expiry of hedges during 2027 is rarely mentioned Years Little
Erosion after 2031 and the timing of long-acting successors The central bear case, modelled in detail (Redburn: ten biosimilars at discounts of 60% to 80%; Dupixent near half of operating profit) Years No
The Amvuttra royalty and its royalty-free successor Known (Morgan Stanley warned in September 2025 that the royalty could be short-lived); followed mainly by Alnylam's analysts 2025 No
Vaccines: Beyfortus against Enflonsia, US policy Well known; consensus for Beyfortus clusters tightly (EUR 1.8bn for 2026) 2025 No

What investors, as distinct from analysts, appear to think. I can only infer it. The shares rose 2.9% in twelve months in which estimates rose 5% and comparable peers rose 24% to 43%, so investors have not paid for delivery. Short interest is 1.2% of the free float, twice the peer median and rising through 2026, and the put-to-call ratio is about two (first set), so a meaningful group is positioned for a fall or is hedging one. The largest single-day move of the autumn attributable to Sanofi alone was the 2.9% fall on the Insmed report. A former Sanofi director describes investors as in a "show me" state, and Morgan Stanley calls the near-term story "catalyst-light" (7 October 2026). All of this fits a market that accepts the next 18 months of earnings as broadly known, attaches little value to them, and will set the price by what management buys and what it pays. I could not test whether investors share the precise points in the table; the memo should say so plainly.

What follows from a narrative this complete.

  • There is little left to discover on the negative side inside 18 months. The first-half trough, the disposals and the reimbursement loss are known by date. The second-quarter 2027 result could still disappoint in presentation, but it would be a known weakness arriving on schedule.
  • The underappreciated items are modest and mostly favourable for near-term numbers: recurring gross-to-net releases, a low group of estimates that must rise, and a management that has beaten its first guide seven years running.
  • The one large unknown is known to be unknown. Everyone expects a transaction; no one can price it. That is why the narrative is complete and the shares still cheap.
  • For 18 months to three years out, the narrative is at its most developed and most negative (the years 2029 to 2033). Our assessment agrees with it. The only elements that could turn it positive are outside Sanofi's present disclosure: clinical data on the long-acting Regeneron antibodies (registration studies from late 2027 or early 2028), the outcome of method-of-use patent challenges, and the quality of what is bought.

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 20 November 2026, 08:11 to 08:29 UK, Opus, ricardinho login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Sanofi (SAN-FR): First set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 20 November 2026 for the Investment Analyst answering the second set (the operating and financial model).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Before you start: five things about the evidence that change how you should read it

  1. The latest Earnings Review covers the third quarter of 2026 (reported 30 October 2026), not the period to June 2026. The script could not confirm its date; the content confirms it (Q3 2026 net sales EUR 14,444m, business EPS EUR 3.55, guidance raised on 30 October 2026). It is therefore more recent than FactSet's last reported period (H1 2026, because Sanofi publishes full financial statements half-yearly). The file labelled "the earlier Earnings Review (2025, quarter 1)" is a hybrid: its executive summary and sections 3, 7, 8 and 9 describe the Q2 2026 print (July 2026), while its financial tables and sell-side sections describe Q1 2025. Treat it as two documents.
  2. Use the brokers' basis for every growth calculation. Business EPS (the company's adjusted figure, which consensus tracks) was EUR 8.11 (FY2023, including the consumer business Opella), EUR 7.12 (FY2024, restated without Opella), EUR 7.83 (FY2025). FactSet's restated revenue history (FY2025 EUR 43,626m) differs from the estimates-basis actual (EUR 45,507m) by 4.3%; consensus growth must be computed from EUR 45,507m. Reported (IFRS) EPS of EUR 4.26 in FY2025 is not comparable with anything the sell-side forecasts.
  3. Three readings of sell-side positioning disagree. FactSet shows a 31% buy share and a mean rating of "Overweight 1.58"; Refinitiv (7 October 2026, via Morgan Stanley) shows 58% Overweight, 38% Equal-weight, 4% Underweight; Morgan Stanley's own count (August 2026) showed 50/50. The difference is definitional (FactSet's "buy" is narrower). Do not build a sentiment argument on any one of them.
  4. The Risks report (8 August 2026) predates the Q3 print and the October Regeneron deal. Its "False Friend" verdict was written before the Q3 beat; Q3 strengthened rather than refuted it, because roughly one third to one half of the Q3 sales beat was again gross-to-net (rebate accrual) adjustments.
  5. Roche is missing from the peer table (its FactSet block failed); use the Roche master digest for qualitative comparison only.

The central judgement in one paragraph (read this before the answers)

Sanofi is a company whose next 18 months are unusually visible and strong, and whose years four to six contain the single largest product cliff in large-cap European pharma. Dupixent (about EUR 20bn annualised, roughly 40% of group sales and, on our reading of the margin structure, more than half of business operating income) drives low-teens sales growth and mid-teens EPS growth in 2026, then a deceleration to roughly 6% sales and 8% EPS growth in 2027 to 2029 as three 2026 tailwinds reverse (non-recurring US gross-to-net releases; the last year of Regeneron development-cost reimbursement; divested mature sales). The US composition-of-matter patent expires in March 2031 and brokers model roughly EUR 20bn of a EUR 25bn base at risk within three years. The internal pipeline that was meant to bridge that gap (amlitelimab, itepekimab, tolebrutinib, balinatunfib, riliprubart) has failed or been cut in 2025 and 2026; management now openly says existing assets are insufficient and the bridge must be bought. The shares (EUR 83.79, 8.2x 24-month-forward P/E, 9th percentile of five years and 2nd percentile of ten) price a large part of that cliff already. For the modelling Analyst the three things that matter most are: (a) model 2027 off a cleaned 2026 base, not the reported one; (b) treat large acquisitions as the probable, not the possible, path, with their dilution and leverage; (c) let the US dollar, now 60% of quarterly sales, be an explicit input, not a footnote.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Sanofi SA (SAN-FR, Euronext Paris), a global biopharmaceutical company reporting a single Biopharma segment since deconsolidating its consumer health business Opella in April 2025, with franchises in immunology (Dupixent, co-developed and profit-shared with Regeneron), rare diseases and haemophilia (ALTUVIIIO, Ayvakit, Qfitlia), neurology, vaccines (influenza, paediatric combinations, Beyfortus with AstraZeneca and Sobi) and declining general medicines (Lantus, Toujeo, Lovenox, Plavix).

Request: T-P1

Peers: AstraZeneca (European large-cap with rising core operating margin while R&D stays above 20% of sales); Novartis (core operating margin moved from 36% in FY2023 to 40.1% in FY2025 after the Sandoz separation, and now faces Entresto generic erosion and Avidity integration dilution); GSK (similar margin level, large vaccines business, restructuring history); Roche (offset the 2018 to 2020 loss of exclusivity of Avastin, Herceptin and Rituxan; centralised cost programme); AbbVie (managed margins through the Humira loss of exclusivity from 2023); Bristol Myers Squibb and Pfizer (large announced cost-reduction programmes against patent expiries); Regeneron (the other side of the Dupixent profit share); Cheplapharm (private buyer of mature brands, the counterparty for Sanofi's September 2026 mature-medicines transfer). Inputs: What share of Sanofi's cost base (cost of sales, R&D, SG&A, other operating expenses including the Regeneron profit share) behaves as fixed versus variable with sales, and how the Regeneron collaboration profit-share expense scales with Dupixent sales in the US (equal split) and outside the US (sliding scale of 55% to 65%)? How did Sanofi's business operating margin move through the Lantus loss of exclusivity (2015 onward) and through the Dupixent ramp (2018 to 2026), with dated figures? What did Sanofi's cost programmes since 2019 (including the "Play to Win" programme and the October 2023 reinvestment decision that withdrew the 32% 2025 margin target) promise and deliver, over what period, and what restructuring expense was booked each year from 2019 to 2026 (company disclosure: EUR 1,396m in FY2024; EUR 563m in H1 2026)? Are Sanofi's restructuring costs recurring in practice, and how do they compare with peers (Deutsche Bank, 27 April 2026: Sanofi's cash restructuring was about 10% of FY2025 free cash flow against 1% to 4% for other large European peers)? What does the company's stated plan to hold R&D expense flat in 2027 and then grow it in line with sales (Deutsche Bank, 18 November 2026, after meeting the head of R&D) imply historically for pipeline output at companies that held R&D flat? What did the end of Regeneron development-cost reimbursement (company guidance: about EUR 300m of business operating income in 2026 and about EUR 800m in 2027) and the September 2026 Cheplapharm and Medley disposals (company guidance: about EUR 1.3bn less 2027 sales) do to the cost base and gross margin? For AbbVie, Roche and Novartis, how did operating margins and absolute cost lines behave in the three years after a product representing a large share of group profit lost exclusivity, and which costs were cut, how fast, and which could not be? Focus: How much operating leverage Sanofi can still extract between 2026 and 2030 while the Regeneron profit share rises with Dupixent, and how far the cost base can realistically flex down when Dupixent loses US exclusivity from March 2031. Whether restructuring should be treated as a recurring operating cost.

Request: T-P2

Peers: AbbVie (Humira gross-to-net and biosimilar erosion from 2023; Skyrizi and Rinvoq pricing; zumilokibart from the Apogee acquisition); Eli Lilly (Ebglyss launch pricing and rebating against Dupixent); Galderma (Nemluvio); Johnson & Johnson (Stelara biosimilar erosion from 2025); Novartis (Entresto US generic erosion; Cosentyx selected for Medicare negotiation); Novo Nordisk (insulin price erosion); Merck & Co (Enflonsia pricing against Beyfortus); GSK and Pfizer (vaccine pricing in US and tender markets); Regeneron (Dupixent net price disclosures from the partner side); Amgen (Medicare negotiation experience). Operating conditions: (1) US commercial-channel competition in type-2 inflammatory disease as rival biologics (Ebglyss, Nemluvio) and later long-acting antibodies and oral agents buy formulary position; (2) US government channels after the December 2025 Sanofi agreement with the US administration (Medicaid prices aligned with other high-income nations, TrumpRx), Medicare Part D redesign, and the CMS rule of November 2026 grouping subcutaneous and intravenous formulations for negotiation; (3) European cost containment (Germany's 2027 savings proposals and mandatory rebate rising to 15.5% in January 2027; EU pharmaceutical legislation and joint clinical assessment); (4) China national reimbursement listing and volume-based procurement; (5) vaccine markets with public tenders, US vaccine-policy changes and new competitors; (6) the post-exclusivity period for a biologic when pharmacy benefit managers own private-label biosimilar vehicles (CVS Cordavis, Cigna Quallent, Optum Nuvaila); (7) large currency moves, given Sanofi's US dollar share of sales (company disclosure: 60.4% in Q3 2026). Inputs: What is the history of Dupixent US net price and gross-to-net deductions from 2020 to 2026, and how large and how frequent were gross-to-net accrual releases (Morgan Stanley, 30 July 2026: EUR 100m to 200m benefit to Dupixent in Q2 2026; Jefferies, 30 October 2026: about EUR 300m of one-off US pricing adjustments in Q3 2026 across Dupixent, Lantus and Toujeo)? Did similar releases at peers recur or reverse in following years? What did Sanofi's CFO say on the Q3 2026 call about pricing returning to a slightly negative contribution in 2027, and how has Sanofi's annual price contribution behaved historically? How are rebates in US atopic dermatitis and asthma biologics changing as Ebglyss and Nemluvio gain first-line share (Deutsche Bank expert panel, September 2026, on Dupixent's share of biologic-naive starts)? What is the expected net-price and share trajectory for a leading biologic after US loss of exclusivity under the private-label model, using Humira (2023 onward), Stelara (2025 onward) and Entresto (IIFL Research: about 96% to 97% of US sacubitril share lost within one year) as precedents? How has Beyfortus pricing held against Enflonsia and maternal RSV vaccines, and how have influenza and pneumococcal vaccine prices behaved in recent seasons? How much of Dupixent's US business sits in commercial versus government channels, and when does it become eligible for Medicare negotiation? How did the Medicaid price alignment and the 78% Lantus list-price cut of January 2024 affect reported net sales? Focus: Whether Sanofi's 2026 price contribution was one-off accounting rather than pricing power, what Dupixent's US net price is likely to do from 2027 to 2030 as competition intensifies, and how steep the post-2031 erosion curve could be under today's payer structure.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Several possible self-reinforcing and self-limiting dynamics (the Regeneron profit share, an accumulating chronic patient base, repeated restructuring, and acquisition-led pipeline replacement) could shape Sanofi's margins and earnings between 2027 and 2030, and the report should test which of them are operating. Peers: AstraZeneca (reinvestment-led loop that rebuilt the pipeline after 2012); Roche (replacement of a legacy cliff by new launches, 2018 to 2020); Novartis (bolt-on acquisition loop and the September 2026 failures of acquired and partnered assets); Bristol Myers Squibb and Pfizer (acquisition-led replacement of patent-cliff revenue); Regeneron (partner whose share of Dupixent economics rises with sales); AbbVie (built Skyrizi and Rinvoq before the Humira cliff). Inputs: Start from these possible dynamics: (1) Dupixent sales growth raises the Regeneron profit-share expense and the US dollar share of sales, limiting operating leverage and raising currency sensitivity (balancing); (2) chronic patients accumulate on therapy, so volume grows even as new-patient share falls, while competitors' consumer advertising enlarges the treated market (reinforcing); (3) late-stage pipeline failures increase reliance on external late-stage and commercial acquisitions, which raise acquisition prices, lower returns on capital and the valuation multiple, and narrow future options (reinforcing negative); (4) repeated reorganisations raise attrition and damage launch execution, which leads to further restructuring (reinforcing negative; evidence in expert calls from 2025 and 2026 on the US specialty organisation and the Tzield relaunch); (5) holding R&D flat raises near-term margins but thins the replacement pipeline (balancing over time); (6) a low share-price multiple makes buybacks highly accretive but also makes share-funded acquisitions unattractive, pushing toward debt-funded deals; (7) the expanded Regeneron alliance (October 2026) re-using the Dupixent sales force and payer relationships for next-generation long-acting antibodies (reinforcing if the successors arrive before biosimilars). For each loop: the evidence it is operating now, dated; precedents at the named peers; and what would strengthen, stall or reverse it. Focus: Which of these loops will most move Sanofi's operating margin and earnings per share between 2027 and 2030, and whether the acquisition-led replacement loop has historically created or destroyed value at large pharma companies facing a single-product cliff.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: In 2027 several company-disclosed items reverse at once (gross-to-net releases, Regeneron reimbursement, divested sales), while royalties, associates, financing and currency move independently, so the earnings-per-share bridge needs each input examined separately. Peers: Novartis (Avidity financing cost and margin dilution guidance; currency mismatch between Swiss franc costs and dollar sales); AstraZeneca (capitalised upfront payments; tax rate effects on core EPS); GSK (associate and joint-venture accounting effects on the gap between core earnings and cash); Roche (unhedged translation exposure); Regeneron (partner disclosures on Dupixent profit share); Alnylam (Amvuttra sales that drive Sanofi's royalty). Inputs: (1) The scale and recurrence of US gross-to-net releases in Sanofi's 2026 quarters and whether any reversal is expected; (2) the Regeneron economics: profit-share expense trend (Regeneron 10-Q, Q3 2026: its share of collaboration profits rose to USD 2,574.9m from USD 1,455.5m), the end of development-cost reimbursement after full repayment of the development balance in Q2 2026, and the terms of the October 2026 alliance expansion (USD 1bn upfront, up to USD 7bn milestones, 50:50 profit split); (3) Amvuttra royalties: the tiered rate reaching 30% on annual sales above USD 1.5bn, Alnylam's Amvuttra sales trajectory, and cannibalisation risk from nucresiran, which carries no royalty to Sanofi; (4) Sobi's US Beyfortus royalty steps from 25% toward 30% to 35% by 2028; (5) equity-accounted income from the retained 48.2% Opella stake and the 26.4% Cheplapharm stake, and the deferred Cheplapharm payments from 2028; (6) the business tax rate after the Opella separation (Morgan Stanley: about 20% from 21%) and any Pillar Two effects; (7) currency: Sanofi's disclosed sensitivity (about EUR 1,069m of sales and EUR 0.23 of business EPS for a 0.05 USD/EUR move) and its hedging horizon; (8) interest cost and leverage under acquisition scenarios consistent with Sanofi's stated capacity and AA rating, and how business EPS treats acquired-intangible amortisation and capitalised upfront payments; (9) share count: buybacks completed in 2025 and 2026 and any new programme; (10) restructuring and amortisation excluded from business EPS, and how sell-side brokers treat them in their forecasts. Focus: How the 2026 to 2027 bridge in business EPS is built line by line, which inputs management guides versus which the sell-side must assume, and how far apart the broker forecasts for 2027 are and why.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 20 November 2026, 12:46 to 13:03 UK, Opus, ricardinho login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Sanofi (SAN-FR): Second set of questions, the operating and financial model

Written 20 November 2026 for the Investment Analyst answering the third and fourth sets (predictability of the forecasts; how far they are reflected in guidance, consensus and narrative).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Before you start: six things about the basis and the evidence that change how you should read the numbers

  1. Three revenue bases exist and they differ by about 4%. Our model inputs use the company's net sales (FY2025 EUR 43,626m, which is also FactSet's restated "fundamentals" revenue). The sell-side revenue consensus (FY2025 actual EUR 45,507m) is net sales plus "other revenues" (about EUR 1.9bn, mostly the US third-party vaccine distribution business VaxServe, which earns a low margin). Growth rates are almost identical on either base; margins are not. Every margin below is on net sales unless stated; multiply by about 0.96 to put it on the brokers' revenue base.
  2. Our EBITDA is business operating income (BOI) plus depreciation and amortisation excluding acquired-intangible amortisation. Sanofi's BOI is after the Regeneron profit share (booked in other operating expenses), after royalty income (Amvuttra), after share of associates and after minorities. FactSet's history EBITDA (FY2025 EUR 17,094m, 39.2%) adds back acquired-intangible amortisation and is not comparable. Consensus EBITDA is unreliable as an anchor: brokers with almost identical FY2026 EPS (about EUR 9.0) submit EBITDA between EUR 14.2bn and EUR 17.3bn, so definitions differ. Business EPS is the only consensus line that can be trusted as like for like.
  3. The company's share count for business EPS is about 1,200m, not the 1,212.6m FactSet diluted figure. Q3 2026 business net income (EUR 9,020m nine-month less EUR 4,765m first half = EUR 4,255m) divided by the reported EUR 3.55 gives about 1,199m. On that basis nine-month 2026 business EPS is about EUR 7.51, so the FY2026 consensus of EUR 9.00 implies a fourth quarter of about EUR 1.49. I disagree with the first set's "at least EUR 7.6" and its conclusion that Q4 is implied 9% down: on the correct share count the implied Q4 is roughly flat on the prior year, which is still undemanding (sales growing about 10%) but less so than it looked, because Q4 2026 carries roughly EUR 250m of lost Regeneron development reimbursement that Q4 2025 did not.
  4. FactSet's historical "free cash flow" line deducts dividends. FY2025: operating cash flow 10,750 less fixed-asset capex 1,762 less dividends 4,772 = 4,216, exactly the figure shown (the same identity holds for FY2023 and FY2024). The block's footnote and the first set (C-52, C-67: "EUR 2.6bn to 4.5bn on FactSet's narrow basis") read it as pre-dividend. Pre-dividend FCF on FactSet's lines was about EUR 9.0bn in FY2025 before intangible capex and about EUR 7.1bn after it. The consensus FCF line (EUR 7.6bn, 8.8bn, 10.4bn, 12.2bn) is the brokers' own definition and is pre-dividend.
  5. Broker names in the financials block's per-broker table are scrambled. The rows labelled "Barclays" and "Rothschild & Co Redburn" carry identical numbers, as do "Jefferies" and "Bernstein Research", and an unnamed row and "Berenberg"; AlphaSense separately quotes Redburn at EUR 9.33 and Bernstein at EUR 9.96 for FY2027. Use the distribution (FY2027 EPS EUR 8.49 to 10.07; median 9.70; mean 9.50), not the names. The first set's attribution of EUR 10.07 to "Jefferies and Berenberg" may be wrong.
  6. "Consensus" for FY2027 EPS depends on the source and the date: FactSet median EUR 9.70, FactSet mean EUR 9.50 (dashboard EUR 9.48), and the consensus quoted by Redburn and Morgan Stanley in early November (EUR 9.16 to 9.18, probably company-compiled or Visible Alpha, and pre-dating the post-Q3 upgrades of about 4%). The bar the market will judge January's 2027 framing against is therefore somewhere between EUR 9.2 and 9.7, and the Consumer should establish which.

Our base case in one paragraph (read this first)

Revenue grows +11.5% in FY2026 (reported, net sales EUR 48.65bn), then +5.0%, +6.0%, +3.7% and +2.1% to FY2030 (EUR 57.3bn), including an assumed EUR 10bn late-stage or commercial acquisition in mid-2027 (organic growth +4.4%, +4.6%, +3.0%, +1.4%). BOI margin on net sales moves from about 27.7% (FY2025) to 29.3% (FY2026), 29.6%, 30.4%, 30.9% and 31.2%. Business EPS is EUR 9.05 (FY2026, +15.6%), 9.61 (+6.2%), 10.45 (+8.7%), 11.13 (+6.4%) and 11.52 (+3.5%); without the acquisition it would be about 9.74, 10.55, 11.06 and 11.30. Organically our view sits on top of consensus for 2027 and 2028 and about 2.5% below it for 2029; the acquisition we judge probable takes about 1% to 2% off 2027 and 2028, and nothing in consensus models it. The three things that matter most for the next Analyst: (1) the first half of 2027 is the trough (EPS growth about +3% to +4%), because almost every 2026-to-2027 headwind lands in it; (2) the operating leverage is real but half-shared, so margin rises about 0.5 point a year, not 1 point; (3) capital allocation is a switch worth about plus or minus 10% on 2028 EPS (a EUR 10bn buyback adds about 8%; a EUR 10bn acquisition takes off about 1%), larger than any operating uncertainty in the window.

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Basis of every input (read first). Revenue is the company's net sales, which is FactSet's fundamentals revenue (FY2025 EUR 43,626m; H1 2026 EUR 22,106m; H2 2025 EUR 23,737m). EBITDA is Sanofi's business operating income (BOI) plus depreciation and amortisation excluding acquired-intangible amortisation and impairments. Sanofi's BOI is after the Regeneron profit share (other operating expenses), after royalty income (Amvuttra), after the share of associates (Opella, Cheplapharm) and after minorities; the model's EBITDA line therefore carries associates and minorities, and they should not be added again. EPS is business EPS on about 1,200m weighted shares (the company's count, implied by Q3 2026: business net income EUR 4,255m over EUR 3.55). Currency: EUR/USD held at about 1.16 for 2027 to 2030. The inputs include an assumed EUR 10bn debt-funded late-stage or commercial acquisition in mid-2027 (acquisitions line; revenue and EBITDA contribution in the growth and margin inputs; interest in net financial items). If the model's revenue base is the brokers' basis (FY2025 EUR 45,507m, which adds about EUR 1.9bn of low-margin "other revenues"), multiply the EBITDA margins by about 0.96 (for example 32.3% becomes about 31.0%); the growth rates need no change.

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

Sanofi reports one segment (Biopharma) since deconsolidating Opella in April 2025; it discloses sales by franchise and product but no segment margins. Our franchise build (net sales, EUR bn, constant currency at about 1.16; 2026 split is our estimate from the quarterly disclosures):

Launch medicines (ALTUVIIIO, Ayvakit, Rezurock, Sarclisa, Qfitlia, Tzield, Infilbray) 5.2 6.45 7.6 8.7 9.7 about 0.80

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Revenue. FY2026: nine months EUR 36,550m (+11.9% reported); Q4 about EUR 12.1bn (+10%, vaccine deliveries shifted into Q4, no further gross-to-net release assumed). FY2027 +5.0% (organic +4.4%): Dupixent +14% (volume about +17% from an accumulating chronic base and COPD; net price about -2%; base effect of 2026 releases about -1.5 points); launches +24%; vaccines flat; general medicines -EUR 1.3bn of disposals plus -7% underlying; acquired asset +EUR 0.3bn. FY2028 +6.0% (organic +4.6%; a further EUR 0.3bn of disposal effect). FY2029 +3.7% and FY2030 +2.1%: Dupixent growth fades to 3% then 0% (long-acting and oral competitors from 2029), launches +14% and +12%.
  • Group net price. About +1 point in 2026 (US gross-to-net accrual releases of about EUR 0.45bn to 0.5bn in Q2 and Q3), about -1.2 points a year from 2027 plus about -1 point base effect in 2027 (Germany's mandatory rebate to 15.5% from January 2027; dermatology rebating; China; flu pricing).
  • EBITDA margin. BOI bridge 2026 to 2027 (EUR bn): from 14.24; Regeneron development reimbursement ends (-0.5, mostly in H1 2027); Q2 2026 Sarclisa provision reversal does not recur (-0.2); disposals (-0.25); general-medicines erosion (-0.37); Dupixent (+1.31); launches (+1.00); Amvuttra royalty at the 30% tier (+0.28); Sobi step (-0.03); R&D +1% (-0.08); SG&A flat; higher depreciation (-0.15); deal (-0.05): BOI 15.13. From 2028 the margin rises about 0.5 point a year: launches keep about 80 cents per euro, R&D grows with sales (+4%, +3%, +2%), SG&A held about flat in absolute terms, Amvuttra growth slows, nucresiran risk from about 2030.
  • Half-years. H2 2026 revenue +11.8% (EUR 26.5bn), EBITDA margin 33.3% (H1 2026 about 31.1%). H1 2027 revenue +6.1%, margin 31.0%: the reimbursement loss, the Sarclisa and Q2 2026 gross-to-net base effects and the start of the Cheplapharm transfer all land here; business EPS about EUR 4.11 (+3% to +4%). H2 2027 revenue +4.1% (Q3 2026 release base, full disposal effect, acquired asset), margin 34.3% (seasonally strong flu quarter).
  • D&A rises with US, Frankfurt, Beijing and Toronto capacity (USD 20bn of US investment through 2030).
  • Net financial items at about 3.3% on average company-basis net debt plus about EUR 0.1bn of other financial costs, less half of Cheplapharm's contingent preferred dividends (about EUR 202m a year from 2028). Net debt: EUR 11.7bn (end-2026), 19.4bn (end-2027, about 1.16 times EBITDA), 18.1bn, 15.4bn, 12.9bn.
  • Tax: about 21% business rate for 2026 (company guidance), 20.5% thereafter (post-Opella mix; Pillar Two floor; Morgan Stanley 20.2% for 2027).
  • Share count: about 4m shares a year issued under employee plans, offset by routine buybacks of about EUR 0.3bn a year; 2026 includes the EUR 1bn buyback completed in April 2026. No large buyback assumed (none announced).
  • Capital spending includes intangibles: the USD 1bn Regeneron upfront in Q4 2026 (capitalised), subsequent alliance milestones and in-licensing, and fixed-asset capex of about EUR 2.0bn to 2.5bn a year.
  • Working capital: 25.8% at end-2025 and 27.7% at June 2026 (seasonal peak); inventory reductions are offset by receivables growing with US sales and the Cheplapharm deferred inventory payments, so we hold 24.5% then 24.0%.
  • Acquisitions less disposals: 2026 Dynavax (EUR 1,645m) and smaller deals; 2027 the assumed EUR 10bn acquisition plus about EUR 1.5bn of bolt-ons, less Medley (sale to EMS; reported asking price at least USD 500m) and initial Cheplapharm cash; 2028 to 2030 bolt-ons of about EUR 2.5bn to 3bn a year less Cheplapharm deferred inventory payments.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

  • FY2026 guidance (30 October 2026): net sales growth "low-teens" and business EPS growth "mid-teens" at constant exchange rates; currency about -1% on sales and -1% to -2% on EPS; business tax rate about 21%. Ours: sales +11.5% reported (about +12.5% to 13% at constant rates), in line; business EPS EUR 9.05 (+15.6% reported), at or just above the top of the range, consistent with a guidance record of raising and beating (raised in July and October 2026; 2025 delivered at the top). Implied Q4 2026 EPS about EUR 1.54, roughly flat on Q4 2025 despite about EUR 250m of lost Regeneron reimbursement in the quarter.
  • FY2027 (no formal guidance; first framework due with results on 28 January 2027): management has signposted pricing "slightly negative", about EUR 1.3bn of sales removed by the Cheplapharm and Medley disposals, about EUR 800m of BOI lost from the end of Regeneron reimbursement (against about EUR 300m in 2026), R&D flat, and operating margin improving "each and every year through 2030". Our inputs reflect each of these; our BOI margin rises 0.3 point in 2027. We expect the first framing to be mid-to-high single-digit EPS growth at constant rates, which our organic EPS (EUR 9.74, +7.6%) would satisfy.
  • 2030 ambitions: Dupixent about EUR 25bn (ours EUR 26.5bn, about 6% above, consistent with consensus peak sales of nearly USD 30bn); launch medicines about EUR 10bn (ours EUR 9.7bn); vaccines about EUR 9bn (ours EUR 8.7bn). Free cash flow of at least 20% of sales: reached on our numbers around 2029 on the company's definition.
  • Leverage: management's guide of about 1 times net debt to EBITDA after acquisitions; ours peaks at about 1.16 times at end-2027 and is back below 1 times during 2028.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Revenue. Company net sales (= FactSet fundamentals revenue; FY2025 EUR 43,626m) plus other revenues (about EUR 1.9bn in FY2025, mainly VaxServe) = the brokers' revenue (FactSet estimates-basis actual FY2025 EUR 45,507m). Our net sales plus about EUR 2.05bn to 2.3bn of other revenues gives brokers'-basis revenue of about EUR 50.7bn (2026), 53.2bn (2027), 56.3bn (2028), 58.3bn (2029) and 59.6bn (2030), against consensus EUR 50.9bn, 53.7bn, 57.1bn and 57.9bn (2029 rests on ten brokers).
  • EBITDA. Ours is BOI plus D&A excluding acquired-intangible amortisation: about EUR 15.7bn (2026) and 16.7bn (2027), against a consensus median of EUR 15.0bn and 16.3bn. The consensus line is not like for like: brokers with FY2026 EPS of about EUR 9.0 submit EBITDA from EUR 14.2bn to 17.3bn. FactSet's history EBITDA (FF_EBITDA_OPER; FY2025 EUR 17,094m) adds back acquired-intangible amortisation and impairments and is not comparable with either.
  • EPS. Business EPS excludes amortisation and impairment of acquired intangibles (including capitalised in-licensing upfronts), restructuring, Opella-related and other one-off items, and their tax effects. FY2025: business EPS EUR 7.83 against IFRS EPS EUR 4.26 and FactSet adjusted diluted EPS EUR 6.25. Our IFRS EPS would be about EUR 2.0 to 2.3 a share below business EPS each year before impairments. Consensus EPS is the brokers' business EPS; our cross-check below is on the same basis. Shares: about 1,200m weighted (company basis), not FactSet's 1,212.6m diluted snapshot.
  • Free cash flow. Our cross-check FCF is after interest, cash tax (business tax less about EUR 0.6bn a year of tax deductions on amortisation and restructuring), lease payments, restructuring cash (EUR 0.9bn in 2026, 0.8bn in 2027 and 2028, 0.7bn in 2029 and 2030) and all capital spending including intangibles, before acquisitions, disposals and dividends. The company's FCF excludes large intangible acquisitions (the USD 1bn Regeneron upfront) and includes disposal proceeds, so it runs about EUR 1bn to 1.5bn above ours in 2026 (company basis about EUR 9bn). FactSet's historical "free cash flow" line deducts dividends (FY2025: 10,750 operating cash flow less 1,762 fixed capex less 4,772 dividends = 4,216) and is not comparable with any forward FCF figure.
  • Net debt. Company definition excludes lease liabilities (EUR 12.2bn at September 2026); FactSet's includes them (EUR 17.2bn at June 2026 against the company's EUR 15.5bn).

Your cross-check: your own earnings per share and free cash flow for each of the five fiscal years

Cross-check FY2026 (year to 31-Dec-26) FY2027 (year to 31-Dec-27) FY2028 (year to 31-Dec-28) FY2029 (year to 31-Dec-29) FY2030 (year to 31-Dec-30)
Earnings per share 9.05 9.61 10.45 11.13 11.52
Free cash flow 7,450 8,550 9,450 11,000 11,600

Earnings per share is business EPS (the brokers' basis) on about 1,200m to 1,203m weighted shares. Without the assumed acquisition it would be about EUR 9.74 (2027), 10.55 (2028), 11.06 (2029) and 11.30 (2030). Free cash flow includes restructuring cash; if the model does not deduct restructuring, expect its FCF to be about EUR 0.7bn to 0.9bn higher than ours, partly offset by about EUR 0.6bn a year if it charges cash tax at the full business rate.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Sanofi SA (SAN-FR, Euronext Paris), a global biopharmaceutical company reporting a single Biopharma segment since deconsolidating its consumer health business Opella in April 2025, with franchises in immunology (Dupixent, co-developed and profit-shared with Regeneron), rare diseases and haemophilia (ALTUVIIIO, Ayvakit, Qfitlia, Rezurock), neurology, oncology (Sarclisa), vaccines (influenza, paediatric combinations, Beyfortus with AstraZeneca and Sobi, Heplisav-B from Dynavax) and declining general medicines (Lantus, Toujeo, Plavix; Lovenox outside the US being transferred to Cheplapharm in 2027)

Request: T-D1

Peers: AstraZeneca (European large-cap that rebuilt its pipeline after its 2012 patent cliff while raising core operating margin); Roche (replaced the 2018 to 2020 Avastin, Herceptin and Rituxan loss of exclusivity with Ocrevus, Hemlibra and Tecentriq); AbbVie (built Skyrizi and Rinvoq before the Humira cliff and bought Apogee in 2026 for a long-acting IL-13 antibody that competes with Dupixent); Novartis (margin expansion after the Sandoz separation; bolt-on acquisitions including Avidity, whose lead asset failed Phase III in September 2026); Bristol Myers Squibb and Pfizer (acquisition-led replacement of patent-cliff revenue with large cost programmes); GSK (vaccines-heavy European peer with a restructuring history); Regeneron (Dupixent partner whose profit share rises with sales); Eli Lilly and Galderma (Ebglyss and Nemluvio, the biologics taking first-line atopic dermatitis share from Dupixent) Operating conditions: a dominant single product still growing strongly but losing share of new patients; partner profit-sharing on the main growth driver; rising US dollar share of sales against a euro-heavy cost base; US pricing agreements and Medicare Part D redesign; European cost containment (Germany's mandatory rebate rising to 15.5% in January 2027); a new chief executive (May 2026) and head of R&D (September 2026) after a run of late-stage pipeline failures; a crowded market for late-stage and commercial acquisition targets before the 2027 to 2032 industry patent expiries Inputs: Can Sanofi grow business operating income faster than sales in 2027 while absorbing, all in one year, the end of Regeneron development-cost reimbursement (company guidance: about EUR 300m of business operating income in 2026 and about EUR 800m in 2027), the non-recurrence of 2026 US gross-to-net accrual releases (Morgan Stanley, 30 July 2026: EUR 100m to 200m on Dupixent in Q2 2026; Jefferies, 30 October 2026: about EUR 300m across Dupixent, Lantus and Toujeo in Q3 2026), the Q2 2026 Sarclisa manufacturing provision reversal, and about EUR 1.3bn of 2027 sales removed by the Cheplapharm and Medley disposals (company guidance)? Is Sanofi's record of holding R&D and SG&A flat while sales grow (Q3 2026: R&D +0.8%, SG&A -0.9% at constant rates on sales +14%) repeatable through 2028, given that its earlier efficiency programmes (Play to Win: EUR 2.7bn delivered, fully reinvested) did not reach the margin? How durable is Dupixent volume growth once its share of biologic-naive starts has fallen (Deutsche Bank expert panel, September 2026: from 80% to 90% to 30% to 40%), and how much of continued growth comes from COPD and other pulmonology indications rather than dermatology? Can the launch portfolio (ALTUVIIIO, Ayvakit, Rezurock, Sarclisa, Qfitlia, Tzield; Q3 2026 sales EUR 1,390m, +31%) keep compounding toward the company's 2030 ambition of about EUR 10bn, given the slow Tzield relaunch? What is Sanofi's record, and that of peers facing a single-product cliff, in turning large late-stage or commercial acquisitions into earnings and returns above the cost of capital (Sanofi: Genzyme, Bioverativ, Ablynx, Provention Bio, Translate Bio, Blueprint Medicines)? Focus: Whether Sanofi's 2027 to 2028 earnings growth rests on strengths it has demonstrably shown (Dupixent volume, launch execution, cost holding) or on strengths it would need to acquire (value-creating large acquisitions, a working R&D engine), and how often companies starting from a similar position delivered margin expansion in the two years after their growth driver's new-patient share peaked.

Request: T-D2

Peers: AbbVie (Humira-to-Skyrizi and Rinvoq transition; zumilokibart); Regeneron (Dupixent partner disclosures, profit share as a percentage of sales); Eli Lilly (Ebglyss); Galderma (Nemluvio); Novartis (Rhapsido, oral BTK inhibitor with head-to-head trials against Dupixent reading out in 2027; currency mismatch between Swiss franc costs and US dollar sales); Kymera Therapeutics (oral STAT6 degrader KT-621, Phase 2b BROADEN2 around year-end 2026); AstraZeneca (tozorakimab in COPD; Beyfortus partner); Merck & Co (Enflonsia against Beyfortus); Alnylam (Amvuttra sales that drive Sanofi's royalty; nucresiran) Operating conditions: chronic-therapy patient accumulation; rebate competition in US dermatology biologics; US vaccine-policy changes and CDC central ordering; currency swings with about 60% of quarterly sales in US dollars (company disclosure: 60.4% in Q3 2026) and hedges expiring by 2027; acquisition timing and size under an AA credit-rating constraint; strategy update promised before the end of 2026 Inputs: How concentrated are the drivers of Sanofi's 2027 to 2030 earnings in Dupixent volume, launch-medicine growth and capital allocation, and how much do the Regeneron profit share (US profits shared equally; outside the US a sliding scale from 65:35 to 55:45 in Sanofi's favour), Sobi's US Beyfortus royalty step-ups (to 30% to 35% by 2028) and Amvuttra royalties (30% on annual sales above USD 1.5bn; Alnylam's royalty-free nucresiran) dampen or amplify them? How wide has the historical dispersion of next-year EPS outcomes been for large-cap pharma companies in the year after a peak growth year flattered by gross-to-net releases, and how often did consensus for that year prove too high or too low? How sensitive is Sanofi's business EPS to EUR/USD (company disclosure: about EUR 1,069m of sales and EUR 0.23 of business EPS per 0.05 move) once hedges roll off after 2027, and how did comparable euro- or franc-reporting peers' earnings move in past dollar swings? What is the distribution of share-price and EPS outcomes for large pharma companies after announcing debt-funded acquisitions of EUR 10bn to 25bn while trading near the bottom of their own multiple range, and after announcing large buybacks instead? How does the timing of 2027 headwinds by quarter (reimbursement loss from Q3 2026, Q2 and Q3 2026 release base effects, Cheplapharm transfers from Q1 to Q3 2027) shape the half-year growth profile? Focus: How wide and how skewed the range of Sanofi's 2027 business EPS is once currency and a possible acquisition-or-buyback decision are included, and whether the operating drivers (Dupixent volume, launches) or the corporate decisions dominate that range.

Request: T-I1

Peers: AstraZeneca, Novartis, Roche and GSK (European large-cap pharma peers whose 2026 guidance histories and 2027 framing practices offer read-across); Novo Nordisk (European peer that cut guidance in 2025 to 2026); AbbVie and Eli Lilly (US immunology peers whose results and access decisions move Dupixent expectations); Regeneron (partner whose quarterly Dupixent disclosures precede or accompany Sanofi's); Alnylam (Amvuttra sales and guidance that set Sanofi's royalty) Operating conditions: the first 2027 guidance framework due with full-year results on 28 January 2027; the strategy update promised before the end of 2026; quarterly sales reporting with full financial statements half-yearly; a pre-quarter aide-mémoire; guidance given at constant exchange rates with separate currency guidance; US midterm elections in November 2026; Germany's rebate increase in January 2027 Inputs: Sanofi raised its 2026 guidance three times (January 2026: high-single-digit sales growth and business EPS slightly faster; July 2026: sales about 10%; 30 October 2026: sales low-teens and business EPS mid-teens at constant rates): how has Sanofi framed its initial guidance for the following year after each strong year since 2019, and how did that compare with consensus at the time? Where does consensus for FY2026 and FY2027 sit against guidance and against the run-rate implied by nine-month 2026 results, and what does consensus assume for the end of Regeneron reimbursement, the disposals, gross-to-net normalisation and pricing in 2027? Which consensus figure will the company and the market use as the bar for 2027 (FactSet median, the company-compiled consensus, Visible Alpha; Rothschild & Co Redburn on 4 November 2026 cited EUR 9.16 and Morgan Stanley on 3 November 2026 cited EUR 9.18 for FY2027)? Why do brokers' FY2027 EPS forecasts span from about EUR 8.5 to about EUR 10.1, and which assumptions explain the spread? Do brokers model any large acquisition or buyback, and how have they revised after past Sanofi deals? What do peers' latest results and guidance imply for European pharma 2027 framing (pricing, currency, Germany)? Focus: How likely Sanofi is to frame 2027 at, below or above consensus in January 2027 and to raise during the year, and in which direction and breadth consensus for 2027 and 2028 is likely to move after the strategy update and the full-year results.

Request: T-I2

Peers: AbbVie and Eli Lilly (narrative on immunology competition and long-acting or oral entrants); Regeneron (narrative on Dupixent from the partner side); Novartis and Roche (European peers whose 2025 to 2026 re-rating followed a cleared policy worry); GSK (another European large-cap trading at a low multiple with a pipeline-duration debate); Novo Nordisk (large European peer whose growth franchise faltered); Kymera Therapeutics (oral STAT6 narrative) Operating conditions: the Pharma/biotech sector's recent relative strength coming on days the market fell; a low multiple near the bottom of its own range; US pricing agreements signed by the large drugmakers by December 2025; the 2027 to 2032 industry patent expiries; investor attention on obesity medicines, in which Sanofi has no franchise Inputs: the 2027 step-down in reported growth as 2026's gross-to-net releases, the Sarclisa reversal, the end of Regeneron reimbursement and the disposals fall out together, and whether it is seen as concentrated in the first half of 2027; the gap between Dupixent's falling share of new patients and its still-rising volume from accumulated chronic patients and COPD; how much of each incremental euro of Dupixent profit Sanofi keeps after Regeneron's share; whether net price erosion or cost growth limits margin expansion from 2027; whether the launch portfolio or Dupixent supplies more of the incremental operating profit; the likelihood, size and financing of a large acquisition versus a buyback, and the arithmetic of each at the current multiple; the gap between business EPS and IFRS EPS and the recurring nature of restructuring costs; the growing US dollar share of sales and EPS sensitivity to EUR/USD; the post-2031 Dupixent erosion curve under PBM private-label biosimilars and the timing of long-acting successors from the expanded Regeneron alliance; Amvuttra royalty cannibalisation by nucresiran Focus: Which of these dynamics investors and analysts covering Sanofi already discuss and price, which only one or two commentators raise, and which no one discusses, especially the half-year profile of 2027, the share of Dupixent's incremental profit that Sanofi keeps, and the capital-allocation choice.

Request: OWN-1

Question: What do US prescription data (total and new-to-brand prescriptions by indication, particularly atopic dermatitis versus COPD and other pulmonology indications), payer and PBM formulary decisions for 2027, and Regeneron's quarterly disclosures show about the trajectory of Sanofi's Dupixent US volume growth and US net price per patient from the second half of 2026 into 2027, and how have rebate levels in US atopic dermatitis and asthma biologics changed since Eli Lilly's Ebglyss and Galderma's Nemluvio gained broad commercial coverage?

Request: OWN-2

Question: What did Sanofi announce at its strategy update under chief executive Belén Garijo (expected before the end of 2026), including any acquisition criteria, return hurdles, leverage limits, mid-term financial targets, R&D spending plans and shareholder-return commitments; which acquisition targets have been reported or rumoured for Sanofi (for example Insmed, reported by Jefferies), at what valuations; and how have share prices, credit ratings and business EPS of large European and US pharma acquirers moved in the first two years after debt-funded acquisitions of EUR 10bn to 25bn made while facing a single-product patent cliff?

Request: OWN-3

Question: How large and how frequent have Sanofi's US gross-to-net accrual adjustments been in each quarter from 2022 to 2026 (including any in Q4 2026), which products they affected, how Sanofi and its auditors describe the estimation process and the use of AI tools to record provisions, and have comparable releases at other large pharma companies (for example Novartis, GSK, AstraZeneca) recurred or reversed in the following year?

Request: OWN-4
